Here $p^{L_1(p)-1}\ge K_0=k^2x^{78}$ below $p_0$, as follows
from the ceiling formula for $L_1$ below. Summing with
$|T_p|\le r$ and dividing by $ra$ costs at most
\[
 O\!\left(m\log x\left[
 (\mathcal L/k+K_0^{-1})\log(2Y)
 +\frac{1+\log p_0}{p_0-1}\right]\right)=o(1).
\]
Indeed $\sum_{p<Y}\log p/p\le2\log(2Y)$ by the factorial
bound below, and the remaining tail is bounded by the integral
of $\log t/t^2$ from $\lceil p_0\rceil-1$ to infinity.
The limit uses the rank and width bounds above. The remaining numerical terms in $l_p$ have the following
bounds. A term $S_k\log_p(2a)$ with
$S_k\ll k+\exp(x^{\varepsilon/4})$ costs
$O(mrV_*S_k\log(2a))=o(ra)$. Below $Y$ there is also a
piecewise constant allowance. Below $p_0$ it is
$O_\varepsilon(b'\log x)$, costing $O_\varepsilon(mr p_0 b'\log x)$
by Chebyshev's bound. Since $b'=a/Y$ this too is $o(ra)$.
Above $p_0$ and below $Y$ the allowance has size
\[
 O_\varepsilon\!\left(b'/x+e^{x^{\varepsilon/4}}
  +(w_*+t_*)x^{\varepsilon/8}\log x+e^{x^\varepsilon/2}\right)
 =O_\varepsilon(b'/x),
\]
where $t_*$ is polynomial in $x$. For the equality use
$w_*=b'/(xn)$, $n\gg x^2$ and
$\log b'=\log x+x^\varepsilon-x^{\varepsilon/3}$.
Chebyshev's bound now gives a total $O_\varepsilon(mrYb'/x)
=O_\varepsilon(mra/x)=o(ra)$. Thus the summation of these
allowances fits in the first bracket term. The arithmetic input
is the lower bound for the local gain with precisely these
allowances, together with control of the omitted nodes.

To choose the common set $T$ of nodes, include each of the $n$
nodes independently with probability $d=x^{-\varepsilon/8}$. Put
$h=2+8\varepsilon$, $K=\lceil8(h+2)/\varepsilon\rceil$ and
$R=K+1$. Consider a cyclic Frobenius orbit of length
$x^{\varepsilon/4}\le n'\le n\le 2x^{h+\varepsilon/100}$ and
condition on a specified node being selected. All other positions
on the orbit are still independent. The local requirements on this
orbit are the following three interval and spacing conditions.

First, each cyclic interval of length $L_2$ must contain at most
$K$ selected positions. Here $L_2=O_\varepsilon(\log x)$, since
it is twice the supremum of the spacings
$\lceil\log_p((kx^{26})^4)\rceil$ and $k$ is polynomial.
At most $L_2$ intervals contain the specified node. A union bound
over subsets of the other positions therefore bounds failure by
\[
 L_2^{K+1}d^K+n'L_2^{K+1}d^{K+1}=O_\varepsilon(x^{-2}).
\]
Indeed $d^K\le x^{-h-2}$, and the extra $d$ in the second term
absorbs both $x^{\varepsilon/100}$ and the logarithmic factor.

Second, divide the orbit from its chosen origin into complete tiles
of $6R+1$ successive cells of length $u=\lceil1/d\rceil$,
leaving a final remainder. A marked tile has its odd-numbered cells
empty and each even-numbered cell contains just one selected
position, of even index. On an unconditioned tile the probabilities
of these two cell events are at least $(1-d)^u$ and
$\lfloor u/2\rfloor d(1-d)^{u-1}$, respectively. Both eventually
exceed $1/10$. Set $c=10^{-(6R+1)}$,
$M_0=\lceil(h+3)\log(x)/c\rceil$ and $g=(M_0+5)(6R+1)u$.
There are at least $M_0+1$ complete tiles eventually by the
lower bound on $n'$. Any run of $M_0+1$ consecutive complete
tiles, read cyclically, has at least $M_0$ independent tiles away
from the conditioned node. The probability that some such run has
no marked tile is at most $n'(1-c)^{M_0}=O(x^{-2})$.

Third, set $N_1=1+\lceil\max(6e gd,(h+3)\log_2 x)\rceil$,
where $e$ is Euler's number. Every cyclic window of length $g$
must contain at most $N_1$ selected positions. Even after
conditioning, failure in a fixed window requires at least
$b=N_1-1$ independent successes. Its probability is at most
$\binom{g}{b}d^b\le(egd/b)^b\le2^{-b}$ (zero if $b>g$).
Summation over starting points gives $O(x^{-2})$ again.

Let $B_p$ count selected nodes on orbits failing one of these tests
for a relevant prime $p<Y$ of large order. The conditional bounds
give $\mathbb E B_p\ll_\varepsilon nd x^{-2}$ by summing over
nodes. The weighted sum of $B_p$ with weights $(\log p)/p$ is at
most four times its expectation with probability at least $3/4$,
by Markov's inequality (automatic for zero expectation).
Also $nd/2\le |T|\le2nd$ with probability tending to one by
the binomial variance bound, since $nd\to\infty$. Hence a single
$T$ satisfies both conclusions. Charging the full count at every
one of these omitted nodes now costs
$O_\varepsilon(ma\,nd\,x^{-2}\log(2Y))=o(ra)$, since
$r=|T|\ge nd/2$. No independence between different primes is
required. This choice precedes the choice of integer weights.

Two prime losses admit a common choice of weights. Choose the integer
weights independently and uniformly in their finite nonempty intervals.
For each candidate prime $p<Y=\exp(x^{\varepsilon/3})$, group the
selected nodes by Frobenius orbit. Suppose each retained part $O$ has
a nonnegative loss fraction $\kappa_{p,O}$ depending only on its
coordinates, with local mean at most $|O|x^{-12}$. Each local
assignment has the same number of extensions to the full box, so
$S_p=m\sum_O\kappa_{p,O}$ has mean at most $mr x^{-12}$.
Put $\theta=x^{-\varepsilon}$ and
\[
 H=\sum_p\frac{\log p}{p}
       (2\mathbf{1}_{S_p>\theta}+4S_p).
\]
For $N=\lfloor Y\rfloor\ge2$, the divisibility
$\prod_{p\le N}p^{\lfloor N/p\rfloor}\mid N!$ and
$\lfloor N/p\rfloor\ge N/(2p)$ give
$\sum_{p\le N}(\log p)/p\le 2\log N$.
The indicator is bounded by $S_p/\theta$. Hence
$\mathbb E H\le 2x^{\varepsilon/3}(2x^\varepsilon+4)mr x^{-12}$.
For $0<\varepsilon<1/52$, $m\le x^{1/2+\varepsilon/2}$ and
$r\le 2x^{2+(8+1/100)\varepsilon}$, this is at most
$24x^{-19/2+(1/3+1+1/2+8+1/100)\varepsilon}=o(x^{-9})$.
By Markov's inequality the fraction of assignments with $H>x^{-9}$
tends to zero. On every remaining assignment the cost of discarding
primes with $S_p>\theta$, charged at $4amr(\log p)/p$, is at most
$2amr x^{-9}$. On the same assignment any index losses
$0\le i_p\le 4amrS_p/p$ satisfy $\sum_p i_p\log p\le amr x^{-9}$.
Both bounds follow term by term from the definition of $H$.
The coefficient in the index estimate can be checked before choosing
the weights. At scale $a=xb$, $b=\exp(x^\varepsilon)$, each
numerator is chosen in $[a,a+a/(kx^{13})]$. For $k\ge5$ this gives
$a_t\le6a/5$. At a retained orbit $O$ the uncorrected odd-power
count, for one of the $m$ lifts, is bounded by
\[
 U_p(O)=\sum_{t\in O}\sum_{h\ge1,\ h\ {\rm odd}}a_t p^{-h}
       =\frac{p}{p^2-1}\sum_{t\in O}a_t
       \le\frac{8}{5}|O|\,a/p .
\]
Here $p^2/(p^2-1)\le4/3$, including $p=2$. Suppose a lift is
charged the positive part of this count after nonnegative deductions
(or any smaller nonnegative amount). Let $v_p(O)$ be the ceiling
of the total charge of its $m$ lifts. For an empty part the charge
is zero. Otherwise put $z=m|O|a/p$. Uniformly for $p<Y$ we have
$a/p\ge x\exp(x^\varepsilon-x^{\varepsilon/3})$, so $z\ge5/2$
for sufficiently large $x$. It follows that
$v_p(O)\le 8z/5+1\le2z$. The index allowance
$i_p=2S_p\max_O v_p(O)$, with value zero for an empty collection,
therefore satisfies $i_p\le4amrS_p/p$ since $|O|\le r$.
To see how this allowance enters the lattice index, work over a
discrete valuation ring with uniformizer $\pi$ and put $h=v_\pi(p)$.
Let $M$ be free of rank $d$ and $N\subset M$ the full-rank lattice
obtained from the orbit operators. Write $N$ in Smith coordinates
with exponents $c_1,\ldots,c_d$. Intersecting $N$ with $p^B M$
replaces each $c_i$ by $\max(c_i,hB)$. Here $B$ is the common
integral exponent and $S=\max_O v_p(O)$, taken as zero for an
empty collection. The orbit estimate needed at level $j\ge1$ is
\[
 \#\{i:c_i>h(B+(j-1)S)\}
       \le d\,m^j e_j\big((\kappa_{p,O})_O\big),
\]
where $e_j$ is the elementary symmetric polynomial (zero above the
number of parts). It suffices at exponent $E=B+(j-1)S$ to exhibit
$g$ exceptional vectors in $M$, with $g$ bounded by the right side,
whose span together with $N$ contains $p^E M$. Indeed the image
of $p^E M$ in $M/N$ is then contained in a module generated by
$g$ elements. Its preimage under a surjection onto that module from
a free module of rank $g$ is free of rank at most $g$, since the
ring is principal. The image thus needs at most $g$ generators.
In Smith coordinates its nonzero summands have lengths $c_i-hE$
for $c_i>hE$. Reduction modulo $\pi$ counts these summands and
proves the claimed bound. In particular the exceptional vectors can
first be constructed in a larger lattice $A$ with
$p^e A\subset M\subset A$. If the same linear orbit operators
generate $N_A$ from $A$ and $N$ from $M$, then $p^eN_A\subset N$
by linearity. Multiplication of
$p^{E_0}A\subset N_A+\sum_{\nu=1}^g R u_\nu$ by $p^e$ gives
$p^{E_0+e}M\subset N+\sum_{\nu=1}^g R(p^e u_\nu)$, with every
$p^eu_\nu$ in $M$. The shift adds $e$ to the common exponent and
preserves the bound on the number of exceptional vectors.

Here is the iteration giving the exceptional vectors from the
one-step inclusions. Order the orbit indices $v$. At each index
there are three lattice positions $D_v\subset M_v\subset A_v$,
meaning inclusions with all other positions fixed. Write $M$ for
the all-middle lattice. Multiplication by $p^{a_v}$ takes $A_v$
into $M_v$, and $p^{b_v}$ takes $M_v$ into $D_v$. Put
$c_v\ge a_v+b_v$. Suppose the commuting operators $y_v$ take
$A_v$ into $D_v$ with the other positions fixed. Write $L_I$ for
the lattice with inner positions on $I$ and middle positions
elsewhere. Suppose $p^{c_*}L_I$ is spanned modulo incoming images
by a set $G_I\subset L_I$ of at most $d\prod_{v\in I}\kappa_v$
vectors; the incoming images are those of $y_v$ from the lattice
obtained by changing position $v\in I$ to outer. Let $N$ contain
the images of $M$ under all products choosing either $y_v^m$ or
$p^{s_v}$ at each $v$, where $0\le s_v\le S$.

Fix $j\ge1$ up to the number of indices. Begin with the first $j$
indices active and all other indices waiting in their given order.
Pay $b_v$ powers of $p$ on entering each active index, moving it
from middle to inner. At active set $I$, apply the one-step
inclusion. An exceptional term has the form of an accumulated
operator monomial applied to a member of $G_I$. On each incoming
term, record one application of $y_v$ and continue with its
preimage in the outer position at $v$. If the exponent there is
still below $m$, pay $c_v$ to return it to inner. At exponent
$m$, pay $a_v$ to move it to middle and activate the next waiting
index. If none is waiting, there are just $j-1$ unfinished
indices. Their remaining operators preserve $M$, since
$M_v\subset A_v$ and $y_v A_v\subset D_v\subset M_v$.
Commutativity therefore puts this term in $N$ after multiplication
by $p^{\sum_{v\ {\rm unfinished}}s_v}$. For $j$ exceeding the
number of indices use the all-scalar product directly.

Each branch terminates because the sum of the operator exponents
increases at every step. Charge the $c_*$ cost of an incoming
step to the index it increments. Even at an exceptional termination
one active index has used at most $m-1$ increments, leaving one
$c_*$ available there. At any index the total cost is bounded by
$b_v+(m-1)c_v+a_v+mc_*\le m(c_v+c_*)$. Thus the common exponent
$B+(j-1)S$, with $B=\sum_v m(c_v+c_*)$, pays every branch;
unused powers multiply its coefficient. For a given active set
$I$ of size $j$, the completed indices are precisely the indices
below $\max I$ outside $I$. Their exponents are $m$, the waiting
exponents are zero, and the active exponents are between zero
and $m-1$. Hence at most $m^j$ monomials act on $G_I$ regardless
of branch order. They give integral exceptional vectors in $M$,
numbering at most $dm^j e_j(\kappa)$ after summation over $I$.
This gives the stated Smith bound in $p$-adic units.

If $S>0$, sum the excess lengths above $hB$ in
intervals of $hS$ successive integers. Each interval costs at most
$hS$ times the count at its left endpoint. In the expansion of
$(\sum_O\kappa_{p,O})^j$, each product at distinct indices occurs
$j!$ times. Thus the length of $M/(N\cap p^BM)$ is at most
\[
 hdB+hdS\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{S_p^j}{j!}
     =hd[B+S(\exp(S_p)-1)]\le hd(B+2SS_p)
\]
when $0\le S_p\le1/2$, by comparison with the geometric series.
For $S=0$ the count above $hB$ vanishes by taking $j$ greater
than the number of parts. For a saturated submodule $M'\subset M$,
$p^BM\cap M'=p^BM'$ by torsion-freeness of $M/M'$. The quotient
of $M'$ by $M'\cap N\cap p^BM$ injects into $M/(N\cap p^BM)$,
so the same length bound applies there with the original rank $d$.
This proves the passage from the orbit counts to the index allowance.
The product of loss fractions in this count has a local explanation.
Fix a single orbit and a free local lattice $P$ of rank $d_v$ over
the valuation ring $R$. Let the columns of $F$ be the vectors in
$P$ obtained from the incoming edges. The local pairing estimate
asks for integral matrices $U,V$ with $UFV=p^D I_n$ and
$n\ge(1-\kappa_v)d_v$. Here $U$ consists of forms integral on
$P$, and $V$ takes linear combinations of the incoming vectors.
One can assemble this identity from tests on individual edges.
Over the unramified local ring with residue field of order $q=p^{n'}$,
an incoming edge at a rank-$k$ lattice $\Lambda$ has source
$p\Lambda\subseteq\Lambda'\subseteq\Lambda$ of index $q^r$.
In $\Lambda/p\Lambda=\mathbb F_q^{k-r}\oplus\mathbb F_q^r$,
the graphs of all linear maps from the first summand to the second
give $q^{r(k-r)}$ distinct sources by inverse image. Hence a rank
bound of $b/q^{r(k-r)}$ on each of these edges gives total rank
at least $b$, provided the edge tests separate as follows.

Choose columns on each edge, with $n$ columns in total. Suppose
that for every coefficient vector on one edge having a unit
coordinate, an integral form on $P$ pairs with the corresponding
combination with valuation at most $hD$, and pairs with every
column on the other chosen edges in $\pi^{hD+1}R$.
For any primitive vector of coefficients on the full list, select
an edge on which it has a unit coordinate. Its detecting form still
detects the full combination, since adding elements divisible by
$\pi^{hD+1}$ preserves a valuation at most $hD$. Stack the forms
as rows of a matrix $A$, using a finite generating set of their
span in $P^*$; such a set exists since $R$ is Noetherian.
Then $AF$ sends no primitive vector into $\pi^{hD+1}R^{\#\mathrm{rows}}$.
In Smith coordinates this says that all $n$ columns have nonzero
pivots dividing $p^D$: a missing or more divisible pivot would
give a primitive counterexample. Multiplying the pivot rows by
$p^D$ divided by their pivots and undoing the basis changes gives
an integral left multiplier $H$ with $HAF=p^D I_n$. Thus the
detectors on separate edges give the required pairing identity.
For this edge construction the dimension lost in assembling the
blocks can be budgeted explicitly. Write $s$ for the number of
selected nodes in the orbit, $r_0$ for the codimension of the
edge, $k_0$ for the ambient local rank, and
$Q=q^{r_0(k_0-r_0)}$. Use $y>0$ for the auxiliary size in the
edge estimate, distinct from the prime cutoff $Y$. The per-edge
rank estimate to be established has the form
\[
 n_e\ \ge\ \frac{d_v}{Q}
 (1-C s r_0^2 k_0/y)(1-k_0c_0/a)^s ,
\]
with $C,c_0\ge0$ and $0\le k_0c_0/a\le1$. The last factor can be
derived from the dimensions of the highest-weight representations. At one selected
node write its dominant highest weight as
$\lambda=(\mu_1+l,\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_{k_0-1})$, with $l\ge a$
and $\mu_1-\mu_{k_0-1}\le c_0$. Denote the Weyl dimension by
$d(\lambda)=\prod_{i<j}(\lambda_i-\lambda_j+j-i)/(j-i)$.
Cancel the factors with both indices in the tail $\mu$. This gives
\[
 \frac{\binom{l+k_0-1}{k_0-1}d(\mu)}{d(\lambda)}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{k_0-1}
       \frac{l+j}{l+j+\mu_1-\mu_j}
 \ge 1-\frac{(k_0-1)c_0}{a}.
\]
Indeed each factor is one minus a quantity in $[0,c_0/a]$,
and the product of $1-z_j$ for $0\le z_j\le1$ is at least
$1-\sum_j z_j$. Let $P_0$ be the product of the displayed binomial
coefficients over the $s$ selected nodes, and let $d_2$ be the
product of their tail dimensions and the full dimensions at the
other nodes. Multiplication gives
$P_0d_2\ge(1-k_0c_0/a)^s d_v$ for the tensor product of the
node representations. In particular the required numerical rank
follows if one realizes at least $(1-Csr_0^2 k_0/y)P_0/Q$ symbols
on the edge and detects their products with all $d_2$ tail indices
on primitive coefficient vectors. The product count alone does
not establish that detection property.

There is a direct check of independence for the polynomial part of
these products over the residue field. Index rows and columns from
zero. Fix a monomial order selecting the diagonal in each ordered
minor. Write $\alpha_c$ for the multiplicity of letter $c$ in a
symbol monomial, with $\sum_c\alpha_c=l$, and $X$ for the leading
exponent array of a monic tail polynomial. In the first-column
model the letters are the entries $(c,0)$. The tail indices have
fixed first column, so subtracting it from the first column of the
product recovers every $\alpha_c$. In the complementary-minor
model the letters are maximal minors of the first $k_0-1$ columns,
omitting row $c$. Their diagonal leading terms use the entry
$(j,j)$ exactly when $j<c$. For the tail indices in this model
row zero is empty and the arrays are interlaced:
$\sum_{i\le u}X_{i,j+1}\le\sum_{i<u}X_{i,j}$. Induction from
column zero gives $X_{i,j}=0$ whenever $i\le j$. Hence the
diagonal of the product reads $\sum_{c>j}\alpha_c$ for
$0\le j<k_0-1$. Successive differences and the known total $l$
recover $\alpha$. In either model subtraction of the letter
exponents then recovers $X$. Distinct tail leading arrays and
symbol monomials therefore give distinct product leading terms.
Taking the largest term in a proposed relation proves independence
in every residue characteristic. Different nodes use disjoint
variables, so the same recovery works node by node.
In particular one may use independent linear combinations of the
symbol monomials: the map on the full monomial span, tensored with
the span of the tail indices, is already injective.

The common factor introduced by the local substitution preserves
this independence. To check it, write $n'$ for the even period.
At an even position $a$, let $g_a$ be the invertible matrix of
the local change of basis. In row-vector notation the adapted
coordinates are $Xg_a$ and the substitution sends $X$ to
$Zg_a^{-1}$. Thus it sends $Xg_a$ to $Z$. In the first $r_0$
columns of an even row the normalized variable $Z_i$ is just
$X_{a\bmod n',i}$. A determinant of these columns with even row
positions $a_j$ and row powers $p^{u_j}$ consequently becomes
\[
 \det\bigl(X_{a_j\bmod n',i}^{\,p^{u_j}}\bigr)_{j,i=1}^{r_0}.
\]
It remains nonzero over the residue field whenever the row labels
are distinct modulo $n'$: specialize $X_{a_j\bmod n',i}$ to
$\delta_{ji}$. There are two lists in the construction. Within
a frame the increasing positions have span less than $n'$. The
other list consists of the last $r_0$ eligible even positions
before a given position. Eligibility is periodic, and the first
frame supplies $r_0$ distinct eligible residues. Every half-open
interval of $n'$ integers therefore contains at least $r_0$
eligible positions. The last $r_0$ lie in one such interval, so
their labels too are distinct.

The common factor is a product of nonnegative integral powers of
these determinants. Each factor may be checked by its own
specialization: their product is nonzero since the residue
polynomial ring is a domain. Denote the product by $H$. Suppose
the independent integral symbols $B_i$ span a direct summand of
the polynomial module. If $\sum_i c_i B_i$ is divisible by the
uniformizer, projection onto that summand shows that every $c_i$
is divisible by it. Thus the reductions $\overline B_i$ are
independent. Multiplication by $\overline H$ and an injective
renaming of the variables preserve this independence. Applied to
each tail index in the preceding product argument, this proves
that a primitive coefficient vector still has nonzero reduction
when the substituted symbols are $H B_i$ in the renamed variables.
In fact the integral summand can be recovered from the rank of the
reduced generators. Let $g$ be that rank, choose $g$ generators
independent after reduction, and use the monomials of their finite
support as coefficient coordinates. Some $g$ rows of the coefficient
matrix give a minor that is a unit. Projection onto those coordinates
followed by the inverse of the minor is a left inverse over the
valuation ring. The selected generators thus span a direct summand
even in the full polynomial module. It suffices to count independent
reductions among the actual generators.

The frame count explains the denominator $Q$ in this rank target.
Write $K=k_0-r_0$. At a node of total degree $l$ the number of
exponent vectors is $B(l)=\binom{l+k_0-1}{k_0-1}$. For an integral
block size $P\ge1$, write each coordinate uniquely as $Pz_i+v_i$,
$0\le v_i<P$. Thus
\[
 B(l)=\sum_{\substack{0\le v_i<P,\ |v|\le l\\|v|\equiv l\bmod P}}
 B\big((l-|v|)/P\big),\qquad |v|=\sum_i v_i .
\]
The congruence determines the last residue from the first $k_0-1$.
Let $f_j,e_j$ denote the first and last positions of frame $j$.
They increase strictly, with $e_{j-1}<f_j$, and both advance by
$n'$ after $J$ frames. The shift uses the positive number
$U=a/(8yk_0^2N_s)$, where $N_s\ge1$ is the size parameter for
the frame. With $H_0=\lfloor\log_p U\rfloor$, its value is
$R_j=\max(0,H_0-e_j+f_{j-1})$: a positive integer $h$ is
admissible in the rounded logarithm precisely when
$p^h\le\lfloor U/p^{e_j-f_{j-1}}\rfloor$, equivalently
$h\le H_0-e_j+f_{j-1}$. In particular
$b_j=e_j+R_j=\max(e_j,H_0+f_{j-1})$ increases strictly and
$b_{j+J}=b_j+n'$. Hence the integral block sizes
$q_j=p^{b_{j+1}-b_j}$ satisfy $\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}q_j=p^{n'}$.
Of the $k_0-1$ free residue coordinates per row, retaining $r_0-1$
at this stage costs a factor $q_j^K$ in the unrestricted residue
count. There are $r_0$ rows per frame, so the full factor is
$\prod_j q_j^{K r_0}=q^{K r_0}=Q$. There is also a uniform lower
bound on the number of lifts of any retained residue class. For
$l\ge k_0P$ every congruent residue vector satisfies $|v|\le l$.
Writing $z=(l-|v|)/P$, the exact ratio is
\[
 \frac{P^{k_0-1} B(z)}{B(l)}
  =\prod_{i=1}^{k_0-1}\frac{l-|v|+Pi}{l+i}
  \ge (1-k_0P/l)^{k_0-1}\ge 1-k_0(k_0-1)P/l .
\]
The first inequality uses $|v|<k_0P$ in each factor. Thus when
$l\ge y k_0^2 P$ with $y\ge1$, any subset of congruent classes
containing a fraction $\delta$ of the $P^{k_0-1}$ possible classes
accounts for at least $\delta(1-1/y)B(l)$ monomials.

For an eligible position $u$ in $(e_{j-1},e_j]$ the first block
size is $P=p^{b_j-u}$. Those intervals partition the integer line,
and translating $u$ by $n'$ adds $J$ to its frame index. Thus
$P$ depends only on the residue of $u$: repeated occurrences impose
the same first congruence. At a frame row, passing to the next
frame replaces $P$ by $Pq_j$. For fixed lower digits $v$ put
$z=(l-|v|)/P$. The intermediate integer $d$ must satisfy
$0\le d<r_0q_j$ and $d\equiv z\bmod q_j$. Its possible values
are exactly $(z\bmod q_j)+h q_j$ for $0\le h<r_0$.
If $z\ge r_0q_j-1$, each gives a nonnegative carry $(z-d)/q_j$
and the exact degree identity
$l=|v|+Pd+Pq_j((z-d)/q_j)$. The carry is determined, including
across the last frame of the period. The size check for this step
is explicit. In the prime range above the frame width condition
$e_{j+1}-f_j\le g$ gives $U\ge p^{e_{j+1}-f_j}$ eventually:
$\log U=x^\varepsilon+O_\varepsilon(\log x)$ since
$1\le N_s\le N_1$ and $a\sim a_0$, whereas
$g\log p=o(x^{\varepsilon/3})$. Hence $b_{j+1}=H_0+f_j$,
so at any row $u\ge f_j$ of frame $j$ one has
$Pq_j=p^{b_{j+1}-u}\le U$. If the available degree after the
reserved factors have been removed is $l\ge a/2$, this gives
$l\ge4yk_0^2N_s Pq_j$. Since $|v|<k_0P$, $y,N_s\ge1$ and
$r_0\le k_0$, it follows that $z>4k_0^2 q_j-k_0\ge r_0q_j-1$.
For a local count whose
lower bound depends only on the lower digits and these $r_0$
intermediate integers in the current frame, summing the product
of the lower bounds factors into the product of their sums, by
finite distributivity.

To pass from counts to ranks, consider the exponents of the product
one coordinate at a time. A position $u\in(e_{j-1},e_j]$ has a
unique representative in one period of these intervals. A frame row
can contribute again in the next frame. Its second weight is
$p^{b_{j+1}-u}=Pq_j$, including at the end of the period. Thus in
a coordinate shared by the two frames the final exponent has the form
\[
                 E=v+P(m+q_j h),\qquad 0\le v<P,\quad 0\le m<q_j .
\]
Here $m$ is the first local exponent and $h$ the second. We restrict
$m<q_j$ on the first $r_0$ coordinates of the shared row, precisely
those in which the second frame may contribute. In every other
coordinate $h=0$ and $m$ is unrestricted.
In either case $E\bmod P$ recovers $v$. At a shared coordinate,
division of $(E-v)/P$ by $q_j$ recovers $h$ and $m$. Elsewhere
$(E-v)/P=m$. The row sums of the recovered first exponents at the
designated frame rows give their intermediate degrees. Thus for
this support pattern the global monomial determines its label
and every local monomial.

For each label and each frame take any finite family of polynomials
independent over the residue field on these allowed supports.
The corresponding products are independent. Indeed, before
identifying variables, the tensor products of the local polynomials
are independent: tensor products of dual basis functionals on their
spans extract each coefficient of a putative relation. The map on
the tensor product of the ambient monomial spaces sends each basis
tuple to its encoded monomial. The recovery just proved makes this
map injective, with disjoint images of the monomial bases for
distinct labels. Thus multiplication preserves the product of the
local dimensions.

We quantify the loss in completing a partial choice of exponents
at the $r_0$ designated rows. Write $q_*=q_j$ and let $\beta$ be
the sum of the local degrees at positions preceding $f_j$.
Suppose each such position $u$ has degree at most $2a p^{u-b_j}$
and $u\le f_j-L_1-1$. Summing the geometric series over distinct
positions gives $\beta\le4a p^{f_j-L_1-1-b_j}$. Since
$b_{j+1}\ge H_0+f_j$ and $p^{H_0}>U/p$, it follows that
\[
 \frac{4y\beta}{q_*}\le16ya p^{-L_1-1-H_0}
 <128 y^2 k_0^2 N_s p^{-L_1}.
\]
Thus $p^{L_1}\ge128 y^2 k_0^2N_s$ suffices for $4y\beta\le q_*$.

Fix a partial choice with degrees $0\le c_i\le2\beta$ on the
designated rows, and prescribe the residues $\rho_i$ of their
completed degrees. For $r_0\ge2$, the congruence
$c_i+\sum_{h=1}^{r_0}w_{ih}\equiv\rho_i\bmod q_*$
has exactly $q_*^{r_0-1}$ solutions in the full digit box.
With any one digit fixed it has exactly $q_*^{r_0-2}$ solutions,
since another digit is determined by the remaining ones.
Removing the largest $2\beta$ values of each digit therefore
discards at most $2\beta r_0 q_*^{r_0-2}$ solutions per row.
For every retained choice the completed degree is at most
$2\beta+r_0(q_*-2\beta-1)<r_0q_*$. The choices on distinct
rows are independent, so for each prescribed residue vector there
are at least
\[
 \left[q_*^{r_0-1}\max\{0,1-r_0/(2y)\}\right]^{r_0}
\]
completions when $4y\beta\le q_*$. For a fixed nonzero partial
polynomial, multiplying it by these distinct monomials gives
independent polynomials over a field, by cancellation. Across
different partial polynomials the following finer test applies.

Let $Z$ be the square array of variables at the designated rows
in the first $r_0$ columns, and let $S$ consist of its entries off
the antidiagonal. Denote by $\pi$ the substitution setting $S$
equal to zero. Write $Z^{w_i}P_i$ for the indexed products.
Suppose $\pi(P_i)=\pm Z^{v_i}B_{\ell_i}$, where the $B_\ell$ use
only variables outside $Z$ and are independent over the coefficient
field. If $i\mapsto(w_i+v_i,\ell_i)$ is injective, the products
are independent. Indeed in any finite nontrivial relation replace
each variable in $S$ by itself times an indeterminate $t$. Let $d$
be the least total $S$-degree of a prefactor $Z^{w_i}$ with nonzero
coefficient. The coefficient of $t^d$ is a relation among
$\pm Z^{w_i+v_i}B_{\ell_i}$ from precisely those minimal indices.
Taking each $Z$-monomial coefficient in turn contradicts the
independence of the $B_\ell$.

There is a direct way to verify the path component of this
injectivity. In the last $r_0$ steps let $\nu_t$ be the decreasing
row of integers at step $t$, indexed from zero. For each level $c$
let $H_t(c)$ count its entries at least $c$. When these counts
increase by zero or one at each step, record in $I_c$ the indices
$r_0-1-s$ of the steps $s$ with no increase, $0\le s<r_0$.
The initial row and the sets $I_c$ recover all rows, since
\[
 H_t(c)=H_0(c)+t-
 \#\{s<t:r_0-1-s\in I_c\},\qquad
 \nu_t(h)\ge c\ \Longleftrightarrow\ h<H_t(c).
\]
Thus a specialized word that retains these sets and the initial
path retains the entire tail. Once the path is recovered, its
antidiagonal exponent $v_i$ is known and subtraction recovers $w_i$.
For the exterior products that realize a column at level $c$,
each rising step $s$ wedges the current column with the row
vector $Z_{s,\cdot}$, and a stationary step leaves it unchanged.
After $\pi$, this wedge inserts the single basis vector with index
$r_0-1-s$, multiplied by its antidiagonal entry. All inserted
indices are distinct. To obtain the coefficient of the full
exterior basis vector at the end, the initial exterior term must
therefore have index set exactly $I_c$. Its contribution is its
coefficient times the product of the inserted entries and the sign
of the ordering permutation. Multiplying over the active levels
gives the asserted monomial factor and the product of the retained
coefficients.

For a fixed terminal row, one part of the independence calculation
concerns the choice of column indices. Let $g=(g_{ab})$ be an
$n$ by $n$ matrix of indeterminates over the residue field.
For an increasing column $J=(j_1,\ldots,j_h)$ write
$D_J=\det(g_{a,j_b})_{1\le a,b\le h}$. In a tableau of fixed
shape the columns increase strictly downwards and the rows increase
weakly to the right. As the tableau ranges over all such fillings,
the products $\prod_J D_J$ are linearly independent. Indeed,
order the variables first by increasing row and within each row by
increasing column, giving earlier variables priority in lexicographic
order. The leading term of $D_J$ is $\prod_a g_{a,j_a}$ with
coefficient one: at the first row where another permutation differs
it must use a larger remaining column. In the leading monomial of
the product, the exponent of $g_{ab}$ counts occurrences of $b$
in tableau row $a$. Sorting those entries reconstructs each row and
hence the filling. Distinct fillings therefore have distinct leading
monomials over every residue field.

This also handles the dual columns in the change of basis by $g$.
A positive level uses the minor of $g$ with initial row set. A
nonpositive level uses a minor of $g^{-t}$ with terminal row set.
For equally large index sets $A,B$, exterior multiplication
pairs the bases indexed by $A,A^c$ to the volume element up to
sign. Applying $g$ to this pairing multiplies it by $\det g$, so
\[
 \det (g^{-t})_{A,B}
 =\pm(\det g)^{-1}\det g_{A^c,B^c}.
\]
The complement of a terminal row set is initial. Fix a finite
interval of levels containing all active columns, padding the
others with empty minors. The number of nonpositive levels in
this interval is fixed. Multiplication by the corresponding power
of $\det g$ thus converts every mixed product into a signed
tableau product, using complementary column indices at the
nonpositive levels. Whenever the resulting fillings are
semistandard of the prescribed shape, the preceding argument
separates their labels, since complementation is reversible.
All these calculations take place first in the rational function
field of $g$ and require no choice of a matrix over the residue
field.

The paths with canonical labels can themselves be read from leading
terms. Number coordinates from zero. At level $c$ of height $h$ the
canonical set is the initial $h$ coordinates for $c>0$ and their
complement for $c\le0$. Consider the variables of the last step
first, giving larger coordinate indices priority for a vector step
and smaller ones priority for a covector step. A stationary level
contributes no variable. At a rise from $h$ to $h+1$, the wedge
coefficient on the new initial set can use indices $0,\ldots,h$.
On its complement the contraction can use exactly the same indices.
The preferred one is $h$, and its coefficient is the preceding
canonical component up to sign. At a fall from $h$ to $h-1$ the
contraction on the new initial set, or the wedge on its complement,
uses indices $h-1,\ldots,n-1$. The preferred one is now $h-1$,
again leaving the preceding canonical component. These assertions
follow by deleting the chosen index in a wedge coefficient and
inserting it in a contraction coefficient.

Consequently the leading monomial in the last-step variables has
exponent $|\mu_N(a)-\mu_{N-1}(a)|$ in coordinate $a$: each level
crossed by that entry contributes once. Its coefficient is the
preceding canonical word up to sign. Induction from the empty
word $1$ shows that this coefficient is nonzero at every step.
Giving successive steps priority in reverse chronological order
therefore separates paths ending at a fixed $\mu_N$: the last
exponents and the known direction of the step recover $\mu_{N-1}$,
and iteration recovers the whole path.

Finally act on vector rows by $g$ and on covector rows by $g^{-t}$.
Exterior multiplication commutes with change of basis, and
contraction does also because the pairing is invariant. Multilinear
expansion thus writes each transformed column coefficient as a sum
of the original coefficients times minors of $g$ or $g^{-t}$.
Grade the variables by diagonal weight, assigning opposite weights
to a vector coordinate and its dual. A positive column indexed by
$J$ contributes $\mathbf1_J$ and a nonpositive column contributes
$-\mathbf1_J$ to the weight, by the wedge and contraction rules.
For sets of the prescribed sizes, every initial partial sum of
the resulting weight is bounded above by the corresponding sum
for the canonical sets: the initial set maximizes intersection
with each prefix, and the terminal set minimizes it. Equality
for all prefixes forces every set to be canonical, since the
deficits are nonnegative integers. The canonical weight is
$\mu_N$, by counting the positive levels occupied and the
nonpositive levels missing in each coordinate.

In a putative relation choose a terminal weight maximal under
these partial-sum inequalities among terms with nonzero coefficient.
After change of basis retain its weight component. Only words
ending at that weight contribute, each as its canonical word
times the product of minors indexed by its original labels.
Independence of the canonical words over the rational function
field separates the paths. For each path, independence of the
products of minors over the ground field separates the standard
labels. This contradicts the choice of a nonzero coefficient.
The argument applies to either block of variables. Products from
the two blocks are independent by the tensor product argument
above, for distinct pairs of standard words. For the cut labels,
consider a terminal rectangle with all $r_0$ entries equal to
$\beta$ and nonnegative entries throughout the tail. At each
active level $1\le c\le\beta$ the final height is $r_0$. Of the
last $t+1$ steps, precisely $r_0-H_{r_0-1-t}(c)$ raise the
height. Reversal of the step indices therefore gives
\[
 \#(I_c\cap\{0,\ldots,t\})
 =t+1-r_0+H_{r_0-1-t}(c),\qquad 0\le t<r_0 .
\]
At $t=r_0-1$ this gives the required column length $H_0(c)$.
For every $t$ the right side decreases weakly with $c$; hence the
$a$th smallest entry of column $c$ is at most the $a$th smallest
of column $c+1$ whenever the latter exists. This proves the row
condition for the cut tableau. Within each column the indices
are distinct by construction. Columns outside the active levels
contribute only the prescribed empty or complementary full sets.
Before the degree inequalities selecting the good paths are
imposed, their weighted count is exact. Write $n=k_0$,
$r=r_0$, let $b_1,\ldots,b_m$ be the prescribed strip sizes
before the final $r$ rows, and put $\beta=\sum_{i=1}^m b_i$.
The weight of a path is the number of standard labels on its
last $K$ entries. Starting at zero, the unrestricted weighted count is
\[
                    \prod_{i=1}^m\binom{b_i+n-1}{n-1}.
\]
Here unrestricted retains the interlacing conditions and the
prescribed final head. To see this, first fix the row $\lambda$
at time $m$. All its entries are at most $\beta$, since at each
earlier step an entry can increase by at most the strip size.
Each final step prepends a further fixed entry $\beta$ to the
head. Deleting that head leaves successive tails of lengths
$n,n-1,\ldots,K$, with precisely the inequalities
$\lambda_i\ge\tau_i\ge\lambda_{i+1}$ between adjacent tails.
Conversely, adjoining the fixed heads to any such chain gives
the required upward strips, their sizes determined by the row sums.
A standard label on the last tail amounts to continuing this
interlacing array down to length one: after translating by a
common integer to obtain a partition, retain successively the
tableau entries at most $j$, for $j=1,\ldots,K$. Successive shapes
differ by a horizontal strip, and the difference recovers the
boxes filled by $j$. For a signed label the nonpositive columns
are first complemented as above; translation shifts the column
indices and any added full columns have a unique filling.

The number of these arrays at fixed $\lambda$ is $d(\lambda)$.
Indeed translate so that the entries are nonnegative and put
$l_i=\lambda_i+n-i$ for $1\le i\le n$. The determinant
$\det[\binom{l_i}{n-j}]_{i,j=1}^n$ equals $d(\lambda)$ by
the Vandermonde formula. For a tail of length $n-1$ its shifted
entries range independently over $[l_{i+1},l_i-1]$.
Summing the smaller determinant row by row and using Pascal's
identity gives the differences of consecutive rows of the full
determinant in its first $n-1$ columns. Its last column consists
of ones, so row subtraction and expansion give exactly the full
determinant. Induction from length one proves the count.

It remains to sum $d(\lambda)$ over the earlier steps. With
$A_l=\det[x_j^{l_i}]$ in independent variables, sum
$A_{l'}z^{\sum_i(l'_i-l_i)}$ over upward interlacing strips.
Their shifted entries satisfy $l'_1\ge l_1$ and
$l_i\le l'_i<l_{i-1}$ for $i>1$. Geometric summation in each
row extracts $\prod_j(1-zx_j)^{-1}$ from the determinant and
leaves rows $R_1$ and $R_i-z^{l_{i-1}-l_i}R_{i-1}$,
where $R_i=(x_j^{l_i})_j$. Their determinant is $A_l$.
Divide by $A_{(n-1,\ldots,0)}$ and specialize all $x_j$ to one:
the quotient specializes to $d(\lambda)$, as seen by expanding
$x_j^{l_i}=(1+(x_j-1))^{l_i}$ and taking the first alternating
term. The coefficient of $z^b$ gives
$\sum_{\lambda'}d(\lambda')=\binom{b+n-1}{n-1}d(\lambda)$
for an upward strip of size $b$. Negating and reversing the row
preserves $d$ and converts a downward strip to an upward one.
Iterating from the zero row proves the formula.

There is a separate bound on the number of unlabelled paths.
Put $N=m+r$ and suppose $b_i\le2a$. At every stage all
coordinates are between $-\beta$ and $\beta$: before the final
steps this follows from the total degree, and afterwards it
follows from interlacing of the tails and the fixed head.
Interlacing lets the number of positive entries increase by at
most one per upward step and the number of negative entries by
at most one per downward step. Thus each row is specified by
its positive entries and its negative magnitudes in order, each
list padded with zeros to length $N$. The initial row is fixed,
so there are at most $(1+2aN)^{2N^2}$ unlabelled paths.
One can bound the label weight lost through cancellation of positive
or negative entries at a strip step. Put $s=K-2N\ge1$ and
$f(t)=\sum_{h=1}^s\log(1+t/h)$. For the final tail of length $K$
write $\alpha^+,\alpha^-$ for the positive entries and absolute
values of the negative entries, both in decreasing order.
In the Weyl product the middle $s$ entries are zero. For an
entry of magnitude $t$ outside this middle interval the $s$
distances to its positions are distinct positive integers, so
their total logarithmic contribution is at most $f(t)$.
There are fewer than $2N^2$ pairs with both positions outside
the middle interval. Each contributes at most $\log(1+2\beta)$.
Thus the log of the label weight is bounded above by
\[
 2N^2\log(1+2\beta)+\sum_{\pm}\sum_h f(\alpha_h^\pm).
\]
The logarithm of the unrestricted count is at least
$\sum_{i=1}^m f(b_i)$, by the binomial product above and
$n-1\ge s$.

For either sign, form the list $B$ of earlier strip sizes of that
sign, most recent first. At every stage the sum of the magnitudes
after deleting the first $u$ is at most the corresponding tail sum
of $B$, with zeros appended to both lists. Indeed a step of the
opposite sign decreases magnitudes coordinatewise for the chosen
sign. At a step of the same sign interlacing bounds the new tail
after $u+1$ deletions by the old tail after $u$ deletions, and the
total increases by at most the new strip size. This proves the
claim inductively from the empty lists. The final $r$ branching
steps preserve both inequalities: for two successive tails
$\lambda_i\ge\tau_i\ge\lambda_{i+1}$ bounds the positive
entries from the left and negative magnitudes from the right.
In particular a deficit $X$ at any tail persists, with its
index shifted once for each subsequent same-sign prescribed strip.

Here is the quantitative effect of such a deficit. Suppose the
same-sign sizes satisfy $B_j\ge s$ and $B_j\ge4B_{j+1}$.
Index from one, put $d_j=f'(B_j)$ and $d_0=0$. For
$D_j=\sum_{h\ge j}(B_h-\alpha_h)\ge0$, concavity and summation
by parts give
\[
 \sum_j[f(\alpha_j)-f(B_j)]\le
 -\sum_j(d_j-d_{j-1})D_j.
\]
Only positions in the list are needed, since the next tail has
bound zero. For $1\le h\le s$, $B_{j-1}+h\ge2(B_j+h)$
when $j>1$. Hence $d_j-d_{j-1}\ge d_j/2\ge s/(4B_j)$.
The empty list contributes zero. A single deficit $D_j\ge X$
therefore subtracts at least $Xs/(4B_j)$ from the logarithmic
bound, uniformly in all the other entries.

For example, suppose at a prescribed step $i$ the opposite-sign
total drops by $X>0$. A strip of that opposite sign must already
have occurred. Its most recent size $B_*$ is the entry at the
shifted deficit index in the final list. If all previous sizes
are at most $4b_*/p$ and $X>\Lambda b_*/(pn)$, this path loses
at least $\Lambda s/(16n)$ in the logarithmic estimate. Writing
$R$ for the unrestricted weighted count, the combined weight of
all paths with such a step is consequently at most
\[
 R\,(1+2aN)^{2N^2}(1+2\beta)^{2N^2}
          \exp[-\Lambda s/(16n)] .
\]
The same path is counted only once, by bounding its final weight
and using the bound on all unlabelled paths. The same argument
applies to a loss of negative mass at a final branching step:
deleting a fixed nonnegative head leaves that mass unchanged,
and the reference list of negative sizes no longer changes.

The other exclusion test compares the $r$th entry with two thirds
of the $r$th most recent vector size, counting the earlier final
steps among the vector steps. If $h<r$ fixed heads have already
been deleted, the tested entry lies at position $j=r-h$ of the
remaining row, and the reference size is $B_j$ in the positive
list of prescribed strips. Before branching take $h=0$. The
test is used only when this list has at least $j$ entries.
If the test fails, the positive part of the tested entry is at
most $2B_j/3$. The tail after it is bounded by the reference
tail after $j$, so the tail beginning at $j$ has deficit at
least $B_j/3$. Its propagation keeps the same reference size
at the shifted index. Thus the final log weight loses at least
$s/12$ in this case as well.

Taking the smaller of the two losses bounds the weight of the
union of the failures, with the same path-count prefactor.
In particular, if $s\ge n/2$ and $\ell=\log(1+2aN)$, their
total fraction is at most
\[
 \exp\{6N^2\ell-\min(\Lambda,n)/32\}.
\]
Here $1+2\beta\le(1+2aN)^2$. We check how the scale hypotheses
enter for degrees obtained by division in a frame. Continue to
write $n=k_0$. For an active residue $t$ let $A_t$ be the degree
remaining from its original degree in $[a,2a]$ after the reserved
factors. The determinant based at a letter takes its $r$ rows
from the preceding even letters. If $v$ is the oldest selected
row, the multiplicity is at most $2a/(yn^2p^{e_j-v})$.
Thus any selected row $u\ge v$ costs at most $2a/(yn^2)$ after
the substitution of weight $p^{e_j-u}$. One residue occurs at most
twice among the frame intervals in a period: its representative
in $(e_{j-1},e_j]$ can recur only among the designated rows in
the following frame. There are at most $N_s$ choices of a base
letter per occurrence and its rows are distinct. The total
determinant cost at $t$ is therefore at most $4aN_s/(yn^2)$.
In particular, if the earlier reserved degree is at most $a/4$
and $yn^2\ge16N_s$, then $a/2\le A_t\le2a$.

At position $u$ put $P_u=p^{b_j-u}$ and $L_u=a/P_u$.
For an interior prescribed row, removing its lower digit vector
from $A_t$ and dividing by $P_u$ costs less than $n$ relative
to $A_t/P_u$. At a row carried from the preceding frame the
prescribed degree is
\[
 \max\{0,\lfloor A_t/P_u\rfloor-(n+r+1)-(nN_s+1)\}.
\]
In either case the prescribed size $B_u$ lies between
$A_t/P_u-2nN_s$ and $A_t/P_u$ for $N_s\ge6$, $2\le r\le n$.
All frame letters satisfy $u\ge f_{j-1}$, so in the prime range
where $b_j=H_0+f_{j-1}$ we have $P_u\le U$, hence
$L_u\ge8yn^2N_s$. It follows, for $n\ge3$, that
\[
             \tfrac25 L_u\le B_u\le2L_u,\qquad B_u\ge n .
\]
The sign of the strip is determined by the parity of $u$.
Two successive prescribed positions of the same sign differ by
at least two. Their sizes thus grow by a factor at least $p^2/5$,
which exceeds four for $p\ge13$. At any later position $v$ all
previous prescribed sizes are at most $2L_v/p$. This supplies
the scale $b_*=L_v$ in the cancellation estimate.

The surviving paths also give bounds on the individual block
degrees. At a tested position $v$ let $o$ be the oldest of the
preceding $r$ vector steps, so exactly $r-1$ later vector steps
precede $v$. Interlacing bounds the current $r$th entry by the
largest entry just after $o$: trace backwards one coordinate
toward the first at each intervening vector step and keep the
coordinate at covector steps. That largest entry is bounded by
the sum of the vector degrees through $o$, which is at most
$B_o\sum_{h\ge0}4^{-h}<2B_o$. The lower test gives the other
bound, $2B_o/3$. Put $\xi_v=\Lambda L_v/(pn)$.
The first $r$ entries are nonnegative, since at most $N$ entries
are negative and $K\ge N$. At a covector step the decrease of the
sum of the first $r$ entries is consequently bounded by the loss
of total positive mass, at most $\xi_v$. At a vector step the
positive mass in the last $K$ coordinates can increase by at most
the old $r$th entry, by interlacing and cancellation of the two
shifted sums. All negative mass lies in those last coordinates,
and its decrease is at most $\xi_v$. The increase in their signed
sum is therefore at most $\xi_v+2B_o\le\xi_v+4L_o$.

For $N\le N_s$, $1+2aN_s\le a^2$ and
$\min(\Lambda,n)\ge1088N_s^2\log a$, the discarded fraction
is at most $a^{-22N_s^2}$, since $1088/32=34$ and
$6N^2\ell\le12N_s^2\log a$. In particular it is below
$a^{-N_s^2}$ for $a>1$. The parameter inequalities and the
reserved-degree bound must hold throughout the chosen weight box.

For the prefix conditions write all coordinates as rows.
For a single prefix conjugate $\operatorname{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)$
by a generic matrix, obtaining $A$. With row coordinates the
substitution on the prefix is $x\mapsto xA$, $y\mapsto yA^{-t}$
on vectors and covectors respectively. In coordinates the coefficient
of $y_b$ in the transformed $y_c$ is $(A^{-1})_{cb}$. In particular
$(xA)(yA^{-t})^t=xy^t$, as required by contraction.

The same test controls division by an unpowered frame determinant.
In a prefix $S$ of width $r$ select $r$ distinct vector rows with
matrix $D$ and determinant $\Delta$. For a row-multihomogeneous
polynomial $F$ let $B$ sum its degrees in the other vector rows
of $S$. A least Laurent exponent at least $B+m$, for integral
$m\ge0$, implies $\Delta^m\mid F$ over the coefficient domain.

Indeed, pass to the fraction field of the coefficients. The generic
determinant is irreducible: comparing extreme row and column
degrees shows that any proper nonconstant factor would use a proper
nonempty set of rows and an equally large set of columns, exclusively.
Some permutation monomial matches a selected row outside those
columns, a contradiction. Localize the polynomial ring at $(\Delta)$.
This gives a discrete valuation ring. Modulo its uniformizer
$\Delta$, the rank of $D$ is $r-1$ since an $(r-1)$-minor survives.
Lift a basis ending in a kernel vector to an invertible matrix $P$
over this ring. Then the last column of $DP$ is divisible by
$\Delta$. Put $A(t)=P\operatorname{diag}(1,\ldots,1,t)P^{-1}$.
Specialization to $P$ preserves the vanishing Laurent coefficients
since $\det P$ is a unit. In the transformed polynomial substitute
the prefix preimages $xA(\Delta)^{-1}$ and $yA(\Delta)^t$ and set
$t=\Delta$, recovering $F$. All covector preimages are integral,
as are the selected vector preimages by the last-column property.
Every other vector costs at most one denominator per unit of row
degree. Each Laurent coefficient retains the row degrees, so every
term has valuation at least $(B+m)-B=m$. This proves divisibility
over the field. Division by $\Delta^m$ has unit leading coefficient
and the same remainder over the domain and its fraction field,
giving integral divisibility as well. On any other prefix the
updated rows of $D$ differ from the original rows by multiples
of a common row times $t-1$. Multilinearity makes its determinant
at most linear in $t$, and its value at $1$ is $\Delta$. By
additivity of least exponents, division by $\Delta^m$ therefore
costs at most $m$ in every other prefix test.

With the dual action, the Laurent exponents on any prefix ending
at a row $\lambda$ lie in $[\lambda_{\rm last},\lambda_1]$ for
each standard word. Indeed the partial exterior tensor at a
positive level of height $h$ transforms by $\bigwedge^h A$.
Its exponents are zero for $h=0$, one for $h$ equal to the block
dimension, and lie in $\{0,1\}$ otherwise. At a nonpositive
level the tensor has complementary degree in the dual space, so
the corresponding exponents are zero at full height, minus one
at height zero, and lie in $\{-1,0\}$ otherwise. These assertions
follow by diagonalizing $A$ over the field of rational functions
in the conjugating matrix. Subsequent exterior operations on
untransformed rows take linear combinations with coefficients
independent of $t$. Adding the lower and upper endpoints over
the levels gives the two extremal entries of $\lambda$, by the
definition of column height. The argument also works with $t$ in
the first diagonal position.

In particular, at an even row the largest tail exponent through
that row is at most its new first tail entry. Interlacing bounds
this entry by the old last head entry, which bounds the head
exponents from below on the preceding prefix. At an odd row the
same comparison uses the old first tail entry and the new last
head entry. On both head prefixes the factor consisting of the
determinant of the previous $r$ vector rows to the power
$m_0=nN_s+1$ increases the lower exponent by $m_0$: all its
rows lie in the prefix and $\det A=t$. The extra monomials in
vector rows have only nonnegative prefix exponents. This gives
the required separation of the two block exponents for the dual
substitution. The row degrees can be checked before any change
of basis. Each changed column height inserts one variable from
the current row by wedge or contraction. Thus each term has
row degree equal to the size of that strip, summed over the two
blocks. At a carried row write
$b_W=\lfloor A_t/P_u\rfloor-(n+r+1)$. To check the incoming
inequalities write $P_u=Pq$ for the previous digit size $P$
and its next radix $q$. The carry is
$b=(A_t-|v|-Pd)/(Pq)$ with $|v|<nP$ and $0\le d<rq$, so
$b>A_t/P_u-n/q-r\ge b_W$. Also $A_t/P_u\ge4yn^2N_s$
by the reserved-degree bound above, giving $b_W\ge nN_s+1=m_0$
for $n\ge3$, $r\le n$, $N_s\ge6$. The strip supplies $b_W-m_0$ variables,
the determinant power supplies $m_0$, and the additional power
of the first coordinate supplies $b-b_W$. At an interior row the
strip supplies the entire prescribed degree. At a designated
outgoing row its degree $c_i$ becomes $c_i+\sum_h w_{ih}$, exactly
the selected degree. Moreover $c_i\le2\beta$: interlacing bounds
the sum of the increases of the entries by the new first entry
minus the old last entry, and both have magnitude at most $\beta$.
Every coordinate exponent in the first block is consequently
at most $w_{ih}+2\beta<q_*$. The residue and multidegree
conditions therefore hold term by term.

These checks give membership in the local polynomial module as
follows. Its generators are products of a head polynomial in the
first $r$ variables per row and a tail polynomial in the other
$K$. The tail uses no carried row: the first $r$ steps are
upward from the zero row, so after $i\le r$ steps at most $i$
entries are positive and none is negative. Every last-block
component there has degree zero. The head uses only frame rows.
The separating integer for a tested row is the first tail entry
after an even step or before an odd step. The prefix bounds above
give its required upper bound on the tail exponents and the lower
bound with offset $m_0$ on the head exponents. The additional
lower bound on the head before either step follows from the test
$2B_o/3$. In an even row the restricted monomial degree counts
the tail variables and is at most $\xi_v+4L_o$; in an odd row
it counts the head variables and is at most $\xi_v$. Indeed the
degrees of the two exterior words are the corresponding block
increments in absolute value. The added powers in tested rows
use head variables only in the even designated rows. This
verifies the generator conditions together with the prescribed
row degrees and digit bounds, for incoming degrees and frame
parameters satisfying the inequalities above.

The frame minors also distinguish the rational planes. Write
$I_0=\{1,\ldots,r_0\}$, let $D_j(I)$ denote the powered row
minor in frame $j$ on columns $I$, and write $D_j=D_j(I_0)$.
Set $h_j=p^{e_{j+1}-e_j}$ and take the polynomial
\[
 D_0\prod_{|I|=r_0,\ |I\cap I_0|=r_0-1}
 \left(\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}D_j^{h_j-1}
       -\prod_{j=0}^{J-1}D_j(I)^{h_j-1}\right).
\]
On its own plane all the replacement minors vanish modulo the
uniformizer. The displayed reduction is therefore the product
of the $D_j$ with multiplicities $\alpha_j$ computed below.
It is nonzero by the distinct-row specialization above.
More precisely, let $L$ denote the displayed polynomial and
$L_0=\prod_jD_j^{\alpha_j}$. After substitution on the chosen
plane each $D_j(I)$ in a replacement factor has all coefficients
in $(w)$. In the expansion of $L-L_0$, the term indexed by a
nonempty set of $b$ replacements therefore belongs to
$(w^{b\sum_j(h_j-1)})$. The same substitution sends
$L^c-L_0^c$ into $(w^{\sum_j(h_j-1)})$ for every $c\ge1$, by
factoring the difference of powers. This precision concerns
the separating polynomial itself, before any division used to
normalize a source vector.

For a different plane take adapted invertible matrices $g,g'$ for
the chosen and the tested plane, respectively. The first $r_0$
rows of $\overline{g'^{-1}g}$ form a matrix $H$ over
$\mathbb F_{p^{n'}}$. On reduction each even row at position $v$
has coefficients from $H$ raised to $p^v$, with Frobenius
exponents read modulo $n'$. The further row power $p^{e_j-v}$
therefore gives the same coefficient matrix $H^{[p^{e_j}]}$
for every row in the frame. More explicitly, that row is
$(\sum_b Z_{vb}H_{bc}^{p^v})^{p^{e_j-v}}$ in column $c$,
where $b$ ranges from $1$ to $r_0$. Consequently the reduced
minor on $I$ is $U_j\det(H_I)^{p^{e_j}}$, with the same
variable determinant $U_j$ for every column set.

If $\det(H_{I_0})=0$, already $D_0$ reduces to zero. Otherwise
$H_{I_0}$ is invertible. There is a nonzero column outside
$I_0$: if all vanished, the first $r_0$ rows of $\bar g'^{-1}$
would lie in the span of the first $r_0$ rows of $\bar g^{-1}$,
and equality of dimensions would give the same plane. By Cramer's
rule some one-column replacement $I$ thus has nonzero determinant.
For either of these two nonzero minors the scalar contributed to
its product over frames equals one. Indeed translate all endpoints
by a common multiple of $n'$ to make $e_0\ge0$. Then
\[
 \sum_{j=0}^{J-1}p^{e_j}(h_j-1)
       =p^{e_0}(p^{n'}-1),
\]
and every nonzero element of $\mathbb F_{p^{n'}}$ raised to that
power is one. The two products in this replacement factor both
reduce to $\prod_j U_j^{h_j-1}$. This proves coefficientwise
divisibility by the uniformizer $w$ on every other plane.

In particular the $c$th power of this polynomial supplies $c$
additional powers of $w$ off the chosen plane. If the remaining
integral factors in a summand have substituted orders at least
$o_1,\ldots,o_d$ there, multiplication by $w^i$ gives order at
least $i+\sum o_l+c$. Normalizing by $w^{i+\sum o_l}$ leaves
order at least $c$; the same lower bound survives a finite sum.
This accounts for the extra separation once the orders of the
individual generator factors and the realization on the chosen
plane have been verified.

For the factors of order one, fix a row $s$ and the $r_0$
preceding even frame rows $v_l<s$ used with it. Put
$q_l=p^{s-v_l}$ and $B_{li}=X_{v_l,i}^{q_l}$ for $i\in I_0$,
reading variable indices modulo $n'$. In the chosen coordinates
write $x_v$ for a vector row and $y_s$ for a covector row.
For even $s$ and $c\notin I_0$, take the determinant on
columns $(1,\ldots,r_0,c)$ with $x_s$ first and the rows
$x_{v_l}^{[q_l]}$ below it (brackets denote entrywise powers).
For odd $s$, take the contraction $x_{v_l}^{[q_l]}y_s^t$
for each $l$. On any tested plane with the same dimension,
reduction places the powered rows in the same Frobenius twist
of the plane. For even $s$, the first row also belongs to that
space of dimension $r_0$, so the determinant vanishes there.
For odd $s$, the covector belongs to its annihilator: the
inverse transpose in the dual change of coordinates preserves
the pairing after the common Frobenius twist. Hence each of
these polynomials is divisible by $w$ after substitution.

On the chosen plane the substitutions are $(X_{\rm head},
wX_{\rm tail})$ for vectors and $(wX_{\rm head},X_{\rm tail})$
for covectors. In the determinant expand down the last column.
Every entry below the first contains $w^{q_l}$ with $q_l\ge2$.
In the contraction the same power divides every tail summand.
Consequently, after division by $w$ the respective reductions
modulo $w$ are
\[
 (-1)^{r_0}X_{s,c}\det B,\qquad
 \sum_{i\in I_0} B_{li}X_{s,i}.
\]
Thus products of these normalized factors have the products of
the indicated symbols as their reductions, by multiplicativity
of the residue map.

The precision needed in realizing the symbols can also be tracked
before counting them. Suppose that on the chosen edge the realized
columns differ from the detected model columns by $w^b$ times the
edge lattice. If $w^2=p$ and $b\ge2(D+1)$, pairing any integral
linear combination of these differences with an integral detector
changes its value by a multiple of $p^{D+1}$. Thus every primitive
combination detected to depth $D$ retains its detector. On another
edge, multiplication by a tail vector with denominator at most
$w^o$ leaves precision at least $w^{b-o}$ if the two edge lattices
multiply into the lattice for the sum of their weights. Taking
$b\ge c_*+o$ preserves the required off-edge precision $w^{c_*}$.
Both estimates are uniform in the number of columns.

Summing over exactly
the $Q$ graph edges above cancels $Q$ even when the right side
is negative. For $A\ge0$ and $0\le B\le1$,
$1-(1-A)(1-B)^s=1-(1-B)^s+A(1-B)^s\le sB+A$.
Thus the exceptional fraction contributed by this estimate is at
most $s k_0(Cr_0^2/y+c_0/a)$, also bounded trivially by one.
In particular, for this contribution the target $s x^{-12}$
is met throughout any weight box on which
$y\ge2C r_0^2 k_0 x^{12}$ and $a\ge2k_0c_0x^{12}$.
More precisely, write $L_1$ for the lower spacing parameter,
$g$ for the gap exponent, $c_*$ for the required separation
at other edges and $c'$ for the offset bound. For $s,r_0>0$
two size conditions for that construction are
\[
 256 k_0^2 s\,y^2\le p^{L_1-1},\qquad
 C_1 k_0^3 r_0^2 s\,p^{5g}
 (c_*+r_0s c_0+2r_0 n'c')y\le a .
\]
These two restrictions can be checked together in the range
$13\le p<k_0^2x^{26}$. Fix $\varepsilon$ and take $y=x^{26}$,
$L_1=\lceil\log_p((k_0x^{26})^4)\rceil$. Then
$p^{L_1-1}\ge (k_0x^{26})^4/p>k_0^2x^{78}$. As $s\le n\le x^3$
eventually, this proves the first restriction. For the second, put
$a_0=x\exp(x^\varepsilon)$, $b'=a_0\exp(-x^{\varepsilon/3})$.
The local value of $a$ is $a_0-4S_0$, where the scale condition is
$k_0S_0\le b'/(xn)$. Thus $a\sim a_0$,
$c'=\lfloor4S_0\rfloor=O(b')$ and
$c_0=\lfloor2b'+8S_0\rfloor=O(b')$. Here $r_0$ is fixed for
fixed $\varepsilon$, $n'\le n$, and $k_0=k\le n^2\sqrt{x}/u$.
The gap $g$ is the product of a factor $O_\varepsilon(1+\log x)$
and $(6r_0+1)\lceil x^{\varepsilon/8}\rceil$. Hence
$5g\log p=O_\varepsilon(x^{\varepsilon/8}(\log x)^2)
=o(x^{\varepsilon/3})$. For clarity, the separation exponent is
$c_*=8(D+1)$, where $D$ rounds up a sum of three terms: a constant
times $40n'x^{-\varepsilon/8}(N_1^3+r_0^5+k_0^2)\log_p a_0$,
$2r_0n'(4S_0)$ and $40r_0n'x^{-\varepsilon/8}(2b'+8S_0)$.
The orbit window count $N_1=O_\varepsilon(1+\log x)$, since it
rounds the maximum of $6\exp(1)g x^{-\varepsilon/8}$ and
$(5+8\varepsilon)\log_2 x$ up and adds one. Consequently
$c_*=O_\varepsilon(x^K b')$ for some fixed $K$. Dividing the
second restriction's left side by $a$ now gives at most
$\exp(-x^{\varepsilon/3}+o(x^{\varepsilon/3}))$.
The same estimates control the degree reserved before counting
paths. Put $H=p^g$ and
$c=c_*+r_0s c_0+2r_0n'c'$. In the reserving product a frame
minor at $j$ occurs with exponent $c(1+\alpha_j)$, where
\[
 \alpha_j=\mathbf1_{\{j=0\}}+
 r_0(k_0-r_0)(p^{e_{j+1}-e_j}-1).
\]
The second family consists of one minor per marked residue with
exponent $(2H+3)c+k_0H$. Each frame minor has total degree at
most $r_0H$: its rows have degrees $p^{e_j-v}$ at the frame
positions $v$. For the minor at a marked position $u$ the rows
are selected from the preceding even marked positions, with
degrees $p^{u-v}$. The previous frame supplies $r_0$ such
positions if $e_{j-1}<u\le e_j$, and
$u-f_{j-1}\le g$. This gives the same degree bound.
There are at most $n'$ factors of each family before taking powers,
since the frame endpoints are distinct modulo $n'$. Thus the
earlier reserve at any residue is bounded by the total degree
\[
 n'r_0H\big[c(2+r_0k_0H)+(2H+3)c+k_0H\big].
\]
All quantities in brackets apart from $c$ and $H$ grow at most
polynomially in $x$, and $c=O_\varepsilon(x^{C_2}b')$ for a
fixed $C_2$. Since $\log H=o(x^{\varepsilon/3})$, division by
$a$ bounds the reserve fraction by
$\exp(-x^{\varepsilon/3}+o(x^{\varepsilon/3}))$. In particular
it is at most $1/4$ uniformly for these primes once $x$ is large.

For the concentration estimate the dimension denoted there by
$n$ is $k_0$. The lower bounds $k_0\ge xm$ and
$m\ge\sqrt{x}/u$ give $k_0\ge x^{3/2-\varepsilon}$.
Since $N_s\le N_1=O_\varepsilon(1+\log x)$ and
$\log a\sim x^\varepsilon$, we have
$N_s^2\log a=o(k_0)$ in the stated range of $\varepsilon$.
With $\Lambda=C_\Lambda N_s^2\log a$ and fixed
$C_\Lambda\ge1088$, this proves the required lower bound on
$\min(\Lambda,k_0)$. It also gives $k_0-r_0-2N\ge k_0/2$
for $N\le N_s$. Also $1+2aN_s\le a^2$ and
$yk_0^2\ge16N_s$ since $y=x^{26}$. These inequalities verify the reserve
and concentration parameters for the prescribed frames throughout
this prime range.
Finally $k_0=O(x^{13/2})$, so both $Cr_0^2 k_0/y$ and $k_0c_0/a$
are $o(x^{-12})$. The per-edge rank estimate, when available,
therefore meets the required local loss budget for these primes
uniformly over the indicated box.
In Smith coordinates for $F$, at least $n$ diagonal entries divide
$p^D$. Indeed, if there were fewer, select the corresponding rows
of $V$ after the Smith change of coordinates. Their common kernel
over the fraction field contains a nonzero vector. Rescale it to
an integral vector with a unit coordinate. On this vector the
remaining diagonal entries, including any zeros, give an image in
$\pi^{hD+1}P$. Applying the integral $U$ contradicts
$UFV=p^D I_n$. Thus a basis of $P$ has at most $\kappa_v d_v$
exceptions to $p^D b\in\operatorname{im} F$.

At a fixed vertex, form products of these basis vectors over the
local factors. Let $B_0$ be a nonempty subset of the factors. Modulo
the sum of the incoming images in the factors of $B_0$, multiplication
by $p^D$ kills every product having even one good coordinate there:
apply the preceding representation in that coordinate and leave the
others fixed. Consequently at most
$(\prod_v d_v)\prod_{v\in B_0}\kappa_v$ product vectors are
exceptional. This count uses the cartesian product of the exceptional
index sets and requires no independence of the arithmetic weights.
For finitely many vertex representatives take the union of their
exceptional vectors transported to the common lattice $L$. Its
cardinality is at most the sum of the local bounds. Write $J$ for
the sum of their span and the incoming images. If the transported
vertex generators span $L$ and their unwanted edge contributions
belong to $p^{D+1}L$, then $p^D L\subset J+p^{D+1}L$.
In the local coefficient ring with $w^2=p$, it suffices that the
normalized edge vector at every other target belong to $w^c$ times
its target lattice, with $c\ge2(D+1)$. Indeed
$w^c=p^{D+1}w^{c-2(D+1)}$; tensoring with integral vectors in
the other factors and transporting by lattice isomorphisms preserves
this inclusion. The extra
power removes the error exactly: the image of $p^D L$ modulo $J$
is a finitely generated module $Q$ with $Q=pQ$. Nakayama's lemma
gives $Q=0$. For the arithmetic operators one still needs the
local pairing matrices, this off-vertex divisibility, the estimate
for iterated operators and the mean of the local loss fractions.

The slope excess in this bound has a quantitative test. At one
complex place let $B_i:V_i\to W$ be the normalized component maps,
before projection onto the target of dimension $N_C$. Put
$N=\dim W$, $d=\sum_i\dim V_i>0$ and $Q=\sum_iB_iB_i^*$.
Suppose $N_C\ge(1-\delta)N>0$, and
\[
 \sum_i\|B_i^*B_i-I\|_{\rm HS}^2\le ed,\qquad
 \operatorname{tr}(Q^2)\le(1+\eta)(\operatorname{tr}Q)^2/N,
 \qquad 0\le e\le1/4,\quad \eta\ge0 .
\]
The trace test can be applied before restriction to the integral
components. Write $B_i=C_iV_i'$, where $C_i$ acts on a space of
dimension $r_i$ and $V_i'$ is an isometric inclusion. Include the
positive normalization of the period map in $C_i$. Put $R=\sum_i r_i$
and $d\ge(1-\lambda)R$. Suppose the full period kernels satisfy
\[
 \|C_i^*C_i-I\|_{\rm HS}^2\le e_0r_i,\qquad
 \|C_i^*C_j\|_{\rm HS}^2\le(1+\eta_0)r_ir_j/D_*
 \quad(i\ne j),
\]
with $D_*>0$ and $\eta_0\ge0$. Compression by isometries contracts
the Hilbert--Schmidt norm. Hence the sum of the squared diagonal
errors on the restricted spaces is at most $e_0R$ and
$\operatorname{tr}Q\ge d-\sqrt{de_0R}\ge
R(1-\lambda-\sqrt{e_0})$. Cyclicity of trace gives
$\operatorname{tr}(Q^2)=\sum_{i,j}\|B_i^*B_j\|_{\rm HS}^2$.
For $i=j$ the triangle inequality on the full space bounds the
summand by $r_i(1+\sqrt{e_0})^2$. For $i\ne j$ use the second
kernel bound. Consequently, when $\lambda+\sqrt{e_0}<1$,
\[
 \frac{N\operatorname{tr}(Q^2)}{(\operatorname{tr}Q)^2}
 \le \frac{(N/R)(1+\sqrt{e_0})^2+(1+\eta_0)N/D_*}
          {(1-\lambda-\sqrt{e_0})^2}.
\]
In particular if $R\ge D_*/z$, $N\le(1+z)D_*$ and
$0\le\lambda,\sqrt{e_0},\eta_0\le z\le1/10$, one may take $e\le2z^2$ and $\eta\le12z$ in the trace test.
Indeed compression gives $e\le e_0/(1-\lambda)$, and subtracting
one from the stated upper bound after substitution gives at most
$(7z+3z^3+z^4)/(1-2z)^2\le12z$. In particular, for a slope
range $H\le bx^C$ and $z=x^{-C}$, $C>0$, these two errors
contribute at most $18b$ to $(\eta+4\sqrt e)H$, uniformly in the
dimensions. In this application the required inequality
$R\ge D_*/z$ follows if each full component has
$r_i\ge D_*/(C_0\Gamma^2)$ and there are at least
$C_0\Gamma^2/z$ components, by summing over $i$.

Deleting a fraction $p$ of the total source dimension leaves an image
whose codimension in $W$ is at most $N(\eta+4p+4\sqrt e)$.
Indeed let $Q'$ be the sum after restricting the maps to the retained
subspaces. Cauchy--Schwarz against the discarded orthogonal
projections gives $\operatorname{tr}(Q-Q')\le d(p+\sqrt{pe})$.
Against the identities it gives $\operatorname{tr}Q\ge d(1-\sqrt e)$.
Thus the fraction $z$ of trace lost is at most $2(p+\sqrt e)$.
Since $0\le Q'\le Q$, one has
$\operatorname{tr}(Q'^2)\le\operatorname{tr}(Q^2)$ by taking
the trace of $(Q-Q')(Q+Q')$. For $Q'\ne0$, Cauchy--Schwarz on
its nonzero eigenvalues gives
$\operatorname{rank}Q'\ge N(1-z)^2/(1+\eta)$, and this last
bound also holds if $Q'=0$. Taking complements proves the claim.

For the application, use the shifted source slopes (subtract $h_i$
on the $i$th component), counted with multiplicity. They have mean
$\sigma$. Suppose each is at most $\sigma+U$, and the smallest
target slope is at least $\sigma-H$, where $U,H\ge0$. At threshold
$\sigma-t$ retain the source filtration spaces of slopes at least
that threshold. Their discarded fraction is at most $U/(U+t)$
for $t>0$, since the nonnegative deficits from $\sigma+U$ have
mean $U$. If the projected maps carry these spaces into the target
filtration at the same threshold, the number of target slopes below
it is at most the codimension just bounded: projection onto the
target cannot increase absolute codimension. Integrating that
number over $0<t<H$ sums the positive deficits of the target
slopes from $\sigma$. Their mean is at least $\sigma-\mu_C$, so
\[
 \sigma-\mu_C\le
 \frac{4U\log(1+H/U)+(\eta+4\sqrt e)H}{1-\delta},
\]
with the logarithmic term interpreted as zero for $U=0$. This
proves the asserted estimate for filtered maps.

For Hermitian lattices the filtration condition follows from an
integral map $f_i:N_i\to C$ of height at most $h_i$, provided
its realization at the chosen place agrees with the projection of
$B_i$ up to change of coordinates and scalar normalization. Here
the target coordinates are common to all $i$. To check the height
factor, use slopes equal to twice the negative log covolume per
real dimension plus the common field constant. For a nonzero map
over a totally imaginary field $K$, put
\[
 h(f_i)=\frac1{n_\infty}\sum_{w\mid\infty}\log\|f_{i,w}\|^2,
 \qquad n_\infty=[K:\mathbb Q]/2 ,
\]
using one complex place per conjugate pair. For any source
sublattice $L$, every nonzero torsion-free quotient of $f_i(L)$
of $K$-rank $k$ is the image of the corresponding quotient of
$L$ of the same rank. Give both quotients the quotient metrics.
At each place the induced map has operator norm at most
$\|f_{i,w}\|$, by minimizing over representatives. The real
Jacobian is at most $\|f_{i,w}\|^{2k}$ by the singular-value
formula. The image lattice is the entire quotient in question,
so its covolume is at most the source quotient covolume times
the product of these Jacobians. Taking normalized slopes shows
that its slope is at least $\mu_{\min}(L)-h(f_i)$. The minimum
over quotients has the same lower bound. Applying this to the
source filtration step of minimum slope at least $\sigma-t+h_i$
puts its image in the target step at $\sigma-t$, by the defining
maximality of the Harder--Narasimhan step. Zero images satisfy
the inclusion as well. Thus the remaining arithmetic requirements
here are integrality and the height bounds for the actual maps,
their identification at the chosen place, and their trace bounds.

For the trivial Dirichlet character the auxiliary fields in one
cyclotomic construction of these intervals admit an elementary
description. Let $q\equiv3\pmod8$ and $d\equiv7\pmod{16}$ be primes,
$e\ge0$, and $m=2^{e+2}$. In the cyclotomic field of conductor $mqd$,
consider the subgroup of units $a$ satisfying
\[
 a\equiv\pm1\pmod m,\qquad
 \left(\frac{a}{q}\right)=\left(\frac{a}{d}\right)=1.
\]
Its fixed field is
\[
 E=\mathbb Q(\zeta_m+\zeta_m^{-1},\sqrt{-q},\sqrt{-d}),
 \qquad [E:\mathbb Q]=2^{e+2}.
\]
Indeed the Chinese remainder theorem splits the unit group into three
factors. The first subgroup fixes the maximal real subfield of
$\mathbb Q(\zeta_m)$. For $p=q,d$ the quadratic Gauss sum
$\sum_{a\bmod p}(a/p)\zeta_p^a$ transforms by the Legendre character
and its square is $-p$, since $p\equiv3\pmod4$. Thus the stabilizer of
all three generators is precisely the displayed subgroup. Its index is
$(\varphi(m)/2)\cdot2\cdot2=2^{e+2}$. Passing to any cyclotomic
conductor divisible by $mqd$ takes the inverse image of the same
subgroup and leaves its fixed field unchanged. In particular, taking
$e=\lceil(2+8\varepsilon)\log_2 x\rceil$ for $x\ge1$ gives
$4x^{2+8\varepsilon}\le [E:\mathbb Q]<8x^{2+8\varepsilon}$.
This determines the fields and their degrees; a uniform arithmetic
estimate on these varying fields is still needed in the construction.

The parameters controlling the twists can be constructed for a
matrix on a free $\mathbb Z_\ell$-module. Let $S$ have size $r$ and
let $u\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$ satisfy $v_\ell(u-1)=c\ge1$,
with $c\ge2$ for $\ell=2$. Fix $j\ge0$ and put $v=u^{\ell^j}$.
For $a,b\in\mathbb Z_\ell$, $a\ne b$, one has
\[
 |v^a-v^b|_\ell=\ell^{-(c+j)}|a-b|_\ell .
\]
For integral exponents this follows by binomial expansion: raising
a principal unit to a power prime to $\ell$ preserves the valuation
of its difference from one, and raising it to $\ell$ increases that
valuation by one under the stated condition on $c$. Extension by
continuity gives the displayed formula and defines the powers on
$\mathbb Z_\ell$.

For each eigenvalue $z$ of $S$ in a splitting field, counted with
multiplicity, choose $s_z\in\mathbb Z_\ell$ minimizing
$|v^s z-1|_\ell$; compactness ensures existence. The eigenvalues
are integral since the characteristic polynomial is monic. If $z$
is a unit, the ultrametric inequality and minimality imply
$|v^a-v^{s_z}|_\ell\le |v^a z-1|_\ell$. If $|z|_\ell<1$,
the right side is one and the inequality still holds. Multiplying
over the eigenvalues gives
\[
 v_\ell(\det(v^a S-I))\le r(c+j)+\sum_z v_\ell(a-s_z),
\]
with $v_\ell(0)=+\infty$. Thus when every distance is at least
$\ell^{-B}$ the determinant is nonzero and its valuation is at most
$r(c+j+B)$. By Smith normal form this bounds the length of the
cokernel on the free module. This also gives a single list of centres for all layers of a
finitely generated module, including its torsion. Let $M$ have at most
$R\ge1$ generators and let $g$ act unipotently on $M/\ell M$.
Choose generators lifting a basis of $M/\ell M$ and write
$\pi:\mathbb Z_\ell^r\twoheadrightarrow M$, $r\le R$, for the
resulting presentation (Nakayama's lemma). Lift the images of the
generators under $g$ to obtain a matrix $A$ with $\pi A=g\pi$.
Its reduction modulo $\ell$ is unipotent since $\pi$ induces an
isomorphism modulo $\ell$. Put $j_0=\lfloor\log_\ell R\rfloor+3$.
Apply the preceding determinant estimate just once, with $j=j_0$ and
$S=A^{\ell^{j_0}}$. Write $H=u^a A$ for an integral twist $a\ge1$.
For $m\le j_0$, $H^{\ell^m}-I$ divides $H^{\ell^{j_0}}-I$ in
the polynomial ring evaluated at $H$, so the earlier cokernel is a
quotient of the later one.

For $m\ge j_0$ put $U=H^{\ell^m}$. Then $U\equiv I\pmod{\ell^2}$:
modulo $\ell$, $(H-I)^r=0$, so $H^{\ell^k}\equiv I\pmod\ell$
once $\ell^k\ge r$, and one further $\ell$th power gives the stated
congruence. Consequently $I+U+\cdots+U^{\ell-1}$ is $\ell$ times
a matrix congruent to $I$ modulo $\ell$. The determinant valuation
of $U^\ell-I$ therefore increases by exactly $r$ whenever
$\det(U-I)\ne0$. The presentation induces a surjection from each
free cokernel onto the corresponding cokernel on $M$. It follows
that outside the balls of radius $\ell^{-B}$ around the same $r$
centres the length on $M$ at every layer $m$ is at most
$r(\max(m,j_0)+c+B)$. The zero module satisfies this directly.
Identification of these module cokernels with arithmetic groups is
a separate descent assertion.

For the upper estimate the direction of the descent map matters:
an injection into a group of bounded length suffices. Here is the
injectivity argument for a nonzero cyclotomic twist. Let $P$ be the
Galois group at a finite layer, $H$ the kernel of its cyclotomic
character, and suppose $P/H$ is topologically generated by $\gamma$
whose character value $u$ has infinite order. Give
$D=\mathbb Q_\ell/\mathbb Z_\ell$ the action through the character
to the power $-e$, where $e\ne0$. Restriction of continuous cocycles
induces an injection
\[
 H^1(P,D)\longrightarrow \operatorname{Hom}_{\rm cont}(H,D).
\]
Indeed coboundaries restrict to zero since $H$ acts trivially. A
cocycle restricting to zero factors through $P/H$. Multiplication
by $u^{-e}-1$ is surjective on $D$, since this scalar is a nonzero
$\ell$-adic integer. Subtracting a coboundary therefore kills its
value on $\gamma$, and hence on all integral powers of $\gamma$.
Continuity and density kill the cocycle everywhere. This injection
restricts to the subgroup satisfying the local conditions. Let $X$ be the maximal abelian pro-$\ell$ quotient of $H$ on
which all inertia subgroups intersected with $H$, and all
decomposition subgroups above $\ell$ intersected with $H$, vanish.
For a class locally trivial at the primes above $\ell$ and
unramified elsewhere, its restriction $f$ factors through $X$:
a local coboundary evaluates to zero on $H$, where the coefficient
action is trivial. Continuity makes the factorization valid through
the closed subgroup generated by the local relations. Write $A$ for
conjugation by a lift of the generator on $X$. The cocycle identity
applied to $gh=(ghg^{-1})g$ gives
$f(ghg^{-1})=\chi(g)^{-e}f(h)$ for $h\in H$, where $\chi$ denotes
the cyclotomic character. Thus $f$ kills $(u^e A-I)X$. At the layer
generated in the cyclotomic quotient by $\gamma^{\ell^m}$ the
same identity gives the operator $(u^e A)^{\ell^m}-I$, using the same quotient $X$. Indeed, in a common algebraic
closure the subgroup $P_m$ of the layer contains $H$, so the kernel
of the restricted cyclotomic character is still $H$. For any
prolongation of a finite place the decomposition subgroup in $P_m$
is the intersection of $P_m$ with its stabilizer in $P$; inertia
is obtained by imposing trivial action on the residue field.
Intersecting either subgroup with $H$ thus gives the original
intersection. Taking all prolongations, with the same residue
characteristic condition above $\ell$, gives identical local relations.
For a finite cokernel its character group into
$\mathbb Q_\ell/\mathbb Z_\ell$ has the same length, by decomposition
into cyclic summands. Hence the injection suffices for the upper
estimate once the uniform generator bound for $X$ is established.
