One source of exceptional twists is proximity to a finite list
of $\ell$-adic parameters. Suppose a tower estimate applies to a
twist $j$ whenever $|j-s_i|_\ell\ge\ell^{-B}$ for each of $r$
parameters in a normed extension of $\mathbb Q_\ell$, with
$|\ell|_\ell=\ell^{-1}$. For an integer cutoff $Z\ge1$ take
$B=\lfloor\log_\ell Z\rfloor$. Each open ball of radius $\ell^{-B}$
contains at most one integer in $[1,Z]$. Indeed two distinct such
integers would have difference divisible by $\ell^{B+1}$, by the
ultrametric inequality, while their ordinary distance is less than
$Z<\ell^{B+1}$. Hence at most $r$ twists must be removed, regardless
of the positions of the parameters or the cutoff. In applying this
observation to varying fields, it is the number of parameters in the
tower estimate that must be bounded uniformly.

A finite exceptional set can be avoided on a whole square window
with explicit dependence on its cardinality. Suppose $0<\eta\le A$,
$X>1$, and $S$ is a set of at most $R$ exceptional twist indices.
Put $D=2A/\eta$ and $Y_i=X^{1/(2D^i)}$ for $0\le i\le R$.
For the window $[Y_i,Y_i^2]$ all the indices that could lie in
$(x^\eta,x^A]$ at some point of the window belong to
$(Y_i^\eta,Y_i^{2A}]$. These last intervals are pairwise disjoint:
the upper endpoint for $i+1$ equals the excluded lower endpoint for
$i$. At least one misses $S$. Its left endpoint $Y=Y_i$ satisfies
\[
 X^{1/(2D^R)}\le Y,\qquad Y^2\le X,\qquad
 S\cap(x^\eta,x^A]=\varnothing\quad(Y\le x\le Y^2).
\]
Several arithmetic estimates can use the same window by taking the
union of their exceptional sets before choosing $i$. Their sets may
vary with $X$ and with the auxiliary primes; a bound on the cardinality
of the union independent of these choices keeps $2D^R$ fixed.
Applying this argument requires proving that outside the excluded
indices the arithmetic estimates hold, as well as bounding their number.

For the modulus-one comparison one cohomological estimate at the
upper endpoint already controls an interval of fixed power length.
Fix the same pair $q,d$ throughout. Put $h=2+8\varepsilon$,
$e(u)=\lceil h\log_2 u\rceil$ and
\[
 E_u=\mathbb Q(\mu_{2^{e(u)+2}})^+
          \mathbb Q(\sqrt{-q},\sqrt{-d}).
\]
Indeed the fixing subgroup is defined by congruence to $\pm1$ on
the dyadic factor and the two trivial Legendre symbols. For
$1\le u\le b$ the extension $E_b/E_u$ is cyclic of degree
$d_u$ dividing $2^{e(b)-e(u)}$: restriction embeds its group
in the cyclic relative group of the real cyclotomic layers.
Let $L_{\ell,j}(E)$ be the $\mathbb Z_\ell$-length of the
localization kernel for $(\mathbb Q_\ell/\mathbb Z_\ell)(-j)$,
unramified away from $\ell$ and locally trivial above $\ell$.
Restriction carries this kernel into the corresponding kernel over
$E_b$, since it preserves the inertia and localization conditions.
At odd $\ell$ it is injective by corestriction, as $d_u$ is a power
of two. For $\ell=2$ inflate to absolute Galois cohomology and put
$M=(\mathbb Q_2/\mathbb Z_2)(-j)$. Inflation--restriction identifies
the kernel of restriction on absolute $H^1$ with the inflation of
$H^1(\operatorname{Gal}(E_b/E_u),M^{G_{E_b}})$.
Fix compatible places above two. The two quadratic factors there
are unramified or split, because $-q\equiv5$ and $-d\equiv1\bmod8$.
The remaining real dyadic cyclotomic layers are totally ramified,
with local degree equal to their global degree. Thus the local
relative Galois group maps isomorphically onto
$\operatorname{Gal}(E_b/E_u)$. Moreover the local and global
$2$-adic cyclotomic images over $E_b$ coincide. Globally the
intersection with the full dyadic cyclotomic tower is just the
specified real layer: the two quadratic factors and their product
each ramify at $q$ or $d$ and their biquadratic compositum is
disjoint from the full tower. Locally the same intersection holds
because the added factor is unramified while all further dyadic
cyclotomic extensions of the real layer are totally ramified.
Consequently $M^{G_{E_b}}=M^{G_{E_{b,w}}}$ at the chosen place $w$.
On the inflation kernels the local restriction map is therefore the
identity on $H^1$ of the same finite group with the same invariant
module. A locally trivial class in that kernel vanishes. Hence
$L_{\ell,j}(E_u)\le L_{\ell,j}(E_b)$ at every prime.

Take $b^{5/6}\le u\le b$ and $t(v)=\lfloor v^{401}\rfloor$.
Suppose at $b$ all lengths for $1\le j<t(b)$ are finite and their
sum weighted by $\log\ell$ over every finite set of primes is at
most $\exp(b^{4\varepsilon/5})$. Restricting the twist range
and applying restriction bounds the corresponding sum at $u$ by
the same $\exp(b^{4\varepsilon/5})$. Since
$b^{4\varepsilon/5}/u^\varepsilon\le b^{-\varepsilon/30}$,
the bound is at most $\exp(u^\varepsilon/2)$ uniformly for all
sufficiently large $b$. Fixed polynomial losses of the size
estimated below are absorbed in the same way. The interval starts
at $Y=b^{5/6}$ and ends at $Y^{6/5}$, which suffices for the pole
argument. To retain a full square window $[W,W^2]$, apply this
comparison on the four subintervals with consecutive endpoints
$W^{2^{i/4}}$, $0\le i\le4$, using $2^{1/4}<6/5$.
It remains to prove the stronger endpoint estimates for the same
auxiliary primes that give the three character bounds.

For the trivial character the number of primes above $q,d$ in
$\mathbb Q(\mu_{2^\infty})$ is uniformly bounded. In fact it is
exactly two above $q$ and four above $d$. At conductor $2^n$,
$n\ge4$, an odd prime $p$ has residue degree
$\operatorname{ord}_{2^n}(p)$ since Frobenius raises roots of
unity to the $p$th power. Put $b=v_2(p+1)$, equal to $2$ for
$q$ and $3$ for $d$. As $v_2(p-1)=1$, successive squaring gives
$v_2(p^{2^k}-1)=b+k$ for $k\ge1$: after the first square the
factor $p^{2^k}+1$ has valuation one. The order is therefore
$2^{n-b}$, and its index in the unit group is $2^{b-1}$.
The prime counts stabilize, giving the same counts in the union.
Thus allowing ramification at these two rational primes introduces
only six places in this tower, independently of their sizes.

Suppose the space of everywhere unramified quadratic characters
of this fixed infinite field has dimension at most $g$ over
$\mathbb F_2$. Allowing ramification above $q,d$ increases the
dimension by at most six: restriction to the six inertia groups
has unramified kernel, and each restriction contributes at most
one dimension since wild inertia has odd residue characteristic
and the tame inertia has procyclic pro-$2$ quotient.

Adjoining each of $\sqrt{-q},\sqrt{-d}$ at most doubles this
new dimension bound. Indeed take the maximal pro-$2$ Galois group
$P$ unramified outside these allowed places. Each quadratic step is
unramified outside them (the quadratic discriminants are $-q,-d$).
The maximal such extension upstairs lies in the same tower: the
normal closure downstairs of a finite Galois $2$-extension upstairs
has $2$-power degree, since over the quadratic field it is a compositum
of two conjugate $2$-extensions. Unramifiedness is preserved in the
compositum. If $\dim\operatorname{Hom}_{\rm cont}(P,\mathbb F_2)=h$,
then $P$ has $h$ topological generators: lifts of a basis modulo the
closed subgroup of squares and commutators generate, since every
proper closed subgroup is contained in an open subgroup of index two.
For an open subgroup of index two choose one generator outside it,
and multiply the other generators outside it by its inverse.
Rewriting words using the two coset representatives then gives at
most $1+2(h-1)$ generators upstairs (the square of that generator,
the remaining generators and their conjugates). A trivial field step
preserves the bound. After both steps the dimension is at most
$4(g+6)$, uniformly in $q,d$. Imposing extra local vanishing can
only reduce it. To obtain $g$, write $K_n=\mathbb Q(\mu_{2^n})$, $n\ge2$,
and $H_n=G_{K_n}$ inside a common absolute Galois group $P=G_{K_2}$.
Let $H=\bigcap H_n$. Any continuous quadratic character of $H$
factors through its image in some finite quotient $P/U$, since its
kernel is open in $H$ and the open normal subgroups of $P$ form a
neighbourhood basis. The images of $H_n$ eventually equal the image
of $H$: in each coset of $U$ this follows by nested compactness.
The character therefore extends to all sufficiently deep $H_n$.

If it is unramified on $H$, these extensions are eventually
unramified as well. Only the finitely many primes ramified in the
extension cut out by $U$ can matter. For each such prime of $K_2$,
fix an absolute inertia group $I$. In $P/U$ the images of
$H_n\cap I$ stabilize at the image of $H\cap I$ by the same
compactness argument. All other prolongations are conjugates;
normality of $H_n,H$ and $U$ makes this stabilization simultaneous
for all conjugates. The character vanishes on the resulting inertia
images since it did so on $H$. Thus any finite independent list of
unramified characters on $H$ extends to a single $H_n$ and remains
independent there by restriction. Class field theory identifies the
finite-layer character dimension with the $2$-rank of the ideal
class group (the fields are totally imaginary). In this particular tower one can take $g=0$. The base $K_2$
has class number one since the Gaussian integers are Euclidean.
At every quadratic step $K_{n+1}/K_n$ the unique prime over $2$
is totally ramified and no other prime ramifies (the translated
cyclotomic polynomial is Eisenstein at $2$, and the cyclotomic
discriminant is a power of $2$). Suppose inductively that $K_n$
has no unramified quadratic extension. If $K_{n+1}$ had one,
its normal closure over $K_n$ would have a finite $2$-group $P'$
as Galois group and would still be unramified over $K_{n+1}$,
being a compositum of conjugate unramified extensions. Inertia
over $2$ in $P'$ therefore has order two. Its normal closure is
all of $P'$: otherwise the quotient would have a quotient of order
two giving an unramified quadratic extension of $K_n$. The inertia
subgroup itself must equal $P'$, since any proper subgroup of a
finite $2$-group lies in a normal maximal subgroup. This forces
$|P'|=2$, contradicting the degree of the original extension.
Induction and the descent just proved give $g=0$, hence the bound
$24$ for the two auxiliary quadratic steps.

Consequently, at the prime two all but at most $24$ twist indices
satisfy the endpoint estimate with a polynomial bound. Take
$L_0=\mathbb Q(i,\sqrt{-q},\sqrt{-d})$ and
$L_\infty=L_0(\mu_{2^\infty})$. Its cyclotomic group over $L_0$
is $1+4\mathbb Z_2$, since the two odd-discriminant quadratic
factors are disjoint from the dyadic tower. For
$L_m=L_0(\mu_{2^{m+2}})$ the generator is the $2^m$th power of
the base generator. The quotient $X$ defined above is an abelian
pro-$2$ quotient of the group upstairs unramified outside $q,d$,
so the $24$-generator bound applies to it. The action of the
cyclotomic generator modulo two is unipotent: the image of a
pro-$2$ group on the finite space $X/2X$ is a $2$-group.
Apply the determinant estimate with $u=5$, $c=2$, $j_0=7$ and
$B=\lfloor\log_2 t\rfloor$, $t=\lfloor b^{401}\rfloor$.
For each pair $q,d$ the same list of at most $24$ centres works
in every layer. At most $24$ integers in $[1,t]$ are within the
excluded open balls. For every other twist the injection above
bounds the kernel length over $L_m$ by
$24(\max(m,7)+2+B)$.

For $m=e(b)$ we have $L_m=E_b(i)$, of degree at most two over
$E_b$. The restriction kernel on $H^1$ has length at most one:
by inflation--restriction it is cohomology of a cyclic group of
order at most two on a subgroup of $\mathbb Q_2/\mathbb Z_2$.
Evaluation of a cocycle on a generator identifies this cohomology
with a subquotient of that subgroup, killed by two. Restriction
preserves local triviality. Thus the total weighted contribution
over $E_b$ of all the nonexcluded indices is bounded by
\[
 t\log2\,\bigl[1+24(\max(e(b),7)+2+\lfloor\log_2 t\rfloor)\bigr].
\]
This is $O_\varepsilon(b^{401}\log b)$ uniformly in $q,d$.
For the excluded indices compare the length with that at the fixed layer $L_7$. In this tower the descent map to
characters of the twisted coinvariants is surjective. Indeed every
equivariant character extends to a cohomology class by
inflation--restriction since the quotient $\mathbb Z_2$ has
cohomological dimension one. At a place above two the extended
class is inflated from the local cyclotomic quotient because the
character kills the decomposition subgroup in the kernel. This
quotient is open in $\mathbb Z_2$ and its $H^1$ on the nonzero
divisible twist vanishes by the generator argument above. Away
from two inertia lies in the cyclotomic kernel and is killed by
the character. Hence the extension has all the prescribed local
conditions.

Write $H=5^j A$ on $X$ for twist $j$. On the free presentation
above $H^{2^7}\equiv I\bmod4$. For $m\ge7$ successive squaring
therefore gives $(H^{2^m}-I)X=2^{m-7}(H^{2^7}-I)X$:
each extra factor $I+H^{2^i}$ on the free lattice is twice
an automorphism commuting with $H$. Project the resulting equality
of images to $X$. The submodule $(H^{2^7}-I)X$ has at most
$24$ generators, as an image of $X$. Thus, whenever the length
at $L_7$ is finite, the length at $L_m$ exceeds it by at most
$24(m-7)$; for $m<7$ the earlier cokernel is a quotient of the
later one. This estimate also holds at a centre, where the free
determinant estimate alone can be infinite.

This also bounds the sum including the exceptional twists on a selected
square window. Write $C\ge1$ for the absolute exponent in the class
group estimate below. Fix $0<\eta=\min(1,\varepsilon/(4C))$,
$D=802/\eta$ and $J=2D^{24}$. Fix the auxiliary pair at the lower
scale $X^{1/J}$ with $\log(512qd)\ll_\varepsilon
(X^{1/J})^{\varepsilon/4}$. Form the exceptional set once at cutoff
$\lfloor X^{401}\rfloor$. The disjoint-interval argument above gives
$W\ge X^{1/J}$, $W^2\le X$ such that at every $b\in[W,W^2]$
each exceptional $j\le b^{401}$ satisfies $j\le b^\eta$.
Here $[L_7:\mathbb Q]\le1024$ and $|\operatorname{disc}L_7|
\le(512qd)^{1024}$ by the conductor-discriminant formula.
The fixed-layer bound below thus bounds each exceptional length by
$\exp(O_\varepsilon(b^{C\eta+\varepsilon/4}))$, and passage to
$E_b$ adds $O_\varepsilon(1+\log b)$ to each. For ordinary indices
use the determinant bound with $B=\lfloor\log_2\lfloor
X^{401}\rfloor\rfloor$ throughout; $\log X\le J\log b$.
Adding their polynomial contribution and the at most $24$ exceptional
terms bounds the entire dyadic weighted sum by
$\exp(b^{4\varepsilon/5})$ for sufficiently large $X$.

The passage from square windows to the intervals used in the pole
argument allows a fixed normalization of the degree. Fix $c\ge1$, put
$h=2+8\varepsilon$ and $k=c^{1/h}$. Suppose the arithmetic bounds
hold throughout $[W,W^2]$ for the dyadic exponents $e(u)$ with
$u^h\le2^{e(u)}\le2u^h$, up to twist $\lfloor u^{401}\rfloor$.
Set $Y=kW$. Once $W\ge\max(k^3,k^{400})$, each
$x\in[Y,Y^{3/2}]$ and $u=x/k$ both belong to $[W,W^2]$.
Indeed $(kW)^{3/2}\le W^2$ and $W\le u\le x$. Then
\[
 x^h\le c\,2^{e(u)}\le2x^h,\qquad
 x^{400}=k^{400}u^{400}\le u^{401}.
\]
Thus the same field at $u$ supplies the required degree at $x$,
and any bound on a sum of nonnegative lengths up to the original
cutoff applies up to $\lfloor x^{400}\rfloor$. Its allowed upper
bound $\exp(u^\varepsilon/2)$ can be enlarged to
$\exp(x^\varepsilon/2)$. If $W\ge X^{1/J}$ for arbitrarily large
$X$ with fixed $J\ge1$, then $Y$ is arbitrarily large as well.
The two arithmetic inputs have different quantifiers. Put
$Z=X^{1/J}$, so $X=Z^J$. Suppose one can select a pair $q,d$ at
every sufficiently large $Z$ satisfying the required congruences and
the three character bounds throughout $[Z,Z^J]$. Suppose also that
\emph{every} pair with those congruences and the prescribed prime
sizes admits the square window above with $W\ge Z$ and $W^2\le X$.
Choose the pair first, including the prime size conditions in the
selection. Its bounds persist on $[kW,(kW)^{3/2}]$, since this
interval lies in $[W,W^2]\subset[Z,Z^J]$. This establishes
simultaneity under the two stated inputs. It would not suffice to
have the square-window estimate for an unspecified second pair.

The uniformity in the real variable in the prime selection admits
the following reduction. For a nonempty finite set $P$ of candidate
odd primes and a fixed truncation $L$, write
\[
 S_p(y;b)=\sum_{n\le L}\Lambda(n)b_n(n/p)\min(1,y/n),
 \qquad |b_n|\le1,
\]
where $(n/p)$ is the Legendre symbol. Suppose that for every such
sequence $b$ and $Z\le y\le X$ the average over $p\in P$ of
$|S_p(y;b)|^2$ is at most $Cy\log y$, with $Z\ge2$.
For fixed $b$ and $\tau>0$, the fraction of primes at which
$|S_p(y;b)|>y^{1/2+\tau}/2$ somewhere on this interval tends to
zero as $Z\to\infty$, uniformly in $X,L,P$ for fixed $C$.

Indeed $S_p$ is piecewise linear, and its right derivative satisfies
$yS'_p(y;b)=S_p(y;(b_n\mathbf1_{n>y})_n)$. Thus the average of
$|S'_p(y;b)|^2$ is at most $C\log y/y$. On any block
$[V,W]\subset[Z,X]$ with $W\le2V$, the fundamental theorem of
calculus and Cauchy--Schwarz give
\[
 \sup_{V\le y\le W}|S_p(y;b)|^2
 \le 2|S_p(V;b)|^2+2V\int_V^W |S'_p(y;b)|^2\,dy .
\]
Its average is at most $4CV\log(2V)$. The exceptional fraction
on this block is therefore at most $16C V^{-2\tau}\log(2V)$.
Summing over $V=2^jZ\le X$ gives
$O_\tau(C Z^{-2\tau}\log(2Z))$, including the last partial block.
This estimate applies first to the candidates for $q$ with $b_n=1$.
For a fixed surviving $q$, it applies on the candidate set for $d$
with both $b_n=1$ and $b_n=(n/q)$; the union of the two exceptional
sets costs at most the sum of their fractions. This controls the
three truncated sums simultaneously, provided the stated mean
squares hold uniformly for these prime sets. Passing to $G_\chi$
also requires control of the logarithmic derivative tail beyond $L$.

Here is a sufficient character estimate for the mean square just used.
Take $X=Z^J$, $L=X^{400}$ with fixed $J>1$. Suppose the primes
in $P$ exceed $L^2$ and belong to one reduced residue class modulo
$M$, with $8\mid M$. Suppose every prime divisor of $M$ up to $L$
is at most $Z^{1/5}$. For odd squarefree $s\le L^2$ coprime to $M$,
assume
\[
 \left|\frac1{|P|}\sum_{p\in P}(p/s)\right|\le Z^{-2J}
\]
except at $s=1$ and at most one further $s=s_*$. The estimate is
needed only for $s$ built from the prime powers in the sum.
Under these assumptions the required mean square is $O_J(y\log y)$.

To prove this, group the terms of $S_p$ by the squarefree part of
$n$ after deleting primes dividing $M$. The weights in absolute
value of the resulting groups are bounded by their masses $a_g$,
formed with $b_n=1$ and without any signs. Quadratic reciprocity
makes the character of each term a fixed sign times $(p/g)$:
the signs at $2$, at primes dividing $M$, and in reciprocity are
constant on the progression. The product of the characters at two
groups $g,h$ is indexed by $g*h$, the squarefree part of $gh$.
The two exceptional contributions to the expanded mean square
are together at most $2\sum_g a_g^2$. Indeed for any fixed
squarefree $s$ the map $g\mapsto g*s$ is an involution, so
$\sum_g a_g a_{g*s}\le\sum_g a_g^2$ by Cauchy--Schwarz, extending
the masses by zero. The remaining contribution is at most
$Z^{-2J}(\sum_g a_g)^2$.

For completeness, Chebyshev's bound $\sum_{n\le t}\Lambda(n)\ll t$
and partial summation give $\sum_g a_g\ll y\log(2L)$.
The mass from all even prime powers is $O(\sqrt y)$, by summing
$\Lambda(m)\min(1,y/m^2)$ below $\sqrt y$ and on the successive
dyadic blocks above it. For each prime $l\le y$, the mass of its
powers is $O(\log(2y))$: powers below $y$ contribute at most
$\log y$, and the geometric tail contributes at most $2\log l$.
Hence $a_1\ll\sqrt y+Z^{1/5}\log(2y)\ll\sqrt y$.
The other groups have prime indices $l$. Those with $l\le y$
contribute $O(y\log y)$ to the sum of squares by
$\pi(y)\ll y/\log y$. For $l>y$ each mass is at most
$2y\log l/l$; on $2^j y<l\le2^{j+1}y$ their squares sum to
$O(2^{-j}y\log(2^{j+1}y))$ by the same prime count. This proves
the claimed mean square since $y\le Z^J$ and $\log L=400J\log Z$.

The first progression is $3\bmod 8$ in $(\sqrt U,U]$ with
$\log U=z=Z^{\varepsilon/5}$. For the second take
$\log U=z(\log Z)^2$ and
\[
 m=q\prod_{3\le l\le z,\ l\ {\rm prime}}l,\qquad M=16m .
\]
The conditions $d\equiv7\pmod {16}$ and $d\equiv-1\pmod m$
specify one reduced class by the Chinese remainder theorem. Uniformly
in the first prime $q$, the primorial bound
$\prod_{l\le z}l\le4^{\lfloor z\rfloor}$ and $q\le e^z$
give $\log M\le4z$ eventually. Since $q>e^{z/2}>L^2$
eventually, every prime divisor of $M$ up to $L$ is at most
$\max(2,z)\le Z^{1/5}$ for $0<\varepsilon<1/52$. Both candidate
intervals have lower endpoint exceeding $L^2$. For either choice
of $(M,U)$ and all $s\le L^2$ one has
$\log U/\log(Ms)\gg_J(\log Z)^2$ for large $Z$: in the
first progression $\log(Ms)=O_J(\log Z)$, and in the second
$\log(Ms)\le4z+800J\log Z$. In particular an error of size
$\exp(-c\log U/\log(Ms))+\exp(-c\sqrt{\log U})$ is
$o(Z^{-B})$ for every fixed $c,B>0$.

The role of a possible real zero in a progression estimate can be
read off before estimating its size. The reduced classes above a
fixed unit class $a\bmod M$ form a coset of
$H=\ker((\mathbb Z/Ms)^\times\to(\mathbb Z/M)^\times)$.
For squarefree odd $s>1$ coprime to $M$, the Jacobi symbol induces
a nontrivial character $J_s$ on $H$ by the Chinese remainder theorem.
For any real character $\chi$ modulo $Ms$, the sum of
$J_s(b)\chi(b)$ over the coset vanishes unless $\chi|_H=J_s|_H$,
by character orthogonality. Thus in a prime count with main term
$(\operatorname{Li}(U)-\chi(b)\operatorname{Li}(U^\beta))/\varphi(Ms)$
the constant term always cancels in the weighted sum. If the
exceptional character matches on $H$, it has mean zero on the
coset, so the unweighted sum of main terms is then exactly
$\operatorname{Li}(U)/\varphi(M)$. A relative error bound for
each class survives summation against $J_s$ with absolute error
at most that relative bound times the sum of the nonnegative main
terms. Finally a single primitive exceptional character can match
at most one such $s$: the part of its conductor supported away
from $M$ must equal $s$, since the local Legendre character is
nontrivial at every prime dividing $s$. To apply this calculation directly to prime counts weighted
by $\log p$, put $D=Ms$ and $Q=ML^2$. For
$\sqrt U\le t\le U$ one has $t\ge D^{12}$ eventually, uniformly
in $s$. Corollary~1.4 and Remark~1.5 of \cite{ThornerZaman} give
in each reduced class $b$ a main term
$(t-\chi(b)t^\beta/\beta)/\varphi(D)$ with relative error
$o(Z^{-B})$ for any fixed $B$. The second term is omitted in the
absence of an exceptional zero. Indeed $\log t/\log D\gg_J
(\log Z)^2$, and $(\log t)^{3/5}/(\log\log t)^{1/5}$
also dominates $(\log Z)^2$.

By the Landau--Page theorem (see \cite{IK}), among primitive real
characters of conductor at most $Q$ at most one has a real zero
above $1-c/\log Q$, for a sufficiently small absolute $c>0$.
An exceptional term with $\beta\le1-c/\log Q$ has
$t^{\beta-1}/\beta\ll\exp(-c\log t/\log Q)=o(Z^{-B})$
(the exceptional zeros in the progression formula exceed $1/2$).
If the character matches on $H$, the sum of the main terms
without the Jacobi weight is $t/\varphi(M)$, so this bounds the
relative contribution in the weighted sum. If it does not match,
orthogonality cancels its contribution exactly. By the conductor
calculation, only one index can therefore escape these bounds.
For every remaining index the absolute twisted log-weighted
cumulative count is $o(Z^{-B})$ times the untwisted cumulative
count on the same coset, uniformly for $\sqrt U\le t\le U$.

To remove the logarithmic weight, the lower endpoint must also be
controlled. At $t=U$ in the original progression, (1.2) and (1.7)
of \cite{ThornerZaman} give a main term at least
$\gg U M^{-3/2}$, using $1-\beta\gg M^{-1/2}$ when a zero
occurs. Here $M=U^{o(1)}$, so the unweighted count in
$(\sqrt U,U]$ exceeds $U^{9/10}$ eventually, even after division
by $\log U$ and removal of the first $\sqrt U$ integers.
Partial summation on this interval uses the upper cumulative
count divided by $\log U$ and the integral with positive kernel
$1/(t\log^2 t)$. Both are controlled by the preceding relative
bound; the lower boundary and primes dividing $s$ cost
$O(\sqrt U)$ since $s\le L^2<\sqrt U$. Relative to the interval
count this costs $O(U^{-2/5})$. Taking $B>2J$ proves the asserted
Jacobi average, hence the mean square for both prime sets.

The same selection controls the tails. At either stage put $H=U^2$.
Montgomery's density theorem for primitive Dirichlet characters
\cite[Chapter~12]{MontDensity} bounds the number of characters of
conductor at most $H$ having a zero in $\Re s\ge 99/100$,
$|\Im s|\le 2H$, by $O(H^{1/5})$. Indeed the density bound at
$\sigma=99/100$ is
$(2H^3)^{3(1-\sigma)/(2-\sigma)}(\log(2H^2))^{13}$,
which is smaller for large $H$. The first-stage conductors $q$
are distinct. At the second stage, for fixed $q$, each list of
conductors $d$ and $qd$ is injective and both are bounded by $H$.
Thus these exclusions cost at most $O(U^{2/5})$ candidates out
of at least $U^{9/10}$ at either stage. Intersecting with the
sets selected by the mean square still leaves a pair for which
all three primitive characters satisfy the stated zero exclusion,
each at its own stage height.

For one of these characters set $D_\chi(s)=-L'(s,\chi)/L(s,\chi)$
and $V=\lfloor L\rfloor+1/2$. Uniformly for $Z\le y\le X$ we have
\[
 y\left|D_\chi(1)-\sum_{n\le L}\frac{\Lambda(n)\chi(n)}n\right|=o(1).
\]
Indeed integrate $D_\chi(1+w)V^w/w$ on $\Re w=1/\log V$ up to
height $H$ in both directions, with the factor $1/(2\pi i)$.
Truncated Perron inversion gives the displayed finite sum with
error $O(V(\log V)^2/H)$. To see the uniformity, its kernel
error for $n$ is $O((V/n)^{1/\log V}/(H|\log(V/n)|))$.
On $[V/2,2V]$ the denominator $|\log(V/n)|\gg1/V$
by the half-integer cutoff; outside it this logarithm is bounded
away from zero. Summing with weights $\Lambda(n)/n$ gives
the asserted bound by $\Lambda(n)\le\log n$.
Move the segment to $\Re w=-1/200$. The pole at $w=0$ contributes
$D_\chi(1)$. Throughout the rectangle the local partial fraction
formula for $L'/L$ (\cite{IK}) gives $D_\chi(1+w)\ll\log(2H)$:
the $O(\log(2H))$ zeros within unit ordinate distance each have
horizontal distance at least $1/200$. The two horizontal integrals
are $O(\log(2H)/H)$ and the new vertical integral is
$O(V^{-1/200}\log^2(2H))$. Now $V\asymp X^{400}$,
$\log H\ll Z^{\varepsilon/5}(\log Z)^2$, and $H$ exceeds every
fixed power of $X$. Multiplication by $y\le X$ proves the claim.

Finally the identity $G_\chi(y)=-S_p(y;b)
-y(D_\chi(1)-\sum_{n\le L}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)/n)$ follows by
splitting at $n=y$, with $p,b$ chosen for the respective twist.
Taking $\tau=\varepsilon$ in the maximal estimate leaves half the
bound for the tail. Hence the same $q,d$ satisfy all three
inequalities $|G_\chi(y)|\le y^{1/2+\varepsilon}$ throughout
$[Z,Z^J]$ for every sufficiently large $Z$. This proves the
three individual estimates in the decomposition of $G$; the
upper bound for the signed term $G-R$ is a further requirement.

Two consequences apply to the comparison of the weights. First,
evaluating the definition of $G_\chi$ at $Z$ gives
$|B_\chi|\le Z^{-1/2+\varepsilon}+\sum_{n\le Z}\Lambda(n)/n
\le 4\log Z$ eventually, by the partial summation bound above.
Second, the exclusions can cover every primitive real character of
conductor dividing $2^a qd$, simultaneously for every $a$. Indeed
its component at $2$ factors through modulus $8$: every odd residue
congruent to $1\bmod8$ is a square modulo $2^a$, by successive
lifting of an odd square root (adding $2^{j-1}$ changes its square
by $2^j$ modulo $2^{j+1}$ for $j\ge3$). Each odd component is
trivial or the Legendre character. Thus one need only repeat the
exclusions above for the four real twists of modulus $8$. Their
conductors are still at most $H$ eventually, and the finite union
costs $O(U^{2/5})$. The primitive nonprincipal characters of
conductor dividing $8$ have a common real zero-free interval
$(1-c_0,1]$ by nonvanishing at one and finiteness. Decreasing
$c_0$ to at most $1/100$ gives this interval for all nonprincipal
real characters in the construction.

Here is the resulting estimate at a discarded weight. Write
$N$ for any primitive conductor in the field, $\delta_\chi=1$
for the principal character and zero otherwise, and
$\psi_\chi(t)=\sum_{n\le t}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)$. For
$\log N\ll x^{\varepsilon/5}(\log x)^2$ and
$y\ge\exp(x^{\varepsilon/3})/3$ one has, uniformly,
\[
 |G_\chi(y)|/y\ll \exp(-x^{\varepsilon/8}),
\]
using $G$ for the principal character. To see this put $v=\log t$,
$A=\log(2N)$ and $T=\exp(\sqrt v)$. The classical truncated
explicit formula and Dirichlet zero-free region \cite{IK} give
\[
 |\psi_\chi(t)-\delta_\chi t|/t
 \ll (A+v)^3\exp\{-c\min(\sqrt v,v/A)\}.
\]
For details one may take $t$ half-integral, then shift by at most
one at cost $O(\log(2t))$. The zeros with ordinates at most $T$
have real parts at most $1-c/(A+\sqrt v)$, including real zeros
by the preceding exclusion. The sum of $1/|\rho|$ there is
$O((A+v)^3)$ by the unit-interval zero count; for ordinates below
one the functional equation also gives $|\rho|\gg 1/A$.
The truncation error divided by $t$ is
$O((A+v)^3/T)$, using a height in $[T,T+1]$ away from zero
ordinates, as in the truncated formula. These estimates give the
display after decreasing $c$.

Differentiating $G_\chi(t)/t$ between integers gives
$(\psi_\chi(t)-\delta_\chi t)/t^2$, with continuity at integers.
Its limit at infinity is zero: for nonprincipal characters partial
summation and the displayed estimate identify
$\sum_n\Lambda(n)\chi(n)/n$ with $-B_\chi$ by taking the limit
from $\Re s>1$; the principal case was computed above.
Integrate from $y$ to infinity. With $v_0=\log y$,
both $\sqrt{v_0}$ and $v_0/A$ dominate
$x^{\varepsilon/8}$ by a positive power of $x$, since
$1/3-1/5=2/15>1/8$. Splitting the exponential bound into the
sum of $e^{-c\sqrt v}$ and $e^{-cv/A}$ shows after integration
that polynomial factors in $A,v_0$ are absorbed by half the
exponent. This gives the claimed error for all discarded arguments
in this range, simultaneously with the three selected estimates.

The elementary size comparison in that estimate can be made uniform
over the auxiliary primes. Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Let $2\le Z\le x$ and
suppose
\[
 \log q\le Z^{\varepsilon/5},\qquad
 \log d\le Z^{\varepsilon/5}(\log Z)^2 .
\]
Write $N=mqd$ and $t=\lfloor x^{401}\rfloor$. For each fixed $A,B\ge0$
and all sufficiently large $x$, uniformly over these choices,
\[
 (Nt)^A+B\exp(x^{4\varepsilon/5})
       \le \exp(x^\varepsilon/2).
\]
Indeed $m<8x^{2+8\varepsilon}$, whence
$\log(Nt)\le\log8+(403+8\varepsilon)\log x+
x^{\varepsilon/5}(1+(\log x)^2)=o(x^\varepsilon)$.
Also $\log(1+B)+x^{4\varepsilon/5}=o(x^\varepsilon)$.
Eventually each summand is at most $\exp(x^\varepsilon/4)$,
which gives the asserted inequality. Thus a polynomial estimate of
this shape for the weighted sum of cohomological lengths would give
the requisite numerical bound. Its uniformity in the primes and
the finiteness of the lengths are separate arithmetic assertions.

There is a further simplification in separating the odd primes in
the cohomological sum. For the trivial character the auxiliary prime
$p_*$, the least prime at least $7$ congruent to $3$ modulo $4$ and
coprime to the conductor, is $7$. The auxiliary quadratic field is
$\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-7})$ and has class number one: its discriminant is
$-7$, and Minkowski's ideal bound $(2/\pi)\sqrt7<2$ gives an integral
ideal of norm one in each class. The second auxiliary prime is the
least prime coprime to $2p_*$ and congruent to one modulo the odd part
of the character order. That order is one, so this prime is $3$.
Consequently the exceptional set in the odd-prime count, defined by
adjoining $3$ to the odd prime divisors of the product of the character
order, conductor, $p_*$, class number and second auxiliary prime,
is exactly $\{3,7\}$. Bounds proved away from this set therefore
require two exceptional odd-prime contributions; the prime $2$ is
treated separately.

For the two fixed auxiliary towers at these primes the unramified
pro-$\ell$ module is zero. Indeed the conductor of the trivial
character is one, so the fixed base at $\ell$ is
$\mathbb Q(\mu_\ell)$. For $\ell=3$ its Minkowski bound is
$(2/\pi)\sqrt3<2$. For $\ell=7$ the bound is
$(4/\pi)^3(6!/6^6)\sqrt{7^5}<5$. There are no ideals of norm
$2,3$ or $4$ in the latter field: the residue degrees of $2$ and $3$
are their orders modulo $7$, respectively $3$ and $6$. Thus both
base fields have class number one. At each step of degree $\ell$
in $\mathbb Q(\mu_{\ell^\infty})$ the unique prime above $\ell$
is totally ramified and all other primes are unramified. The preceding
inertia argument applies verbatim with a cyclic extension of degree
$\ell$: its conjugates generate an $\ell$-extension over the upper
layer, while every maximal subgroup of the resulting finite
$\ell$-group downstairs is normal of index $\ell$. An unramified
extension of degree $\ell$ upstairs would therefore give one downstairs.
Induction rules this out throughout the tower. The finite-quotient
descent above applies with values in $\mathbb F_\ell$, giving the
same vanishing at the infinite layer. Any nonzero abelian pro-$\ell$
quotient has a quotient of order $\ell$, proving the assertion.
Consequently the fixed towers at $3$ and $7$ contribute no exceptional
centres to the square-window selection. This concerns the fixed
cyclotomic towers; the twisted groups over fields depending on $q,d$
enter the separate character count below.

The same field description removes one branch of the character
count altogether. Write $j$ for the twist index, $1\le j<t$, and
suppose $q,d>2t+1$. Every auxiliary character $\eta$ factors through
$\mathrm{Gal}(E/\mathbb Q)$, of order $2^{e+2}$. Its order is
therefore a power of two. In particular there is no nonprincipal
character of odd order.

More precisely, consider an odd prime $\ell$ with either
$\ell\in\{3,7\}$ or $\ell-1\mid 2j$, taking $q,d>7$ as well.
In the divisibility case $\ell\le2j+1<q,d$; in the exceptional case
$\ell\le7<q,d$. Thus $\ell\nmid N$. Passing from $\eta$ to its
prime-to-$\ell$ order part leaves it unchanged, since its order is
a power of two. Removing its local component at $\ell$ also leaves it
unchanged, since $\ell\nmid N$. Hence the odd-order condition on this
modified character singles out the principal character exactly.
The parity condition $\eta(-1)=(-1)^j$ then forces $j$ even.
At $\ell=3,7$ the principal character in fact contributes zero
for every positive twist $j$. To see this, restrict the class over
$\mathbb Q$ to $K=\mathbb Q(\mu_\ell)$. Restriction is injective:
corestriction followed after restriction multiplies by $\ell-1$,
a unit on the $\ell$-primary coefficients. Over $K$ the coefficient
module is $(\mathbb Q_\ell/\mathbb Z_\ell)(-j)$, or a finite direct
sum thereof after extending the coefficient ring. The cyclotomic
quotient is procyclic with generator of infinite order, so the
restriction argument above injects the local kernel into characters
of $G_{K(\mu_{\ell^\infty})}$. Their inertia restrictions vanish:
away from $\ell$ the original class is unramified or locally trivial,
and at $\ell$ it is locally trivial. Each restricted character has
finite $\ell$-primary image by compactness. A nonzero one would
give an everywhere unramified quotient of order $\ell$, already
excluded. Extra local vanishing at primes dividing $N$ preserves
this conclusion. Thus the principal term vanishes independently of
the auxiliary level and of the size of the twist.
For $j\ge2$ the remaining nonprincipal characters in these
ranges all satisfy the same even-order condition. The uniform
estimate required for them is polynomial: with $v_{\ell,j,\eta}$
the normalized length multiplied by $\log\ell$, it suffices that
$v_{\ell,j,\eta}\le(\ell Nj)^C$ for a fixed $C\ge0$.
Indeed $\ell\le\max(7,2t+1)$ in this part of the sum. There are
fewer than $t$ twists, at most $[E:\mathbb Q]$ characters for
each pair $(\ell,j)$, and at most $\max(7,2t+1)$ choices of prime.
Since $[E:\mathbb Q]\le N$, their total is at most
\[
 Nt\max(7,2t+1)\bigl(Nt\max(7,2t+1)\bigr)^C .
\]
This polynomial fits the exponential allowance above. The missing
estimate is the individual bound with a common exponent, including
for twists up to $t-1$. Primes outside $\{3,7\}$ with
$\ell-1\nmid2j$ are counted separately.

For Case II we can evaluate the auxiliary conductor loss uniformly
on the chosen window. After removing the component at two, each
character of the field has order at most two, since it then factors
through the two quadratic sign coordinates. Its prime-to-$\ell$
part is the character itself. Hence the modulus defining the
auxiliary residue degree is the least common multiple of four and
a divisor of two, namely four. Set $f=\operatorname{ord}_4(\ell)$
and $n_0=v_2(\ell^f-1)$. For $\ell\equiv1\pmod4$ we have
$2^{n_0}\le\ell-1$. Otherwise $f=2$ and
$2^{n_0}=2\cdot 2^{v_2(\ell+1)}\le2(\ell+1)$.
Writing $2^b$ for the conductor of the component at two, the
auxiliary conductor $H$ in Case II for the modulus-one problem
is the least power of two at least
\[
 \max\{\ell N4^{n_0},\ 2^{b+6}\ell^3\}.
\]
Since $2^b\mid N$ and $\ell\ge3$, this gives $H\le128N\ell^3$.
For the Frobenius comparison take the level $M=\ell HN$ and
the common character exponent $k=2(\ell-1)H$. Indeed the residual
cyclotomic character has order dividing $\ell-1$, the auxiliary
character of conductor $H$ has order dividing $\varphi(H)$, and
the original character has order dividing $2\varphi(2^b)$ when
$b\ge1$ (and dividing two for $b=0$). This follows by separating
its component at two from the two quadratic sign coordinates.
All three characters have conductor dividing $M$. Let $p$ be the
least prime at least five prime to $M$; any prime divisor of
$6\ell N+1$ qualifies, since $H$ is a power of two.

Only a polynomial Frobenius count is required. More precisely,
suppose the trace comparison below bounds the weighted length by
$2\log|D_p|$, where the nonzero integer $D_p$ satisfies
$\log|D_p|\le C\varphi(k)[M(j+1)]^A\log p$ with absolute
$A,C$. Then $k\le256N\ell^4$, $M\le128N^2\ell^4$, and $j+1\le2j$,
so the resulting bound is
\[
 v_{\ell,j,\eta}\le 512C\,256^A
 N^{2A+1}\ell^{4A+4}j^A\log(6\ell N+1).
\]
This proves the required growth for any fixed counting exponent $A$.
It applies also when the Frobenius count is carried out after
adjoining roots of unity to the coefficient field. Indeed let
$O'/O$ be the resulting finite extension of valuation rings.
In an $O$-basis of $O'$, continuous cochains with the extended
discrete coefficients are a finite direct sum of the original
cochain complexes. Restriction and inertia conditions act
coordinatewise, so their cohomological kernel extends by
tensoring with $O'$. Finite length therefore descends, and for
a finite kernel its length is multiplied by the ramification
index $e(O'/O)$: each summand $O/\pi^a$ becomes
$O'/\pi^aO'$. Division by the absolute ramification index gives
the same normalized length in either field. The trace comparison
and polynomial count for the arithmetic representations are still
needed to obtain the displayed inequality unconditionally.

The length comparison in this individual estimate has an elementary
algebraic part. Let $A$ be a graded algebra over a discrete valuation
ring $O$ with finite residue field and uniformizer $\pi$, let $x:A\to O$ be an augmentation
vanishing in nonzero degrees, and put $I=\ker x$. In degree $-1$
let $Q$ be $(I/I^2)_{-1}$ modulo its torsion and the images of
the defining equations of that degree. Suppose $Q$
has finite length. A finite $O$-module $S$ injecting additively
into the weight $-1$ derivations at $x$ modulo $\pi^k$ has length
at most that of $Q$, provided the derivations have integral lifts
of the same weight and vanish modulo $\pi^k$ on the equations.
Indeed the product rule kills $I^2$ and integral lifts kill torsion.
The reductions therefore factor through $Q$ and are determined by
their restrictions there, since they vanish in every other weight
and on $O$. Thus $S$ injects into
$\operatorname{Hom}_O(Q,O/\pi^k)$. For finite residue field,
cardinalities give the claimed length inequality: each summand
$O/\pi^b$ of $Q$ contributes only $\min(b,k)$ to the dual length.
The numerical factor in comparing this length with trace valuations
can be computed on the graded pieces. Write $r\ge1$ for the rank of
the torsion-free weight $-1$ cotangent lattice. Suppose its $r+1$
equations span it over the fraction field. Smith reduction of their
linear parts gives $r$ diagonal entries $\pi^{c_i}$ and a zero
column, with $\operatorname{length}_O Q=\sum_i c_i$. The change
of columns is invertible over $O$, so it preserves the ideal of
equations before any of them is squared.

In the smooth chart after the weight-zero equations have been cut,
take respectively $r,s,r'+1,s'+1$ variables of weights $-1,-2,1,2$.
Count monomials by the absolute negative weight $u$ and the positive
weight $v$. Their generating series is
\[
 H(X,Y)=\frac{1}{(1-X)^r(1-X^2)^s(1-Y)^{r'+1}(1-Y^2)^{s'+1}}.
\]
The backward differences with steps $(2,0),(0,2),(4,2)$ and
orders $r-1+s,r'+s',1$ multiply this series by
$(1-X^2)^{r-1+s}(1-Y^2)^{r'+s'}(1-X^4Y^2)$.
The resulting coefficient equals $2^{r-1+r'}$ whenever
$u\ge r+3$ and $v\ge r'$. Indeed, put $A=(1+X)^{r-1}(1+Y)^{r'}$.
Writing $1-X^4Y^2=(1-Y^2)+Y^2(1-X^4)$ splits the product into
$A/((1-X)(1-Y))$ and a series of $X$-degree at most $r+2$.
The first coefficient in this range is $A(1,1)$. In particular
the formula applies to $(u,v)=(2d_0+4d_2,2d_1+2d_2)$ for large
multidegrees. A squared Smith entry has valuation $2c_i$, so
weighting this coefficient by those valuations gives
$2^{r+r'}\operatorname{length}_O Q$.

There is a corresponding upper multiplicity calculation. At a
characteristic-zero point suppose that every weight piece modulo
the unsquared equations has local length at most one. Squaring
the $r+1$ equations of weight $-1$ and the $r'$ equations of weight
$1$ raises this bound to at most $2^{r+1+r'}$. In fact for a
homogeneous equation $f$ the kernel of $B/(f^2)\to B/(f)$ is
an image of $B/(f)$ by multiplication by $f$, with the weight
shifted by $\deg f$. This works modulo all the other equations and
survives localization in weight zero. Induct on the equations,
using the bound in every weight.

For a graded piece $V$ finite over $O$ and a degree-zero operator
$a$ invertible on its generic fibre, put
$E_a(V)=\operatorname{length}(V/aV)-\operatorname{length}(\ker a)$.
On the torsion submodule kernel and cokernel have equal length.
The snake lemma and Smith form on the free quotient therefore give
$E_a(V)=v_\pi(\det a_{\rm gen})$. Over an algebraic closure,
decomposing the generic fibre into local factors of its commutative
operator algebra evaluates this as $\sum_\xi m_\xi v_\pi(\xi(a))$:
on a factor of length $m_\xi$ the operator is its scalar plus a
nilpotent. Thus if these pieces have the preceding upper multiplicity
bound and their support is covered by the chosen points, then
$E_a(V)\le 2^{r+1+r'}\sum_\xi v_\pi(\xi(a))$, since integrality
gives nonnegative valuations. To conclude the factor-two inequality
one must realize the lower bound
$2^{r+r'}\operatorname{length}_O Q\le E_a(V)$ on such a piece
for the trace difference $a$. The coefficient computation alone
does not compare the specialized modules.

One comparison between the two orders of specialization is general.
Let $R$ be a Noetherian standard multigraded algebra over a
Noetherian local ring and $M$ a finite graded module. Take a list
$\mathbf f$ of homogeneous equations of nonnegative multidegrees
and $a$ of degree zero acting injectively on $M$. Suppose the large
multidegree pieces of $M/(a,\mathbf f)M$ have finite length over
the base. Writing $\chi_d$ for the alternating sum of Koszul
homology lengths in multidegree $d$, one has for all large $d$
\[
 \chi_d(\mathbf f;M/aM)\le E_a((M/\mathbf f M)_d).
\]
Here all lengths use the same base. To see this, first note that the
Euler characteristic for any such list is eventually nonnegative,
also without injectivity. Koszul homology is finite and supported on
the intersection of the module support with the common zero locus.
The finite-length condition passes to homologies and subquotients:
they are killed by the equations in homology, and finite graded
modules supported on that intersection admit a filtration by shifted
prime quotients there. For the final quotient $N$, $R/\operatorname{Ann}N$
embeds in a finite sum of shifts of $N$ by evaluation on homogeneous
generators; each supporting prime quotient is an image of this ring. Thus finite length holds in all sufficiently large degrees.

Use additivity of $\chi_d$ from the long exact homology sequence
to reduce positivity to shifted homogeneous prime quotients, and
induct on the list length. On a domain a nonzero first equation
is regular, so delete it after quotienting. If the first equation
vanishes, the Euler characteristic is the difference of the two
shifted tail characteristics. A nonzero element of the same degree
on the domain gives that same difference on its cokernel by
additivity, to which induction applies. If the domain has no element
of this degree, its large pieces vanish by standard generation.
The empty list gives a length. Finally, order the Koszul list with
$a$ first to get the left side of the displayed inequality.
Permuting the list leaves homology unchanged. Removing the $f_i$
one by one replaces the characteristic by that of the quotient
minus that of the shifted annihilator, by the two-row Koszul
homology sequence. The latter characteristic is eventually
nonnegative. This gives the stated upper bound.

Here is the quotient that transfers the monomial calculation to the
lower estimate. Let $\phi$ be a weight-preserving formal chart over
$K=\operatorname{Frac}O$, centered at $x$, with the weights above.
Assume that homogeneous elements of $A$ map onto the linear
coordinates after extending scalars to $K$. Choose their images
as formal parameters: the invertible matrix of linear parts gives
the inverse substitution successively in each total degree. The
new parameters are still homogeneous. If a smooth chart initially
has weight-zero variables, equations of weight zero with independent
linear parts spanning those variables can be taken as the variables
themselves by the same inverse substitution; setting them to zero
gives the chart used here. Write $F_*$ for the last weight $-1$
equation, with zero linear part. If $\phi(a)=0$, replace $a$ by
$a+F_i g$ where $F_i$ has nonzero linear part and $g$ lifts a
positive weight-one parameter. The chart is a domain, so the new
image is nonzero. Neither $x(a)$ nor the values at solutions of
the unsquared equations change. Multiplication by this new image
is injective on the chart image algebra, including after adjoining
the formal degree variables. Over $A_0$ form
the multigraded algebra with piece of degree $d=(i,j,k)$ given by
$N_0^iN_1^jN_2^k$, tagging pieces by formal degree variables, where
\[
 N_0=A_{-2},\qquad N_1=A_2,\qquad
 N_2=A_0F_*^2+N_0^2N_1.
\]
Take as sections in degrees $e_0,e_1,e_2$, respectively, the first
$r$ squared $F_i$ and the $s$ weight $-2$ equations; the $r'$
squared weight-one and $s'$ weight-two equations; and $F_*^2$.
Assume $N_0,N_1$ are finite over $A_0$. In the chart retain only
coefficients with negative weight total $u=2i+4k$, counting
negative weights in absolute value. Every term has negative total
at least $u$: this holds in each generating piece, since a term
of weight $-1$ with negative total one must be a linear parameter
of weight $-1$. Thus $F_*$ has negative total at least two.
At the retained degree the positive total is $v=2j+2k$ by homogeneity.
These projections multiply, since no term below the retained
degree can enter a product. Their common graded image $L$ is a
quotient of the chart image of the multigraded algebra. A
weight-zero coefficient acts there by its constant $x(b)$, so
the quotient factors through specialization by any trace difference
$a\in A_0$ with $x(a)=0$.

Each $L_d$ is a finite torsion-free $O$-module of rank
$[X^uY^v]H$. Indeed it lies in the $K$-space of those monomials
and is finite since $A_0$ acts through $O$. Each monomial occurs
up to a nonzero scalar: group its negative parameters into
$i+2k$ packets of weight two, using a single weight-two
parameter or two of weight one, and its positive parameters
into $j+k$ such packets. Their lifts belong to $N_0$ and $N_1$
respectively; assemble $k$ triples in $N_0^2N_1\subset N_2$.
Clear scalar denominators in the lifts. This gives the entire
generic rank, hence the dimension of $L_d/\pi L_d$ as well.
Furthermore the section for $F_i^2$ acts on $L$ as
$\pi^{2c_i}$ times the section for $y_i^2$, where $y_i$ lifts
the corresponding Smith basis vector: $F_i-\pi^{c_i}y_i$ has
zero linear part and its product with $F_i+\pi^{c_i}y_i$
has negative total at least three.

To see the effect on the Euler characteristic, remove the scalar
powers from these $r$ sections one at a time. For a section $b$
of degree $e$, the multiplication complex for $\pi^h b$ is the
complex for the composite of $b:L(-e)\to L$ followed by
$\pi^h:L\to L$. The mapping-cone triangle gives the sum of their
Euler characteristics after tensoring with the Koszul complex
of the remaining sections. Repeating with $\pi$ and using its
injectivity on $L$ gives $h$ copies of the remaining characteristic
on $L/\pi L$. This is the backward difference of its Hilbert
function in the remaining degrees, by the alternating sum of the
chain lengths. Each removal therefore adds $2c_i$ times the
coefficient computed above. The final characteristic is nonnegative.
For large degrees, additivity and positivity on the kernel of the
surjection onto $L$ give the same lower bound on the chart image
modulo $a$. These comparisons assume the finite-length condition
above for the equations and $a$ (replacing a product by one of its
factors only shrinks the common zero locus). Applying this construction
to the arithmetic deformation requires that chart and finiteness
condition. On the opposite specialization one can compare the two
multiplicities directly wherever a product $fg$, with $f\in A_{-2}$
and $g\in A_2$, is nonzero at the point. Localize the weight-zero
ring there, writing $B$ for the local ring and $A'$ for the localized
graded algebra. Both $f$ and $g$ are units in $A'$, since $fg$ is
a unit in $B$. In particular $A'_{2h}=Bg^h$ for every integer $h$:
division by $g^h$ takes that piece into $B$. The localized $N_0,N_1,N_2$
are the full pieces of weights $-2,2,-2$, respectively, since they
contain the units $f,g,f^2g$. Thus in degree $d=(i,j,k)$ the tagged
piece identifies with $A'_w$ for $w=2(j-i-k)$.

Let $I$ be the homogeneous ideal of the squared and unsquared sections
listed above together with the weight-zero equations. If $i,j,k\ge1$,
each section of degree $e_m$ can be multiplied by the entire weight
piece corresponding to $d-e_m$. Hence in degree $d$ its multiples
give precisely its contribution to $I_w$. The same holds for each
weight-zero equation with multiplier in degree $d$. It follows that
$(A'/I A')_w$ surjects onto the localized tagged piece after imposing
these equations and any further chart relations. Its length therefore
bounds the latter length from above. To apply the bound
$2^{r+1+r'}$ one still needs arithmetic control of the support:
each contributing point must admit such a product and satisfy the
length-one estimate for the unsquared equations. For the latter
estimate, write $C$ for the localized graded quotient by the
unsquared equations. It suffices that $C_0$ be Artinian local and
that $C$ have no nonzero homogeneous nilpotents. Indeed, $C_0$ is
then a field (the zero ring gives only zero pieces). For any nonzero
$h\in C_w$, the element $h^2g^{-w}$ has weight zero and is nonzero.
It is therefore invertible, which makes $h$ a unit. Division by
$h$ identifies $C_w$ with $C_0$, of length one over $B$. This
argument covers odd weights as well, which arise in the successive
weight shifts when the equations are squared.

For a scalar Galois action through $\alpha:G\to O^\times$ on
$K/O$, where $K=\operatorname{Frac}(O)$, the first step in constructing
the injection is explicit. Suppose $\alpha(g_0)-1$ is a unit.
Each continuous cohomology class has a unique cocycle $z$ with
$z(g_0)=0$: subtract the coboundary of
$(\alpha(g_0)-1)^{-1}z(g_0)$ from any representative. This choice
is additive and $O$-linear. If a subgroup of classes is killed by
$\pi^k$, then $\pi^k z$ represents zero and is still normalized,
hence vanishes identically. Its values before multiplication
therefore lie in $\pi^{-k}O/O\simeq O/\pi^k$. This gives an
injection into finite-coefficient cocycles whenever the residual
character is nontrivial and the group has finite length. Both hypotheses hold for the nonprincipal characters in the
even-order branch above. Indeed $\ell\nmid N$, so the Chinese
remainder theorem gives
$\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb Q(\mu_{N\ell})/\mathbb Q)
\simeq (\mathbb Z/N)^\times\times(\mathbb Z/\ell)^\times$.
Choose an element in the first factor on which $\eta$ is nontrivial
and take the identity in the second. For any Galois lift $g_0$
the cyclotomic character is one modulo $\ell$, whereas
$\eta(g_0)$ remains nontrivial in the residue field: the kernel of
reduction on units is pro-$\ell$ and $\eta$ has $2$-power order.
Thus $\alpha=\eta^{-1}\chi_\ell^{-j}$ satisfies the unit condition
for every $j$ with this same $g_0$.

There is a uniform bound for the number of cyclic summands of this
kernel $S_j$, even when the twist is large. Put $L=\mathbb Q(\eta,\mu_\ell)$,
$n=[L:\mathbb Q]$, and $k=O/\pi$. Then $n\mid
\operatorname{ord}(\eta)(\ell-1)$ is prime to $\ell$. Restriction of
$S_j[\pi]$ injects into $\operatorname{Hom}(G_{L,\Sigma},k)$, where
$\Sigma$ consists of the places above $\ell$. Indeed normalize the
cocycle as above; it then takes values in $M[\pi]\simeq k$ for
$M=(K/O)(\eta^{-1}\chi_\ell^{-j})$. On $G_L$ the residual action
is trivial, so the restriction is a homomorphism. On inertia away
from $\ell$ the full action over $L$ is trivial and the restricted
class is unramified, hence the cocycle vanishes there. A zero
restriction makes the cocycle descend to the group of order $n$,
whose first cohomology over $k$ vanishes by averaging. This proves
injectivity on classes.

Choose an identification $\mu_\ell\simeq\mathbb F_\ell$ over $L$.
Kummer theory identifies the $\mathbb F_\ell$-valued homomorphisms
with classes of elements having valuations divisible by $\ell$
outside $\Sigma$, modulo $\ell$th powers. Taking the ideal outside
$\Sigma$ and dividing its exponents by $\ell$ maps these classes
into $\mathrm{Cl}(\mathcal O_{L,\Sigma})[\ell]$, with kernel the
$\Sigma$-units modulo $\ell$th powers. The unit theorem and elementary
divisors therefore give
\[
 \dim_k S_j[\pi]\le n+|\Sigma|+\log_\ell |\mathrm{Cl}(L)|
 \ll n\log(2\ell N),
\]
using the class number bound below and
$|\operatorname{disc}L|\le(\ell N)^n$. Extension from
$\mathbb F_\ell$ to $k$ preserves the dimension. Finiteness of $S_j$
itself follows by restriction to $L$ and the discriminant estimate
below. If $a_j$ is the least integer with $\pi^{a_j}S_j=0$, the
elementary divisors bound its length between $a_j$ and
$a_j\dim_k S_j[\pi]$. Hence it suffices to bound $a_j\log\ell/e_O$
polynomially, with $e_O$ the ramification index.

A single integer can bound the congruences of Frobenius traces
in this estimate. Fix a good prime $p\ge5$, $j\ge2$, and let
$B_p=p^{j+1}z_1+p^jz_2+pz_3+1$, with $z_i^k=1$.
This is the trace of the diagonal system with exponents $(j+1,j,1,0)$
and these finite character values. Suppose $P\in\mathbb Z[X]$ is monic
of degree $d$, and every complex root $v$ satisfies
$|v|\le(3\sqrt p+1)p^{(j+1)/2}$. Then
$D_p=|\mathrm N_{\mathbb Q(\mu_k)/\mathbb Q}P(B_p)|$ is a positive
integer with $\log D_p\le 2\varphi(k)d(j+1)\log p$. Indeed, writing
$y=p^j$, the trace bound is at most $7y/2$, since
$\sqrt p\,p^{(j+1)/2}\le y$ and $p^{(j+1)/2}\le y/2$.
Thus $|v-(yz_2+pz_3+1)|\le 9y/2+p+1<py$,
using $p+1<y/2$. For every conjugate of the $z_i$ this proves
$0<|v-B_p|<2py\le p^{2(j+1)}$. Multiply over roots and embeddings.

At any $\ell$-adic embedding, normalize $v_\ell(\ell)=1$.
For any submultiset of roots $v_i$ of $P$,
$\sum_i v_\ell(B_p-v_i)\log\ell\le\log D_p$:
all omitted factors of the norm are integral, and
$\ell^{v_\ell(D_p)}\le D_p$. Consequently a comparison bounding
the normalized kernel length by twice this sum of valuations gives
a weighted length at most $4\varphi(k)d(j+1)\log p$.
In particular, a Galois-stable multiset of $d$ integral eigenvalue
systems supplies $P=\prod_s(X-\operatorname{tr}_s)$: its coefficients
are rational algebraic integers. Distinct systems of the same trace
supply separate factors. To check the trace bound it suffices that
the four eigenvalues have moduli at most $\sqrt p\,c$ and product of
modulus $c^4$, where $c=p^{(j+1)/2}$. At least one then has modulus
at most $c$, and summing the four moduli gives the required bound.
In the modulus-one application a single imaginary quadratic field
suffices for the count at each coefficient prime $\ell$. The level
condition is unramifiedness outside $2\ell Np_1$, where $N=mqd$,
$m$ is a power of two and $p_1$ is either one or the second auxiliary
prime, three. For sufficiently large windows $q,d>19$. Choose
$r=11$ if $\ell\ne11$ and $r=19$ otherwise, and put
$K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-r})$. Every representation being counted is
unramified at $r$, whereas $K$ has discriminant $-r$.
Write $H=G_K$ and $I_r$ for inertia at a prolongation of $r$.
Since inertia maps onto $\operatorname{Gal}(K/\mathbb Q)$, one
has $G_{\mathbb Q}=I_r H$. Thus each representation has exactly
the same image on $H$ as on $G_{\mathbb Q}$. In particular its
restriction stays irreducible. Even the intertwining spaces are
unchanged: a map intertwining the restrictions intertwines every
$ih$, $i\in I_r$, $h\in H$, since both actions of $i$ are trivial.

We can also choose the good prime to split in this same field.
Put $C=6\ell M$ and take any prime divisor $p$ of $1+rC^2$.
Then $p\ge5$, $p\nmid r\ell M$, and $-r\equiv (C^{-1})^2\pmod p$.
Hence $p$ splits and $p\le1+19(6\ell M)^2$. At a place of $K$
above $p$ the local field is $\mathbb Q_p$, so the Frobenius
multisets of a representation and its restriction agree exactly.
Consequently a Galois-stable list classifying these restrictions
by their full systems, with integral Frobenius eigenvalues at this
place, supplies the required polynomial with one factor per label.
Two different labels may give the same trace. Conversely, once
each restriction has a cuspidal label matching its Frobenius
polynomials outside a finite set, one label cannot account for two
inequivalent representations. Indeed the traces of two such
restrictions agree on all of $H$ by continuity and Chebotarev
density. Over the algebraically closed coefficient field let $A$
be the linear span of the simultaneous matrices
$(\rho(h),\rho'(h))$ in the product of the two matrix algebras.
It is a unital algebra. By irreducibility both projections are
surjective by Burnside's theorem. Their kernels are maximal
two-sided ideals. If different, their sum is $A$, so the Chinese
remainder theorem gives an element projecting to $(I,0)$, contrary
to trace equality on $A$. If equal, the projections identify the
two matrix algebras by an automorphism. It is inner: for the images
$F_{ab}$ of the matrix units choose nonzero $v$ in the image of
$F_{11}$; the four vectors $F_{i1}v$ are a basis on which $F_{ab}$
act as the original matrix units. This gives the intertwiner on
$H$, hence on $G_{\mathbb Q}$ by the inertia argument. Only two
fixed fields occur as $\ell$ varies. Matching at the chosen split
prime is still needed to evaluate $P(B_p)$ there.

More generally five independent quadratic characters suffice
when an unramified auxiliary place is unavailable. Reducibility
on an index-two subgroup gives $V=W\oplus gW$ for a simple
summand $W$ and $g$ outside the subgroup. The opposite signs on
these summands give a self-twist. The self-twists form a group of
order at most $\dim\operatorname{End}(V)=16$ by independence of
their character spaces; five independent signs generate order $32$.

Both selected fields have class number one: Minkowski's bound
$(2/\pi)\sqrt r$ is less than three and two is inert for both.
Hence every full integral lattice is free by Steinitz classification.
At integral level the finite adelic lattice classes thus have one
orbit with stabilizer $\mathrm{GL}_4(\mathcal O_K)$. At level
three the stabilizers are torsion-free by the reduction argument
below. They act freely after subdivision on the contractible nerve
constructed below. There are finitely many cell orbits: every
simplex has a common frame, and the number of vertices with that
frame is uniformly bounded, as is the number of frame orbits.
There are finitely many level-three components by finite index.
This gives a fixed finite cell model at that level.
A finite cellular complex with $b$ cells in the
degrees counted gives, on a cover of degree $I$, total cohomology
dimension at most $bI\dim\Xi$ for any coefficient system $\Xi$:
lift the cells and bound cohomology by the cochains. For principal
level $3M$ the relative index inside integral level is at most
$|\mathcal O_K/(3M)|^{16}=(3M)^{32}$ by reduction of matrices.
For an algebraic coefficient of $\mathrm{GL}_4$ with highest weight
entries bounded in magnitude by $H$, Weyl's dimension product has
six factors, each at most $2H+1$. Taking one factor at each of
the two embeddings gives $\dim\Xi\le(2H+1)^{12}$. It remains to identify which classes can be counted.

Here is the injection for cohomological cuspidal summands. Work
modulo the split real centre, with matching central characters.
Suppose each summand has a nonzero vector at the prescribed finite
level and nonzero relative Lie algebra cohomology with coefficient
$\Xi$ at infinity. In each degree the relative cochains of the
archimedean summand are finite dimensional: they are maps from a
finite exterior power to the tensor product with $\Xi$, equivariant
under the maximal compact group, and admissibility bounds each
required compact type. In a degree of nonzero cohomology choose
a nonzero cocycle perpendicular to the coboundaries. Tensoring with
the level vector gives a harmonic differential form on the quotient.
Indeed in the invariant frame exterior differentiation is the relative
differential (differentiate coefficients and subtract the bracket
terms). With the invariant metric on the associated bundle its
formal adjoint likewise preserves each unitary cuspidal summand:
infinitesimal right translations have adjoints their negatives,
and the exterior and coefficient matrices act on the finite tensor
factors. Thus perpendicularity in the relative complex gives
coclosedness on the quotient. Inequivalent cuspidal summands are
orthogonal in the unitary realization, hence give linearly
independent forms in each degree.

To see when this independence passes to ordinary cohomology, use the
compactification by corners of the locally symmetric quotient. For a
harmonic combination $\omega$ let $\omega^\vee$ be its conjugate
Hodge dual, with values in the dual flat bundle. Coclosedness gives
$d\omega^\vee=0$. The scalar cuspidal coefficients and their right derivatives decay
faster than any power in a cusp. Here is the estimate at fixed
level. In a Siegel chart let $Y$ be the largest expanding simple
root of the diagonal part $a$, enlarged to at least one. Modulo
the split centre, $g$ and $g^{-1}$ have norms polynomial in $Y$
on this chart. Every fixed right derivative $Df$ of a smooth
cuspidal vector satisfies $|Df(g)|\le C_DY^B$ with the same $B$.
Indeed apply local Sobolev on a fixed ball about the identity to
$u\mapsto Df(gu)$. The ball translated by $g$ projects with
multiplicity at most the number of lattice elements in $gUU^{-1}g^{-1}$
for its closure $U$. Their matrix entries are polynomially bounded
in $Y$ and lie in a fixed fractional ideal, giving a polynomial
bound independent of $D$. Each squared Sobolev integral on the ball
is bounded by this multiplicity times a global squared $L^2$ norm
of a derivative; all are finite for smooth vectors of the unitary
representation.

Take the maximal parabolic omitting a simple root attaining $Y>1$.
Its unipotent radical for $\mathrm{GL}_4$ is an additive rectangular
matrix group. On its compact lattice quotient the constant term is
zero by cuspidality, also for right derivatives. At fixed finite
component this is the ordinary torus average: the additive finite
adeles are the sum of rational points and the compact open lattice
fixing that component, by the Chinese remainder theorem. In upper
triangular coordinates the bounded unipotent part normalizes the
radical, and conjugation by $a^{-1}$ contracts each radical root
direction by $O(Y^{-1})$ because its root contains the omitted
simple root and every simple root is bounded below in the chart.
Thus an order $h$ derivative in the torus variable costs
$O_h(Y^{-h})$ times right derivatives of order $h$; along a fixed
direction this follows by conjugating its one-parameter subgroup,
and the radical directions commute. For the mean-zero function on
this fixed torus, absolute Fourier summation bounds its supremum by
$C_m\|\Delta^m f\|_\infty$ for $2m$ greater than the real torus
dimension, since the nonzero dual-lattice frequencies have summable
inverse $2m$th powers. This gives $O_{D,m}(Y^{B-2m})$ for $Df$.
There are finitely many chart types, and no uniformity of $C_{D,m}$
in the level is needed for independence. Both forms consequently
extend smoothly by zero at the faces. In split-torus coordinates
$y_i=\exp(-\alpha_i(H))$ normal to a corner, changing between
invariant and coordinate frames costs at most fixed powers of
$\prod_i y_i^{-1}$: the unipotent root coordinates scale by torus
characters, $d\log y_i=dy_i/y_i$, and the algebraic coefficient
and its dual have finitely many weights. On a compact part of a
boundary chart the remaining frame coefficients and their derivatives
are bounded. Each fixed ordinary derivative thus tends to zero by
rapid decay, giving the asserted smooth extensions.
If $[\omega]=0$ in the interior, its extended class is zero on
the compactification since pushing the boundary collars inward
is a homotopy equivalence and the flat bundle extends by that
equivalence. Choose a smooth primitive there in positive degree.
Stokes' formula paired with the closed $\omega^\vee$ gives
$\int\omega\wedge\omega^\vee=0$ since the boundary pullback
of $\omega^\vee$ vanishes. This integral is $\|\omega\|_2^2$;
in degree zero exactness already means zero. Thus these cuspidal
summands contribute independent classes and their number is at most
$b(3M)^{32}(2H+1)^{12}$. Application to the restrictions requires
their cuspidal realization with these level, cohomology and
Frobenius properties. The comparison of the kernel length with trace
valuations is also required.

At finitely many chosen local subgroups where these
classes vanish, write $z(g)=(\alpha(g)-1)b_v$ in $K/O$.
The group of classes is finite and there are finitely many $v$,
so a single $k'$ kills all chosen $b_v$ as well as every value of
every $z$. Using $\pi^{-k'}O/O\simeq O/\pi^{k'}$ for both gives
the very same coboundary identities over the finite coefficient ring.

Write $B=O/\pi^{k'}$, $\Psi=\eta\chi_\ell^j$ and
$\alpha=\Psi^{-1}$. The diagonal representation used here has entries
$(d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3)=(\beta\Psi,\Psi,\beta,1)$ for an auxiliary
character $\beta$. Thus $d_2/d_0=d_3/d_1=\alpha$ independently
of $\beta$. Put $U=E_{20}+E_{31}$ (indices start
at zero). Over $B[\delta]/(\delta^2)$ the formula
\[
 r_z(g)=(1+\delta z(g)U)r_0(g)
\]
is multiplicative: conjugation by $r_0(g)$ multiplies $U$ by
$\alpha(g)$, and the product rule is precisely the cocycle identity.
The coefficient of $\delta$ in entry $(2,0)$ is $z(g)d_0(g)$,
so this matrix construction retains the entire cocycle. On the
chosen local subgroup it is conjugation of $r_0$ by
$1-\delta b_v U$, as one sees by multiplying out. Each $b_v$
can be lifted to $O$, giving a local integral first-order conjugation
with this reduction. For the alternating form $J$ with antidiagonal
$(1,1,-1,-1)$ one has $U^tJ+JU=0$ by multiplication. Thus
$1+\delta z(g)U$ preserves $J$ and $r_z(g)$ has multiplier
$d_0d_3=d_1d_2=\beta(g)\Psi(g)$, just as $r_0(g)$ does.
Conjugation by $\operatorname{diag}(ab,b,a,1)$ multiplies both
entries of $U$ by $b^{-1}$, giving the required bidegree $(0,-1)$.
On each local subgroup above, transporting an $r_0$-stable flag
by $1-\delta b_v U$ gives an $r_z$-stable flag with identical
characters on its successive quotients. Local conjugacy also preserves trivial action on
any inertia subgroup on which $r_0$ is the identity. For the global
normalization, $r_z(g)$ preserves the two coordinate planes with
indices $\{0,2\}$ and $\{1,3\}$ for every $g$, since both $U$
and the diagonal matrices preserve them. Moreover
$z(g_0^{-1})=-\alpha(g_0)^{-1}z(g_0)=0$. Hence the matrix
\[
 r_z(g_0)+d_0(g_0)r_z(g_0^{-1})
\]
is diagonal, and its required entries $(0,2)$ and $(2,0)$ vanish
even over the finite coefficient ring of dual numbers. These
normalization equations impose no further restriction on the class.

There is no further loss of length in passing from the matrix
entries to the reduced derivation, once an integral graded
presentation with the stated local conditions is available. Indeed
write $R\twoheadrightarrow S$ for the presentation and assume $S$
is topologically generated by the entries of the universal matrices.
A continuous first-order point over $B[\delta]/(\delta^2)$ with
fixed reduction defines a derivation by extracting its $\delta$
coefficient. On $R$ this derivation kills the kernel of the
presentation, since the point factors through $S$. Its values on
the matrix entries determine it by the product rule and continuity.
There they are linear in $z$, and entry $(2,0)$ recovers $z$;
hence the resulting assignment of reduced derivations is additive
and injective. This uniqueness applies even if there is more than
one choice of integral lift.

The rigidity condition needed at a characteristic-zero point of the
graded quotient has a concrete test. Write $A_K$ for the graded
algebra over an algebraically closed field $K$ of characteristic
zero, $I$ for its homogeneous ideal of relations, and $\xi$ for a
point killing $I$ that is nonzero on elements of both positive and
negative degree. Differentiate its grading orbit at the identity:
on a homogeneous $f$ of degree $n$ the derivative is
$D_0(f)=n\xi(f)$. Additivity of degrees gives the product rule;
homogeneity of $I$ gives $D_0(I)=0$. This derivative is nonzero
since some $n\ne0$ has $\xi(f)\ne0$. Thus the condition that any
two tangent derivations annihilating $I$ be dependent is equivalent
to vanishing of the tangent space modulo $K D_0$. Concretely one
may fix such an $f$ and subtract $D(f)D_0/D_0(f)$ from a given
derivation $D$. It suffices to prove that every remaining derivation
annihilating both $I$ and $f$ vanishes. This isolates the arithmetic
rigidity assertion from the one tangent direction forced by grading.

Here is why this tangent calculation suffices for the length estimate.
In the unsquared quotient over the algebraically closed generic
field $K$, assume the weight-zero algebra $B_0$ is finite-dimensional
and the full graded algebra $B$ is finitely generated over it.
Finiteness of the orbit cover at points nonzero in both weight
directions follows already from the first condition. Indeed choose
homogeneous $f,g$ of weights $p,-q$ with $p,q>0$ and $f(x)g(x)\ne0$.
Any $y$ agreeing with $x$ on $B_0$ also has $f(y)g(y)\ne0$,
by evaluating $f^qg^p$. Localize at this invariant. Both $f$ and
$g$ are now units, and $x,y$ still agree in degree zero.
For a homogeneous $h$ of degree $n$, the invariant $h^p f^{-n}$
shows that $h(x)=0$ exactly when $h(y)=0$. For any two nonvanishing
elements $h,h'$ of degree $n$, evaluation of
$h(h')^{p-1}f^{-n}$ and $(h')^pf^{-n}$ shows that
$h(y)/h(x)=h'(y)/h'(x)$. These common ratios multiply under
addition of degrees. They extend to a character of the subgroup
of $\mathbb Z$ generated by the nonvanishing degrees, by taking
quotients. This subgroup is $d\mathbb Z$ for some $d>0$.
Choose a $d$th root of the character value at $d$ in $K$.
Scaling by that root takes $x$ to $y$ in every degree.
There is thus at most one such orbit above each maximal ideal
of $B_0$, hence at most $\dim_K B_0$ orbits in total.

For the integral finiteness needed here, let $R_0$ be the complete
Noetherian local weight-zero ring, with finite residue field and
coefficient uniformizer $\pi$. It is finite over $O$ exactly when
$R_0/\pi R_0$ has dimension zero. Indeed in that case the special
fibre is Artinian and finite-dimensional over the coefficient
residue field. Lifts of a basis span $R_0$ over $O$: successively
expand a remainder modulo $\pi$, divide by $\pi$, and iterate.
The coefficients converge in $O$, and the remainders lie in
$\pi^mR_0\subseteq\mathfrak m_{R_0}^m$ after $m$ steps.
The converse follows by reducing a finite module modulo $\pi$.
If the special fibre has positive dimension, choose a nonmaximal
prime in it and an element of its maximal ideal outside that prime.
In the fraction field of the resulting domain the element is
nonzero and transcendental over the finite residue field of $O$:
any nonzero algebraic element satisfies $z^{\ell^a-1}=1$ for some
$a>0$, impossible for an element of the maximal ideal. Thus the
integral assertion requires excluding positive-dimensional
special-fibre quotients for the arithmetic weight-zero ring.
Such a prime can be lifted algebraically through a graded ring
whose degree-zero part is $R_0$, or through a completed presentation
admitting the continuous projection onto weight zero. In either
case the projection is $R_0$-linear and sends $1$ to $1$.
Tensor it with the residue field of the chosen prime. The resulting
fibre ring is nonzero because the projection still splits the unit
map. Any prime of that fibre gives a characteristic-$\ell$ point
retaining the transcendental invariant. In the complete Noetherian
local presentation this also gives a continuous specialization.
Indeed quotient the full special fibre by the kernel of this point
to obtain a complete local domain $A$ in which the chosen invariant
$z$ is nonzero in the maximal ideal. Extend $z$ to a system of
parameters $z,y_2,\ldots,y_h$, where $h=\dim A$: the dimension
of $A/(z)$ is at most $h-1$ by adjoining $(0)$ below each chain
over $(z)$, and the height theorem gives the reverse bound.
Some minimal prime of $(y_2,\ldots,y_h)$ omits $z$, since its
height is at most $h-1$ and the full parameter ideal is primary
to the maximal ideal. In the quotient by that prime, $z$ is a
nonzero parameter. This one-dimensional complete local domain
is finite over $k[[z]]$ for the coefficient residue field $k$,
by the same successive expansion, now modulo $z$.
The map from $k[[T]]$ sending $T$ to $z$ is injective since the
only nonzero prime of $k[[T]]$ contains $T$. The valuation ring
of its finite fraction field extension is a complete discrete
valuation ring containing the domain, and the map is local:
a power of its maximal ideal lies in $(z)$. The composite from
the presentation is therefore continuous, preserves its residual
matrices after residue field extension, and sends $z$ to positive
finite valuation.

Such a specialization is impossible if the off-diagonal support
of the normalized matrices is acyclic. Draw $i\to j$ for $i\ne j$
if some $r(g)_{ij}$ is nonzero. In a topological ordering the
matrices are triangular, so their diagonal entries are continuous
characters $\delta_i$. Let $U$ be the common kernel of the residual
diagonal representation and the auxiliary finite character $\psi$,
and $m=[G_{\mathbb Q}:U]$. For each finite inertia element $s$,
$s^m$ belongs to $U$. Away from $\ell$ the local conditions make
$r(s^m)=1$: at the prescribed ramified primes this uses the kernel
of $\psi$, and elsewhere the residual kernel. At $\ell$ the
ordinary inertia characters at $s^m$ reduce to one; in the ordinary
basis its matrix is therefore unipotent in characteristic $\ell$.
In either case all eigenvalues are one, giving $\delta_i(s)^m=1$.
Modulo each power of the uniformizer the finite character
$\delta_i^m$ is thus unramified at every finite prime. Its fixed
field has absolute discriminant one and hence is $\mathbb Q$ by
Minkowski's bound. Separatedness gives $\delta_i^m=1$ in the
valuation ring itself.

In fact a nonconstant weight-zero invariant forces a nonconstant
\emph{group trace}, even in the presence of cycles. To see this let
$K$ be the fraction field of the complete discrete valuation ring
just constructed, and let $k'$ be its field of elements algebraic
over $\mathbb F_\ell$. This field is finite: reduction embeds it
in the finite residue field since every nonzero element has finite
multiplicative order. Suppose every $\operatorname{tr}r(g)$ is
in $k'$. In the $k'$-algebra spanned by the group matrices every
element then has trace in $k'$, by linearity and the group law.
The multiplier also has values in $k'$ by its prescribed reduction.

All four coordinate projectors belong to this algebra. Indeed the
block-normalizing group matrix preserves the two coordinate planes
$\{0,2\},\{1,3\}$, with disjoint residual spectra across the planes.
Its eigenvalues are algebraic over $k'$: for a symplectic similitude
in rank four the characteristic coefficients are determined by the
multiplier and the traces of the matrix and its square (use
$2e_2=(\operatorname{tr}M)^2-\operatorname{tr}(M^2)$ and
$e_3=\theta\operatorname{tr}M$). The two block polynomials thus
have coefficients in $K$ algebraic over $k'$, hence in $k'$.
Their resultant is a unit by residual separation. Bezout gives
the projector $P$ onto $\{0,2\}$ as a polynomial in the matrix.
With $H=r(g_0)+\theta(g_0)r(g_0^{-1})$, the normalization makes
$PHP$ diagonal on that plane with distinct residual entries:
their difference reduces to $(\alpha(g_0)-1)(\Psi(g_0)-1)$.
Their sum and sum of squares are traces in $k'$, so both entries
are algebraic over $k'$ and lie in $k'$. Interpolating inside
$P M_4(K)P$ gives $E_0,E_2$. Taking symplectic adjoints gives
$E_3,E_1$, since the adjoint sends $r(g)$ to $\theta(g)r(g^{-1})$.

Products of entries along closed directed walks now lie in $k'$:
insert the corresponding projectors and take the trace of the product.
For a bidegree-zero monomial let $b_i$ be the number of row indices
$i$ minus the number of column indices $i$. The two weight equations
$b_0+b_2=b_0+b_1=0$ and $\sum b_i=0$ imply $b_i=b_{3-i}$.
In a second copy of the monomial replace each step $i\to j$ by
$3-j\to3-i$ in the inverse group matrix. This changes the product
only by signs and multipliers in $(k')^\times$. Its imbalances are
$-b_{3-i}$, so the combined edges split into closed walks. The
square of the original monomial therefore lies in $k'$, and the
monomial itself does as well. This holds also for adic limits of
polynomials in these monomials since $k'$ is finite and closed.
It contradicts the positive finite valuation of $z$. In the
acyclic case above all group traces are sums of finite-order
diagonal values, giving the promised exclusion. In general it
