suffices to check Frobenius traces at good primes. Indeed if a trace
lies outside $k'$, it differs modulo some fixed power of the uniformizer
from every element of that finite field. The representation modulo
that power has finite image. Chebotarev gives a good prime in the
conjugacy class of the offending element in this quotient. Its
Frobenius trace still lies outside $k'$.

Invert a product $fg$ of weights $-2,2$ as in the multiplicity
calculation. At each closed point $z$ of this open set, the orbit map
pulls $g$ back to $g(z)t^2$ on $\mathbb G_m$. Thus the orbit closure
is an irreducible curve through $z$: its coordinate algebra lies in
$K[t,t^{-1}]$ and contains a transcendental element. The local
dimension at $z$ is at least one. If the tangent space is spanned
by $D_0$ there, its dimension is one, so the Noetherian local ring
is regular of dimension one and hence reduced. If this holds at
every closed point of the open set, the localized algebra is
reduced: any nonzero nilpotent survives at a maximal ideal containing
its annihilator. Its weight-zero part is a localization of a
finite-dimensional algebra; at a point of its support it is
therefore a field. These are precisely the two conditions used above
to bound each unsquared weight piece by one.

To pass from a tangent of the graded quotient to a tangent of a
representation, consider its bigraded coefficient algebra $S$.
Suppose the graded quotient maps isomorphically onto the subalgebra
of first degree zero. At a lift whose representation matrices span
the full matrix algebra there are nonvanishing entries $u,v$ in
positions $(0,1),(1,0)$, respectively: otherwise that coordinate
functional would vanish on the full span. Their bidegrees are
$(1,0),(-1,0)$. After inverting $uv$, the element $u$ is a unit and
the localized algebra is Laurent polynomial in $u$ over its part
of first degree zero. Indeed a homogeneous element of first degree
$m$ becomes degree zero on multiplication by $u^{-m}$, and
distinct first degrees cannot cancel. A tangent $\xi+\epsilon D$
on the quotient extends across this localization since its value
on $uv$ is a unit in $K[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^2)$. Assigning $u$
its value at the chosen lift extends the tangent to $S$ and recovers
that lift on reduction. If the resulting representation tangent is
given by infinitesimal diagonal torus action with parameters $a,b$,
its value on bidegree $(m,n)$ is $(ma+nb)\xi$ there, provided the
representation entries determine the coefficient point over the dual
numbers. Restriction to $(0,n)$ then gives $D=bD_0$. The arithmetic
input in this argument is rigidity of the lifted representation
with its prescribed local conditions.

At a characteristic-zero symplectic similitude representation $r$
with fixed multiplier, write a first-order lift as $r+\delta Z$.
The product law gives $b(g)=Z(g)r(g)^{-1}$ the cocycle law for
conjugation by $r$. Differentiating the multiplier equation shows
$b(g)^*=-b(g)$, where $X^*=J^{-1}X^tJ$. To compare this cocycle
with a polarized adjoint representation let $P(X)=(X-X^*)/2$.
This projection commutes with conjugation by every similitude.
For a quadratic character $\nu$ let the action on matrices be
ordinary conjugation on $\operatorname{im}P$ and conjugation times
$\nu$ on $\ker P$. This defines an action since both summands are
stable. In it the same $b$ is a cocycle. If $b(g)=g\cdot X-X$
for that action, applying $P$ gives
$b(g)=r(g)P(X)r(g)^{-1}-P(X)$. Hence the deformation is conjugation
by $1-\delta P(X)$, with symplectic infinitesimal generator.
We explain why an inner tangent respecting the normalized entries
is diagonal. Write it as $[Y,r]$ with $Y$ symplectic in the Lie
algebra sense. At the block-normalizing element the matrix is
$M=\operatorname{diag}(d_i)+E$, where $M,E$ are block diagonal,
$\|E\|_{\max}<1$, and $|d_i-d_j|=1$ across distinct blocks.
The normalization makes $[Y,M]$ block diagonal as well. If $L$
is the largest norm of an off-block entry of $Y$, the off-block
entries of $[Y,E]$ have norm at most $L\|E\|_{\max}$ by the
ultrametric inequality. At an entry attaining $L$ the commutator
with the diagonal has norm $L$. Their sum being zero forces $L=0$.
For the other normalization set
$H=r(g_0)+\theta(g_0)r(g_0^{-1})$, with $\theta$ the multiplier.
The entries $02,20$ of both $H$ and its derivative $[Y,H]$
vanish. On the block $\{0,2\}$ these derivative entries are
$(H_{22}-H_{00})Y_{02}$ and its signed counterpart with $Y_{20}$.
The residual diagonal characters at $g_0$ are
$(\alpha\Psi,\Psi,\alpha,1)$, so $H_{00}-H_{22}$ reduces to
$(\alpha-1)(\Psi-1)$, a unit when both residual values differ
from one. Thus $Y_{02}=Y_{20}=0$. The symplectic relation pairs
the other possible off-diagonal entries $13,31$ with these two,
and pairs opposite diagonal entries with opposite signs. Hence $Y$
is in the two-dimensional diagonal Lie algebra and acts on each
bidegree by the weight formula above.

The same normalization identifies the isomorphisms that matter in
the trace count. Let $r,r'$ be absolutely irreducible normalized
points with the same multiplier and residual diagonal data, and
let $U$ intertwine them invertibly. At the first normalizing element
write $M=D+E$, $M'=D+E'$ with the same diagonal lift $D$.
Off the blocks, $UM=M'U$ gives
$(d_i-d_j)U_{ij}=(UE-E'U)_{ij}$. The right side has norm at
most $L\max(\|E\|_{\max},\|E'\|_{\max})$, with $L$ the largest
off-block norm of $U$. Thus $L=0$. Now $UH=H'U$ gives
$(H_{22}-H'_{00})U_{02}=(H_{00}-H'_{22})U_{20}=0$.
Both diagonal differences are units by the residual computation
above. Hence $U$ is diagonal on $\langle e_0,e_2\rangle$.
Moreover $U^*U$ commutes with $r$ because the multipliers agree,
so it is scalar by Schur's lemma. The symplectic pairing identifies
the action on the complementary block with the inverse transpose
on the first block times this scalar, in the paired basis.
Thus $U$ is diagonal and $U_{00}U_{33}=U_{11}U_{22}$.
Up to scalar its diagonal is $(st,t,s,1)$.

Consequently a finite cover of these normalized matrix points by
diagonal torus orbits contributes at most one representative per
isomorphism class once repeated orbits are deleted. Indeed two
isomorphic matrices are in the same orbit by the preceding calculation;
conversely conjugation gives an isomorphism. For the passage from
matrices to points one needs algebraic determination on each weight
piece. Here is the argument when the closed weight-zero algebra is
finite over $O$. Use finitely many homogeneous topological generators
among the centered entries (a spanning set of the relative cotangent
space suffices by successive approximation in the complete local
ring). In a fixed bidegree the exponent vectors of monomials have
finitely many componentwise minimal elements, by Dickson's lemma.
Every monomial of that degree is thus a minimal monomial times a
monomial of weight zero. The $O$-span of weight-zero monomials is
a submodule of a finite $O$-module, hence finite and compact.
The span in the given bidegree is a finite sum of its multiples,
hence compact and closed in the separated presentation. It contains
all limits of homogeneous partial sums in that degree. Consequently
two algebraic points agreeing on the entries agree on each weight
piece, even when defined only on the direct sum of those pieces.
On the subalgebra of
bidegrees $(0,j)$ the two-parameter action reduces to multiplication
by $t^j$ (with $t$ the parameter for the second degree).
Thus two lifted points representing isomorphic systems also restrict
to the same one-parameter orbit. In a comparison valid for every
finite orbit cover one can therefore choose a cover of minimum size
before summing trace valuations. This removes repetitions caused by
bases even when distinct systems have equal Frobenius trace.

The arithmetic vanishing needed here concerns the Bloch--Kato
Selmer group for this polarized adjoint action, for a suitable
imaginary quadratic character $\nu$. The irreducibility required
for this application is on $H=G_K\cap\ker\chi_\ell$. Here it follows
for the ordinary irreducible points with the indicated level.
Indeed $G_{\mathbb Q}/H$ is abelian. If the restriction were
reducible, the conjugates of any simple $H$-submodule would span
the whole representation by irreducibility. Since a sum of simple
modules is semisimple, a proper summand admits an $H$-equivariant
projection. Subtracting its normalized trace gives a nonzero
traceless element of $\operatorname{End}_H(V)$. On this traceless
space the conjugation operators commute, hence have a common
eigenvector $U$ over the algebraically closed coefficient field.
Its kernel is $G_{\mathbb Q}$-stable, so $U$ is invertible. Its
character $\phi$ is trivial on $H$, satisfies $\phi^4=1$ by
determinants, and is nontrivial by Schur's lemma. It is continuous:
its kernel is the closed centralizer condition on $r(g)$ and has
finite index.

We show that this character is unramified everywhere at finite
places. Take $K$ ramified only at the auxiliary prime away from
the level and $\ell$, as above. At that prime $r$ is unramified,
so inertia centralizes $U$. At every other prime away from $\ell$,
both $K$ and the cyclotomic tower are unramified, so inertia lies
in $H$. At $\ell$ let $s$ be any inertia element. The four ordinary
inertial eigencharacters $\psi_i$ consist of finite characters
times cyclotomic powers of weights $(j+1,j,1,0)$, with $\psi_3=1$.
Choose inertia $t$ in the kernels of the finite parts such that
$\chi_\ell(t)$ has infinite order; the cyclotomic image of this
finite-index subgroup is open in $\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$.
The self-twist preserves the eigenvalue multiset of $r(st^{4n})$.
Since this multiset contains one, for every $n\ge0$ some $i$ satisfies
$\phi(s)\psi_i(s)\psi_i(t)^{4n}=1$. An index repeats, forcing
$\psi_i(t)$ to have finite order, hence $i=3$ and $\phi(s)=1$.
The field cut out by $\phi$ is now unramified at all finite primes;
Minkowski's discriminant bound forces it to be $\mathbb Q$.
This contradiction proves irreducibility on $H$.

The irreducibility test after base change can be imposed by avoiding
one finite extension. At a point defined over a finite extension of
$\mathbb Q_\ell$, let $P$ be the joint image of $(r,\chi_\ell)$
on $G_K$. It is compact and preserves a lattice (the sum of all
translates of a fixed lattice is bounded). Over $\mathbb Z_\ell$
it embeds in some $\mathrm{GL}_m(\mathbb Z_\ell)$. Its open normal
subgroup $U=P\cap(1+\ell^2 M_m(\mathbb Z_\ell))$ is a finitely
generated pro-$\ell$ group. Indeed the successive congruence quotients
embed by their leading coefficients in $M_m(\mathbb F_\ell)$.
Taking $\ell$th powers preserves the leading coefficient and raises
its depth by one, so these subspaces increase and eventually stabilize.
Lifts of bases through the last
increase generate densely by successive cancellation in the filtration.

Put $V=\overline{U^\ell[U,U]}$. Then $U/V$ is finite by finite
generation, and $V$ is normal in $P$. Let $M/K$ be the finite Galois
extension cut out by $P/V$. If $L/K$ is linearly disjoint from $M$,
the image $P'$ of $G_L$ surjects onto $P/V$. Thus $P'\cap U$
surjects onto $U/V$ and equals $U$: a proper closed subgroup would
have proper image in a finite $\ell$-group quotient, hence lie in
an index-$\ell$ subgroup containing $V$. It follows that $P'=P$.
Taking the kernel of the cyclotomic coordinate now preserves the
entire matrix image, so the irreducibility just proved over
$K(\mu_{\ell^\infty})$ holds over $L(\mu_{\ell^\infty})$ as well.
Scalar twisting preserves every invariant subspace.

We record how vanishing over a finite CM extension would give the
required coboundary over $\mathbb Q$. Let $L$ contain $K$, let
$L^+$ be its maximal real subfield, and choose complex conjugations
$c_0,c_1$ for $K,L$ respectively, acting on a common algebraic
closure, with $c_1$ fixing $L^+$. Put $w=c_1c_0^{-1}\in G_K$.
Suppose $P_0$ realizes the polarization on $G_K$, so that for
$g\in G_K$ one has
$r(g)^tP_0r(c_0gc_0^{-1})=\theta(g)P_0$ and $c_0$ acts on the
adjoint by $X\mapsto-P_0^{-1}X^tP_0$. For a twist
$r_L=\kappa\,r|_{G_L}$ set $P_1=P_0r(w)^{-1}$.
For $g\in G_L$, writing $g'=c_1gc_1^{-1}$, direct multiplication gives
\[
 r_L(g)^t P_1 r_L(g')=\kappa(g)\kappa(g')\theta(g)P_1 .
\]
Indeed $g'=w(c_0gc_0^{-1})w^{-1}$. The scalar twist cancels in
ordinary conjugation on $G_L$, and on $c_1=wc_0$ the restricted
adjoint acts by $X\mapsto-P_1^{-1}X^tP_1$. These formulas identify
the entire action on $G_{L^+}$, since $G_L$ has index two there.
In rank four the multiplier test for the twisted point is therefore
$\kappa(g)\kappa(g')\theta(g)=\chi_\ell(g)^{-3}$.

There is a separate identification of the representation at the
ordinary point. Write $q:G_L\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma$ for the
quotient used to define it. Let $B$ represent the ordinary
determinants with the prescribed residual data and ordered diagonal
characters. A pair consisting of such a determinant and characters
over a coefficient ring $C$ gives a map $B\to C$ by this universal
property. Specialization at $y:C\to\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell$
therefore gives a point of $B$. If the determinant on $q(g)$
has characteristic polynomial equal after specialization to that of
$r_L(g)$, its trace on $q(g)$ equals $\operatorname{tr}r_L(g)$.
For $k\in\ker q$ one obtains, for every $g$,
\[
 \operatorname{tr}\bigl(r_L(g)(r_L(k)-1)\bigr)=0 .
\]
If $r|_{G_L}$ is absolutely irreducible, the span of these matrices
$r_L(g)$ is the full matrix algebra by Burnside, since twisting by
nonzero scalars preserves the span. Testing against matrix units
gives $r_L(k)=1$. Thus $r_L$ itself factors uniquely through
$q$, continuously for the quotient topology, and the ordinary
characters at the point are the specialized ordered characters.
In checking the characteristic polynomials the twist acts on the
coefficient of $X^{4-i}$ by $\kappa(g)^i$, by homogeneity of the
principal minors. This identifies the representation once the
ordinary determinant pair over $C$ has been constructed.

For the determinant part of this construction, suppose the matrices
are defined over a commutative overring $A\supset C$ and their
characteristic polynomials have coefficients in $C$ depending only
on $q(g)$. This suffices even before passing to a field. For a
finite list of group elements with matrices $M_i$ introduce variables
$t_i$. For a word $w$ let $M_w$ and $t_w$ denote the respective
products in the order of its letters. Then
\[
 \det(1-\textstyle\sum_i t_iM_i)
 =\prod_{[w]}\det(1-t_w M_w),
\]
where $[w]$ runs over primitive nonempty words up to cyclic rotation.
Each degree uses finitely many factors. For generic matrices over
$\mathbb Q$ take formal logarithms, using
$\log\det(1-Z)=-\sum_{m\ge1}\operatorname{tr}(Z^m)/m$,
as follows by logarithmic differentiation in a scalar parameter.
The $k$th power of a primitive word of length $d$ occurs in $d$
rotations with the same trace; division by $kd$ gives $1/k$,
the corresponding term on the right. Each coefficient identity
has integral polynomial coefficients in the generic entries, hence
specializes to every $A$. The degree-four part on the left is
$\det(\sum_i t_iM_i)$. On the right all coefficients belong to
$C$ and depend only on the images of the letters in $\Gamma$.
Thus these polynomials define the determinant on formal linear
combinations of quotient elements. Combining repeated letters and
multiplying two such combinations respect the determinant identities:
test them first in independent polynomial variables over $A$, where
they are matrix identities, then use $C\hookrightarrow A$ and
specialize. This gives a multiplicative degree-four polynomial law
over $C$, including after arbitrary scalar extension.

Let $J$ be the sum in $C$ of the extended modular ideals for two
distinct auxiliary places with the same twist. A point kills $J$
precisely when both induced points kill their modular ideals.
Put $I=JA$ in the matrix-entry ring. All components through a
residual prime $p$ lie in $V(I)$ exactly when $IA_p\subset\sqrt{0}$,
by localization of minimal primes. Then every domain-valued lift
with kernel contained in $p$ kills $I$. Containment of one component
is insufficient: in $k[x,y]/(xy)$ the ideal $(x)$ vanishes on one
component through the origin, yet the lift $x\mapsto t$, $y\mapsto0$
to $k[[t]]$ does not kill it.

Residual points joined by a family $A\to k[t]$ at $t=0,1$ share
a component: a minimal prime below its kernel lies below both
endpoint kernels. Thus containment of all components in $V(I)$ at
one endpoint gives containment of one at the other. Along a chain
from a cuspidal point it suffices to show at each endpoint that
containment of one implies containment of all.

For this implication work in the completed local ring $R$. For
adjacent minimal primes $P,Q$ with $IR\subset P$, seek an arc
$a:R\to k'[[t]]$ killing $P+Q$, with irreducible generic representation
and distinct ordinary inertia characters at each place. If every prime
below $\ker a$ contains $IR$ for such arcs killing $IR$, then $Q$
contains $IR$. A connected graph of these intersections propagates
containment to every minimal prime. Nilpotence descends to the local
ring since completion is faithfully flat.

Here is a dimension criterion for selecting the arc. Write $\pi$
for the coefficient uniformizer and assume
$\dim R/(P+Q)\ge N+1$, $N\ge1$. Let $A_i$ cut out the finitely many
reducibility loci and $B_j$ the finitely many inertia-character
collisions in the special fibre on $V(IR)$. Suppose
$\dim R/A_i<N$ and $\dim R/\mathfrak b<N$ for every minimal
prime $\mathfrak b$ over a $B_j$ containing no $A_i$. Then some
prime $\mathfrak p\supset P+Q+(\pi)$ with $\dim R/\mathfrak p=1$
contains none of the $A_i,B_j$.

Indeed imposing $\pi$ drops dimension by at most one, by the
parameter theorem. Choose a component of this quotient of dimension
$e\ge N$. Its prime contains none of the $A_i$ or the listed
$\mathfrak b$, by dimension. In the resulting local domain choose
one nonzero element from the image of each of those ideals and
multiply them together with a nonzero nonunit. The product $u$
starts a system of parameters $u,u_2,\ldots,u_e$: its quotient has
dimension $e-1$ by the parameter theorem and the domain condition.
Some minimal prime over $(u_2,\ldots,u_e)$ avoids $u$, since otherwise
that ideal would be primary to the maximal ideal, contrary to the
height theorem. Modulo this prime $u$ is a parameter, so the quotient
has dimension one. Its inverse image avoids all $B_j$ as well:
a prime containing $B_j$ contains a minimal prime over it, and that
prime either contains an $A_i$ or is on the list.

Put $D=R/\mathfrak p$. The simple roots of $X^{|k|}-X$ lift the
finite residue field $k$ into this complete equicharacteristic
domain. For any parameter $z$, lifts of a basis of $D/zD$ generate
$D$ over $k[[z]]$ by successive reduction and completeness.
Its fraction field is a finite extension of $k((z))$; expansion in
a uniformizer identifies the valuation ring with $k'[[t]]$ for a
finite residue extension $k'/k$. It contains $D$ by integrality.
The inclusion is local since a power of $m_D$ lies in $zD$.
Thus the arc has exactly the selected kernel.

In rank four the dimension loss from a collision can be counted
in the coefficient ring. Suppose there are $g$ places of local
degree $h$ above $\ell$, and put $d=gh$. Work in a prime quotient
of $C/(J,\pi)$ where the ordinary inertia characters are residually
trivial and occur in inverse pairs $(0,3),(1,2)$. Assume the fibre
where all these characters are trivial is zero-dimensional.
At each place $h$ inertia values suffice to prescribe a character:
local reciprocity identifies the image of inertia in the abelianized
decomposition group with the local units, whose pro-$\ell$ part has free rank
$h$. The finite torsion maps trivially into the principal units of
a local domain of characteristic $\ell$: the prime-to-$\ell$ part
is trivial by reduction and the $\ell$-power part by Frobenius.
These tests apply in every further prime quotient.

If two labels agree on the cyclotomic kernel in inertia, equality
on one further element forces equality on all inertia: for odd
$\ell$ the cyclotomic image is procyclic and the characters are
continuous. The same holds if the kernel was first intersected
with a finite-index subgroup, by the torsion argument. At this
place at most one independent character remains: either the two
inverse pairs become identified, or one character equals its own
inverse and is trivial. Imposing $h$ values at this place and
$2h$ at each other place, together with the one equality just
described, cuts out the trivial-character fibre set-theoretically.
The parameter theorem bounds the dimension of the collision
quotient by $2d-h+1$.

The cost of passing to matrix entries on the absolutely irreducible
locus is at most $2+4g$ in the following finite type presentation.
Let $A$ be generated over the complete Noetherian local coefficient ring $C$
by the representation entries and one lower unitriangular symplectic
matrix at each place. Suppose the traces and multiplier belong to
$C$, and a distinguished group element is diagonal with distinct
diagonal entries at the residual point. Work first at a prime $p$ of
$A$ below that point, in the field $\operatorname{Frac}(A/p)$.
Adjoin the four diagonal eigenvalues to $\operatorname{Frac}(C/(p\cap C))$;
they are algebraic by the characteristic polynomial over $C$.
The spectral projectors $E_i$ are now polynomials in this one group
matrix. Thus any product of entries around a closed path is in the
resulting field: insert the projectors between the group matrices
and take the trace, then expand to get a linear combination of
traces of group elements.

Regard an entry $r(s)_{ij}$ as a step from $i$ to $j$.
Absolute irreducibility ensures some group matrix has a nonzero
entry in each position, by the full matrix span. Adjoin one
nonzero entry in position $01$ and another in position $02$.
If the latter is $r(s)_{02}$, the inverse matrix has entry
$r(s^{-1})_{13}=-r(s)_{02}/\theta(s)$ in our symplectic basis.
We now have paths from $0$ to every index with known nonzero
products $b_i$, taking the path $0,1,3$ for $i=3$.
For each $j$ choose a return entry $c_j\ne0$ from $j$ to $0$
(use the empty path for $j=0$). The closed product $b_jc_j$
determines $c_j$. Then the closed product $b_i r(s)_{ij}c_j$
determines every entry. Each lower unitriangular matrix $U$
adds at most four parameters $U_{10},U_{20},U_{30},U_{21}$:
the symplectic equations give $U_{32}=-U_{10}$ and
$U_{31}=U_{20}-U_{21}U_{10}$. This proves the transcendence
degree bound.

For a representing deformation ring the identification
$R=\widehat{A_x}$ follows when $A$ parametrizes the same normalized
matrices and flags: local maps from $A_x$ to an Artinian local test
ring factor through some $A_x/x^n A_x$, so the completion represents
the lifts at $x$. Uniqueness on the Artinian quotients and passage
to their inverse limits identify the complete rings. For a prime
$q\subset R$, take $p=q\cap A$. Then $R/q$ is a quotient of the
completion of $(A/p)_x$, of the same dimension as $(A/p)_x$
before taking that quotient. The dimension formula for the finite
type domain $A/p$ over the universally catenary local domain
$C/(p\cap C)$ bounds this dimension by $\dim C/(p\cap C)$ plus
the transcendence degree just computed (the closed residue extension
is algebraic). This proves the claimed dimension bound in the
completed presentation. If each reducibility locus has dimension
at most $d+20C_0+1$, the
required two strict inequalities hold for $N=2d+1-2g$ whenever
$1\le g\le C_0$ and $d\ge30(C_0+1)^2$. Indeed
$N-(d+20C_0+1)=d-2g-20C_0>0$, while
$N-(2d-h+1+2+4g)=h-6g-2>0$ since $h=d/g$.
The intersection bound is needed along a chain. Suppose
$R=S/(f_1,\ldots,f_b)$ with $S=O[[X_1,\ldots,X_a]]$ for a
complete discrete valuation ring $O$, and $a-b\ge N+1$. Join two
minimal primes by an edge if their intersection as components has
dimension at least $N+1$. This graph is connected. Indeed a
partition without a crossing edge would give ideals $U,V$ in $S$
defining the two unions of components, with $\dim S/(U+V)\le N$.
Adjoin to both ideals lifts of a system of parameters for this
quotient, say $t_1,\ldots,t_s$ with $s\le N$, obtaining $U',V'$.
Their sum is primary to the maximal ideal $m$. Each of their
varieties still has positive dimension: before the cut every
component has dimension at least $a+1-b$, by the height theorem
and catenarity. Put $n=a+1$. Hartshorne--Lichtenbaum vanishing
over the complete local domain $S$ gives
$H^n_{U'}(S)=H^n_{V'}(S)=0$, whereas $H^n_m(S)\ne0$ by local
nonvanishing. Mayer--Vietoris thus gives
$H^{n-1}_{U'\cap V'}(S)\ne0$. But $U'\cap V'$ has the same radical
as $(f_1,\ldots,f_b,t_1,\ldots,t_s)$, and its \v Cech complex
has length at most $b+s\le a-1=n-2$, a contradiction.

The required arithmetic presentation count is consequently
$a-b\ge 2+2d-2g$. To see the constants, let $h^0,h^1,h^2$ be
the dimensions of infinitesimal automorphisms, deformations and
obstructions in the adjoint complex with ordinary flag conditions.
Before imposing the inertia-character equations, let $a$ be the
relative tangent dimension. Under the hypotheses of the following calculation,
at most $h^2$ relations suffice, $a+h^0\ge h^1+2$ and
$h^2+h^0+6d\le h^1+4d$. Subtraction gives $a-h^2\ge2+2d$.
Prescribing trivial ordinary characters on a cyclic inertia subgroup
with multiplier one adds at most two equations per place: set the
first two diagonal characters to one at a generator, and their
partners are their inverses. The quotient represents exactly the
lifts satisfying these character conditions. This gives the desired
count once the tangent, obstruction and Euler estimates are verified
for the deformation problem. The local dimensions here can be read
off directly: a symplectic Borel in rank four has Lie dimension six
(two diagonal and four positive root spaces). An anti-symplectic
involution in odd characteristic has complementary Lagrangian
eigenspaces, paired perfectly. A commuting symplectic Lie algebra
element is an arbitrary endomorphism of one eigenspace and its
negative transpose on the other, giving dimension four.

For clarity we give the Euler calculation, including the effect of
the inertia quotient. Let $F$ be totally real of degree $d$,
$M=\mathfrak{sp}_4(k)$ over the finite residue field of odd
characteristic $\ell$, and $S$ the places above $\ell$.
At $v\in S$ use the quotient $M_v=M/\mathfrak b_v$ of dimension
four. Suppose the residual representation is triangular after
ordering its four characters, and no ratio of these characters
equals the residual cyclotomic character $\omega$ on $G_F$.
Then $H^0(F,M^*(1))=0$: the composition factors of the endomorphism
representation are precisely the character ratios, as follows by
tensoring the residual filtration with its dual. An equivariant map
$M\to k(1)$ would give that factor in the endomorphism space.

Enlarge $S$ to $S'$ containing the ramification of the data, and
first use $G'=G_{F,S'}$. Use the full local module $M$ at places
in $S'\setminus S$. The degree-two global-to-local map onto the
sum of the local $H^2$ is surjective by the Poitou--Tate tail and
the vanishing just proved; quotienting $M$ by $\mathfrak b_v$
preserves local surjectivity by cohomological dimension two.
Hence the shifted mapping cone has zero $H^3$. Its Euler
characteristic is the global characteristic minus the local sum:
the former is $-10d+4d$ by the global Euler formula and the
real-place calculation above, and the latter is $-4d$ by the
local Euler formula. Thus its characteristic is $-2d$.

Suppose now $G'\twoheadrightarrow\Gamma$ kills the closed normal
subgroup generated at the extra places by $J_v$, the images of
inertia subgroups of finite index prime to $\ell$ in inertia.
Use only the local terms at $S$ for $\Gamma$. Write $e$ for the
dimension of the kernel of inflation on $H^2$ and $h^i_G$ for
$\dim H^i(G,M)$. Inflation--restriction gives
\[
 h^1_{G'}-h^1_\Gamma+e
 \le\sum_{v\in S'\setminus S}\dim H^2(F_v,M).
\]
Indeed the left side is the dimension of the invariant
homomorphisms from the kernel to $M$. Restriction to its normal
generators is injective. Locally, writing $I$ for full inertia
and $J\triangleleft G_{F_v}$ for the subgroup of $I$ whose image defines $J_v$,
$J$ acts trivially on $M$. Taking invariants of the prime-to-$\ell$
quotient identifies $\operatorname{Hom}(J,M)^{I/J}$ with
$H^1(I,M)$. Frobenius invariants of this last finite space have
the dimension of its coinvariants, which is $\dim H^2(F_v,M)$
by Hochschild--Serre for inertia at $v\nmid\ell$.

For either group, exactness expresses the alternating sum through
degree two of its cone as $h^0_G-h^1_G+\dim\ker(\mathrm{res}^2)$
plus the sum of local $h^1-h^0$. Inflation takes the kernel of
degree-two restriction for $\Gamma$ into that for $G'$: at the
extra places the restriction map factors through $H^2(G_{F_v}/J,M)=0$
(finite prime-to-$\ell$ inertia and unramified quotient
$\widehat{\mathbb Z}$). Thus the first kernel has dimension at most
the second plus $e$. At each omitted place $h^1-h^0=h^2$ by the
local Euler formula. The displayed inequality now bounds the
truncated characteristic for $\Gamma$ by $-2d$, exactly the
Euler estimate used above. Applying it requires the stated
character-ratio condition for the tower. In the following conductor
range one can check it already at the initial field. Write the
four residual characters, in some order, as
$1,\bar\xi,\bar\Psi,\bar\xi\bar\Psi$, with $\xi$ odd of primitive
conductor $2^n$ and $\Psi$ even. Suppose that $\bar\Psi$ on inertia
at $2$ has exponent dividing $2^b$. Take the initial totally real
field fixed by the simultaneous conditions
$\bar\Psi=\phi$, $\eta=\eta'$ and $\kappa=1$, where
$\phi,\eta,\eta'$ are quadratic and $\kappa$ has $\ell$-power
order. If $n\ge b+4$, every ratio is different from $\omega$.
Indeed a ratio is $\bar\xi^r\bar\Psi^s$ with $r,s\in\{-1,0,1\}$.
For $r=0$ use complex conjugation, which fixes the totally real
field and has cyclotomic value $-1$. For $r=\pm1$, choose an
inertia element at $2$ acting by $3$ on the $2^n$th roots of unity;
the local cyclotomic extension is totally ramified. Its value under
$\bar\xi$ has order $2^{n-2}$. Indeed
$v_2(3^{2^j}-1)=j+2$ for $j\ge1$, by successive squaring.
Thus $3^{2^{n-3}}$ is the nonidentity element in the kernel of
reduction modulo $2^{n-1}$, on which primitivity forces the value
$-1$. This value survives reduction since $\ell$ is odd.
Raise the inertia element to $2^{\max(1,b)}\operatorname{ord}(\kappa)$.
The resulting element fixes the initial field, kills $\bar\Psi$,
and still has nontrivial $\bar\xi$ value. Its cyclotomic value is
one since $\ell\ne2$. Finally, for each ratio $\rho$ the image of
$\rho/\omega$ has order prime to $\ell$. Restricting to a subgroup
of $\ell$-power index preserves that image: its image index divides
both the group index and the original image order. This proves the
same separation throughout extensions of $\ell$-power degree.
For the auxiliary character in Case II one may take $b$ to be the
conductor exponent of the $2$-primary component of the Dirichlet
character $\psi$ in $\bar\Psi=\omega^e\bar\psi$: on inertia at $2$
only this component contributes, and its image has order dividing
$2^b$ by reduction to $(\mathbb Z/2^b\mathbb Z)^\times$.
The choice of $n$ in that case takes the ceiling of a base-two
logarithm whose argument is at least $2^{b+6}$. It therefore
satisfies the required separation.

The inertia description
itself follows from the specified local deformation condition. Let
$K_0$ be the kernel on $G_F$ of the four residual diagonal
characters together. It is open and normal, of index dividing
$(|k|-1)^4$, since its quotient embeds in $(k^\times)^4$.
Outside $S$ the condition prescribes triviality on $I_v\cap K_0$.
Take $S'$ to include the ramified places of the finite extension
cut out by $K_0$. Outside $S'$ this kills all inertia. The remaining
quotient of $G_{F,S'}$ is obtained by killing the closed normal
closure of the images of $I_v\cap K_0$ for $v\in S'\setminus S$,
and $I_v/(I_v\cap K_0)$ embeds in $G_F/K_0$ as required.
This construction uses the diagonal characters even when the
residual deformation has nonzero off-diagonal entries.

The passage from a complete obstruction space to the number of
equations also has a short algebraic proof. Choose a minimal
relative power series presentation $S\twoheadrightarrow R$ before
the inertia-character equations, and write $I$ for its kernel.
With $m$ the maximal ideal of $S$, minimality means
$I\subset m^2+\pi S$. The extension $S/mI\to R$ has kernel
$E=I/mI$ annihilated by $m$. Suppose its universal lifting
obstruction belongs to $H^2\otimes_k E$, is functorial under
quotients of $E$, and vanishes precisely when a lift exists.
Contraction then injects $E^*$ into $H^2$. Indeed if a nonzero
functional annihilated the obstruction, pushing out along it
would give a split extension $S'\to R$ with kernel $k$.
The canonical map $S\to S'$ and the map through the section
differ by an $O$-derivation to $k$. It kills $m^2+\pi S$, hence
$I$, while their difference on $I$ is the chosen nonzero
functional. This contradiction gives $\dim E\le h^2$, and
Nakayama lifts a basis to generators of $I$. The arithmetic task
here includes compatibility with the normalization and continuity
of the lifts. The obstruction for a representation with invariant
flags can be seen at the cochain level. Choose a continuous
set-theoretic lift $s(g)$ with prescribed multiplier. In the lifted
local flag basis choose a continuous upper triangular lift $s_v(g)$
with the same multiplier for $g\in G_v$. Identify the congruence
kernel with $M\otimes E$ by $1+X\mapsto X$. The product defect
$u(g,h)=s(g)s(h)s(gh)^{-1}-1$ is a two-cocycle by associativity.
In a common basis put $v_v(g)=s(g)s_v(g)^{-1}-1$ modulo the
residual Borel. The defect of $s_v$ is Borel-valued, hence
$d v_v=u|_{G_v}\bmod\mathfrak b_v$. Thus $(u,(v_v))$ is a
degree-two cocycle of the shifted cone. Changing a global lift
adds a coboundary in this cone, and moving a lifted flag adds the
local coboundary of its infinitesimal displacement in $M_v\otimes E$.
Conversely a cone coboundary gives a global cochain cancelling the
product defect and local displacements cancelling the flag defects.
The corrected lift is a homomorphism preserving the corrected flags.
Every formula commutes with quotienting $E$ since the kernel has
square zero.

For a small extension of complete Noetherian local rings with
finite residue field and finite kernel $E$, the set-theoretic
lifts used here can be chosen continuous. For either the symplectic
similitude group or its upper triangular Borel, map a matrix to
its reduction together with its multiplier in the larger ring.
This map onto compatible pairs is surjective: lift the unit
multiplier by a diagonal similitude, then use smoothness of the
split symplectic group or its Borel to lift the multiplier-one
part. Its kernel is finite and the groups are profinite, by
reduction modulo powers of the maximal ideal. An open normal
subgroup avoiding the nonidentity kernel elements maps
homeomorphically onto its image, an open subgroup of the target.
Choosing lifts on its finitely many cosets gives a continuous
section. Composing with the Galois data gives the asserted cochains.

There is also no extra obstruction to restoring the chosen basis.
At the distinguished element the lifted matrix reduces to a diagonal
matrix with four distinct residual eigenvalues. Hensel factorization
and the four spectral projectors split the free module into free
eigenlines lifting the prescribed ones. The similitude pairing of
two lines is zero unless their eigenvalues multiply to the
multiplier: for unpaired indices the difference is a unit by
reduction. On paired lines the form is perfect, so rescaling their
generators restores the original symplectic basis modulo the kernel
and its pairing exactly. The resulting basis change reduces to the
identity. Transport the local flags by it. Their normalized lower
unitriangular representatives exist uniquely by Gaussian elimination:
the leading minors are units by reduction. For a symplectic flag
this lower factor is symplectic, by uniqueness of the lower times
upper factorization under the involution $g\mapsto (g^*)^{-1}$.

Finally let $Z$ consist of the continuous cone cocycles in degree
one, with local flag displacements taken modulo $\mathfrak b_v$.
The map $M\to Z$ sending $X$ to its global coboundary and local
classes has kernel $H^0$ and cokernel $H^1$. Thus
$\dim Z=h^1+10-h^0$. Requiring the value of the global cocycle at
the distinguished element to be diagonal imposes at most eight
linear conditions, since the diagonal in $M$ has dimension two.
Each remaining cocycle gives a normalized dual-number lift:
multiply the residual matrices on the left by $1+\epsilon z(g)$
and move each flag by the negative of its local displacement,
using the convention $z|_{G_v}=dX_v\bmod\mathfrak b_v$.
The cocycle and flag equations give multiplicativity and flag
invariance; the normalized flags and the matrices recover the
cocycle and displacements. This injection into the relative tangent
space of the representing ring proves $a+h^0\ge h^1+2$.

One can compute this class on the quotient unramified outside the
chosen set of places $S$. Take $S$ to contain all places above
$\ell$ and let $N$ be the closed normal subgroup generated by
inertia outside $S$.
If the adjoint factors through the quotient, it acts trivially on
$N$. On each generating inertia group the unramified local condition
says the cocycle is a coboundary, hence zero there. Its restriction
to $N$ is a homomorphism, and its kernel is closed and stable under
conjugation by the full group, by the cocycle identity. It therefore
vanishes on $N$ and induces a continuous cocycle on the quotient.
Suppose the restricted Selmer cocycle is a coboundary for that
adjoint. Subtract this coboundary on $G_{\mathbb Q}$ and call the
result $z$, so $z|_{G_{L^+}}=0$. The affine action
$g*x=gx+z(g)$ has $0$ fixed by $G_{L^+}$. Its orbit is finite,
being indexed by cosets of this finite-index subgroup. The
barycentre $b$ of the orbit is fixed by every $g$, giving
$z(g)=b-gb$. This proves descent without a normality assumption on
$L^+/\mathbb Q$. Its application still requires a twisted automorphic
point over a CM field $L$ with the indicated multiplier. Specifically,
if the twist is attached to a polarizable regular algebraic cuspidal
representation of $\mathrm{GL}_4(\mathbb A_L)$ with this polarization,
and $L$ meets the disjointness condition above, Thorne's
Theorem~6.1 gives $H^1_f(L^+,\operatorname{ad}r_L)=0$
\cite{ThorneAdj}. The local conditions on the restricted class
must still place it in this group.

At a finite prime $p\ne\ell$ the local condition is
unramifiedness of the class, which follows by averaging. Indeed the
local deformation condition at a designated ramified prime requires
the lift to be trivial on inertia intersected with the kernel of the
finite character $\eta$. At the other primes away from $\ell$ the
corresponding requirement uses the kernel of the residual
representation. In either case there is an open normal subgroup
$H$ of inertia $I_p$ on which the entire lift is trivial. Both the
adjoint action on the symplectic summand and the tangent cocycle
therefore descend on inertia to $F=I_p/H$. Set
$y=-|F|^{-1}\sum_{h\in F} b(h)$ in that summand. Summing
$b(gh)=b(g)+g\cdot b(h)$ over $h$ gives $b(g)=g\cdot y-y$ on
inertia, as required. Division by $|F|$ is valid in characteristic
zero even when $\ell$ divides that order. At $p=\ell$ there is a separate character test. On an open
local subgroup where the finite inertial characters disappear, write
$\kappa_i$ for the ordinary diagonal characters of the specialization,
numbered from zero. Their restrictions to inertia are
$\chi_\ell^{w_i}$ with $(w_0,w_1,w_2,w_3)=(j+1,j,1,0)$.
For $i<h$ the test excludes $\kappa_i=\kappa_h\chi_\ell$ on
this subgroup. Its inertial cyclotomic image is infinite (it has
finite index in the cyclotomic image of local inertia), so this
equality would force $w_i-w_h=1$. For $j\ge3$ only $(0,1)$ and
$(2,3)$ survive this comparison; for $j=2$ one must also test
$(1,2)$. For each remaining pair the ratio
$\kappa_i/(\kappa_h\chi_\ell)$ is unramified there, hence is
nontrivial exactly when its value on a lift of residue Frobenius
is different from one, by continuity and procyclicity of the
unramified quotient. For the distinguished Frobenius lift $s$ in the chosen
open subgroup the auxiliary data prescribe $\chi_\ell(s)=1$.
Let $v=\overline\Psi(s)$ and $u=\overline\xi(s)$, where $\xi$
is the auxiliary finite character in
$\beta=\chi_\ell\omega^{-1}\xi$ and $\overline\omega=
\overline\chi_\ell$. To verify the order of specialization, let $B$ be the henselian local
domain of the ordinary component, $r_B$ its representation, $x$ its
diagonal point with characters $\psi_i$, and $y$ the point in question.
Write $a_i=\psi_i(s_0)$ for the distinct coefficient-ring values at
the distinguishing inertia element. Put $A=r_B(s_0)$ and
$P_i=\prod_{h\ne i}(A-a_h I)$. The ordinary component is defined
with $\det(XI-A)=\prod_i(X-a_i)$, with $P_i r_B(g)P_h=0$ for
$i>h$, and with $P_i(r_B(\tau)-\psi_i(\tau)I)P_i=0$ on inertia.
These polynomial identities survive base change. Over the fraction
field and at $y$, choose eigenvectors of $A$ in the order $a_i$.
Each $P_i$ acts by a nonzero scalar on the $i$th line and by zero
on the other three. The second identity therefore kills every
entry below the diagonal of $r_B(g)$ in these bases, and the third
prescribes its diagonal on inertia. The diagonal entries multiply
under the group law and hence are characters. For fixed $i,g$ put
\[
 d=\prod_{h\ne i}(a_i-a_h),\qquad
 t=\operatorname{tr}\!\left(\prod_{h\ne i}(r_B(s_0)-a_h I)\,r_B(g)\right).
\]
In each ordinary triangular basis the polynomial factor has diagonal
zero except in position $i$, where it equals $d$. Hence
$x(t)=d\psi_i(g)$ and $y(t)=d\kappa_i(g)$. At the generic point
$t/d$ is the corresponding diagonal entry of $r_B(g)$ and satisfies
its monic characteristic polynomial.

Thus $z=t/d-\psi_i(g)$ is integral over $B$. The finite domain
$S=B[z]$ is local, since a finite algebra over a henselian local
ring is a product of local rings. Both points extend to $S$ with
values in $\overline{\mathbb Q}_\ell$, by lying over their kernels
and embedding the algebraic residue field extensions. As $d$ has
nonzero image at both points, the first extension sends $z$ to zero
and the second sends it to $c=\kappa_i(g)-\psi_i(g)$. In particular
$z$ belongs to the maximal ideal of $S$. If $|c|_\ell\ge1$, then
$1/c$ is integral over $\mathbb Z_\ell$. Reversing a monic equation
for $1/c$ gives $P\in\mathbb Z_\ell[X]$ with $P(0)=1$ and $P(c)=0$.
Now $P(z)$ is a unit in $S$ with zero image at the second point,
a contradiction. Therefore $|c|_\ell<1$. Taking $g=s$ gives the
residual values $(uv,v,u,1)$ in order.
The residual ratios for $(0,1)$ and $(2,3)$ are therefore both
$u$, and for $(1,2)$ the ratio is $v/u$. Thus $u\ne1$ and,
when $j=2$, $u\ne v$ suffice at every specialization with these
labels. Both follow from the auxiliary condition
$uv^i\ne1$ for every integer $i$, taking $i=0,-1$. For Case II in the modulus-one construction this separation at
$\ell$ follows directly from the conductor. Choose a Frobenius lift
$s$ over $\mathbb Q_\ell$ with $\chi_\ell(s)=1$: multiply any lift
by an inertia element, using total ramification of the cyclotomic tower.
Write $2^a=\operatorname{ord}(\psi)\le 2^{\max(1,b)}$ by the two
quadratic factors away from two. For $H=2^n$ as above choose the odd
character $\xi$ taking $-1$ to $-1$ and $5$ to a primitive
$2^{n-2}$th root. Indeed $5$ generates the principal units
$1\bmod4$ modulo $2^n$ since $v_2(5^{2^r}-1)=r+2$.
Thus $\xi$ is faithful there and has conductor $2^n$.
If $uv^i=1$ for some integer $i$, raising to $2^a$ gives
$\xi(\ell)^{2^a}=1$: the cyclotomic factor at $s$ is one,
$\xi$ is unramified at $\ell$, and reduction preserves its
two-power order. Squaring yields $\ell^{2^{a+1}}\equiv1\pmod{2^n}$
by faithfulness on principal units. Yet
\[
 2^{v_2(\ell^{2^{a+1}}-1)}
 =2^a 2^{v_2(\ell^2-1)}\le 2^a(\ell^2-1)<H
\]
by successive squaring and $H\ge2^{b+6}\ell^3$.
This proves separation for the specified rational Frobenius, also
after raising it to any odd power since $u,v$ have two-power order.
For a local subgroup of even residue degree the power must be checked
separately. Applying the local criterion to the arithmetic
representations still requires component membership and the global
Selmer vanishing input.

For $j=1$ the conductor separation at the exceptional primes can be
made explicit. Write $2^c$ for the conductor of the $2$-component
of $\eta$. On removing this component one is left with a product of
quadratic characters modulo $q,d$, of order at most two. The least
common multiple of this order and four is therefore four. The
multiplicative order of $\ell$ modulo four is two for both exceptional
primes. Thus the parameter $v_2(\ell^2-1)$ is $3$ at $\ell=3$ and
$4$ at $\ell=7$. The conductor test
$2^c<16\ell^2 4^{v_2(\ell^2-1)}$ becomes respectively
$c\le13$ and $c\le17$, since the two right sides are $9216$ and
$200704$.

In this range the field cut out by $\eta$ has degree at most $2048$
at $3$ and $32768$ at $7$. Indeed the $2$-component is even and, for
$c\ge3$, factors through the cyclic group
$(\mathbb Z/2^c\mathbb Z)^\times/\{\pm1\}$ of order $2^{c-2}$.
For $c<3$ it is trivial. Adjoining the two quadratic character
components can raise the order of the product only to the least common
multiple of this bound and two. The field cut out by the product has
degree equal to its order. These bounds concern field degrees;
estimating the cohomological lengths in those fields remains necessary.

The local term in degree two admits a single bound even for large
twists and ramified characters. By local duality its dual over
$\mathbb Q_\ell$ is the invariant subgroup of
$M=(\operatorname{Frac}O/O)(-j)\otimes\eta^{-1}$, where $O$
contains the character values. Put $h=\operatorname{ord}(\eta)$.
Every invariant is killed by $z^{jh}-1$ for every
$z\in\mathbb Z_\ell^\times$: lift $z$ to cyclotomic inertia
(the cyclotomic tower is totally ramified) and raise its action
to the $h$th power. For odd $\ell$, taking a primitive
$(\ell-1)$st root gives zero invariants unless $(\ell-1)\mid jh$.
Taking $z=1+\ell$ then bounds their normalized $O$-length by
$1+v_\ell(jh)$; normalized length means length divided by the
ramification index. At two, $z=5$ gives the bound $2+v_2(jh)$.
These valuations follow by binomial expansion, first raising to
the prime-to-$\ell$ part and then successively to the $\ell$th
power (at two the initial valuation is two).
For each $j$ the sum weighted by $\log\ell$ is therefore at most
\[
 2\log 2+\sum_{\ell\le jh+1}\log\ell+\log(jh)
 \ll jh+1
\]
by Chebyshev's bound, since $\sum_\ell v_\ell(jh)\log\ell=\log(jh)$.
Writing $n=[E:\mathbb Q]$, both $h$ and the character count are
at most $n$, so the total for $j<t$ is $O(t^2n^2)$. Over $E$ the
cyclotomic image at $w\mid\ell$ has index dividing
$[E_w:\mathbb Q_\ell]$ by intersection with the cyclotomic tower.
This degree divides $n$ since $E$ is Galois. Hence every $z^n$ is
in the image, and taking $h=n$ at each of at most $n$ places gives
the same polynomial bound for the local sum over $E$, even for
$t=\lfloor x^{401}\rfloor$.

The finite class group calculation arising from cyclotomic descent
has the following concrete form. Fix a base field $L$ containing
$\mu_{\ell^c}$ but not $\mu_{\ell^{c+1}}$, where $c\ge1$ for odd $\ell$ and $c\ge2$ for $\ell=2$.
At level $\nu\ge c$, put $F=L(\mu_{\ell^\nu})$, let $\gamma$ act
on the roots by the exponent $1+\ell^c$, and set $a=(1+\ell^c)^j$.
Such a generator exists since the cyclotomic extension above level $c$
is cyclic of prime-power degree and every nontrivial intermediate
extension contains its first degree-$\ell$ layer. Write $A$ for the
ideal class group of $F$ in additive notation. The number of functions
$f$ from its finite primes to $\mathbb Z/\ell^\nu$ satisfying
\[
 \sum_P v_P(u)f(P)=0\quad(u\in F^\times),\qquad
 f(\gamma^{-1}P)=a f(P)
\]
is exactly
\[
 \left|A\big/\big(\ell^\nu A+(\gamma^{-1}-a)A\big)\right|.
\]
Indeed $f$ extends uniquely to a homomorphism on fractional ideals.
The first equation makes it factor through $A$, and the second makes
it annihilate $(\gamma^{-1}-a)A$. Since the target is killed by
$\ell^\nu$, it factors through the displayed quotient. Conversely
every homomorphism from that quotient satisfies both equations.
Each cyclic summand of the quotient has order $\ell^h$ with
$0\le h\le\nu$, and admits exactly $\ell^h$ homomorphisms to
$\mathbb Z/\ell^\nu$, proving the count. In the preceding odd-prime application
$c=1$ by the ramification calculation above. Finiteness of the class
group gives a finite count at each level. To control the global kernel
by this descent one needs a bound independent of $\nu$.

One factor in the class group comparison can be evaluated in every
layer. Put $n=[F:L]=\ell^{\nu-c}$, $r=r_2(L)$, and
$U_F=\mathcal O_F^\times/\mu(F)$. For $a=(1+\ell^c)^j$ as above,
\[
 [U_F:(\gamma-a)U_F]=\frac{(a^n-1)^r}{a-1},
 \qquad
 v_\ell([U_F:(\gamma-a)U_F])
 =r(\nu+v_\ell(j))-(c+v_\ell(j)).
\]
Here the free unit group is written additively. To prove the first
identity, note that $L$ is totally imaginary and each of its $r$
complex places splits into $n$ complex places on which $\gamma$ acts
as an $n$-cycle. By the logarithmic unit theorem the real span of
$U_F$ is the hyperplane of sum zero in this permutation representation.
On the full space the characteristic polynomial is $(X^n-1)^r$;
on the hyperplane it is $(X^n-1)^r/(X-1)$. Evaluating at $a>1$
gives the absolute determinant, hence the index on the integral
lattice. Finally for odd $\ell$ the binomial expansion gives
$v_\ell((1+\ell^c)^h-1)=c+v_\ell(h)$ for $h\ge1$.
For $\ell=2$, an odd power preserves the valuation of the difference from one, and each squaring increases it by one since the base is $1\bmod4$. This gives the same formula. Apply it with $h=jn$ and $h=j$.

Thus the $\ell$-part of this unit index grows by a factor
$\ell^r$ whenever $\nu$ increases by one. A uniform estimate for the
class group quotient needs a matching upper estimate on the arithmetic
objects to which the units are compared; the determinant alone
does not give it.

One ingredient in the upper estimate is detection of powers by
reduction. Let $m=\ell^\nu$, assume $\mu_m\subset F$, and let
$\beta\in F^\times$. If $\beta$ is an $m$th power in the residue
field at every prime of $F$ of residue degree one over $L$, apart
from finitely many exceptions, then $\beta\in F^{\times m}$.

Indeed, otherwise $K=F(\beta^{1/m})$ is a nontrivial Galois extension:
all the roots differ by elements of $\mu_m$. A nonidentity automorphism
fixes no root, since fixing any one root fixes $K$. By the Chebotarev
density theorem a positive-density set of unramified primes of $F$
has Frobenius in the conjugacy class of such an automorphism. Exclude
the primes dividing $m$ or the divisor of $\beta$. At every remaining
prime the roots reduce distinctly and Frobenius has no fixed root,
so the reduction of $\beta$ is not an $m$th power. The primes of $F$
of relative residue degree at least two have density zero: their
Dirichlet series for real $s>1$ is bounded by
$[F:L]\sum_{\mathfrak p\subset\mathcal O_L}
(\mathrm N\mathfrak p)^{-2s}$, summed over nonzero prime ideals,
which stays bounded as $s\downarrow1$. Thus the positive-density set
contains a relative degree-one prime outside the exceptional set,
a contradiction. Applied to a quotient of two elements, this proves
that distinct classes modulo $F^{\times m}$ are distinguished by
some such reduction. The argument allows the detecting prime to
depend on the pair of classes and on $\nu$.

The rank factor in the count can be understood from block sums.
Write $D_\ell$ for the homology map induced by $g\mapsto
\operatorname{diag}(g,\ldots,g)$ with $\ell$ blocks. Stabilization by
identity blocks identifies the levels in the following calculation.
For a degree-three class $z$, the two successive differences have
stable image
\[
 P(D_\ell)z=(D_\ell-\ell^3)(D_\ell-\ell^2)z.
\]
In fact in every odd degree $m\ge3$ the image of the $m-1$
successive differences has rational rank at most $r_2(L)$ for $L$
totally imaginary. To see this, start at level $n\ge 2m+4$. The
target level $\ell^{m-1}n$ is at least $4m+5$, so Charney's stability
theorem for Dedekind domains identifies the target homology with
stable homology. Only injectivity at this final level is needed.
In stable rational homology the operator is
$\prod_{h=2}^m(D_\ell-\ell^h)$.

With direct sum as multiplication, stable rational homology is the
free graded-commutative Hopf algebra on its primitives, whose degree
$i$ part is $K_i(\mathcal O_L)\otimes\mathbb Q$ by the rational
Hurewicz theorem for the plus construction. The block map is diagonal
followed by $\ell$-fold direct sum. It therefore multiplies a
primitive by $\ell$, and a product of $h$ primitives by $\ell^h$.
Every monomial in positive degree $m$ has between one and $m$
factors. The displayed polynomial kills all monomials of length at
least two. Its image is contained in the degree-$m$ primitives,
whose dimension is $r_2(L)$ by Borel's rank theorem for a totally
imaginary field (see \cite{CharneyStab,WeibelK}, especially
\cite[IV, \S1]{WeibelK}). This proves the asserted rank bound at
the finite target level.

For a finitely generated abelian group $M$ of rank at most $r$, any
image of $M$ in a group killed by $q=\ell^\nu$ has at most
$q^r|M_{\rm tors}|$ elements, by factoring through $M/qM$ and applying
the elementary divisor theorem. For the integral torsion estimate put $d=[L:\mathbb Q]$ and let $S$ be a finite
subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_N(\mathcal O_L)$. Then
\[
 |S|\le 3^{dN^2}.
\]
Indeed reduction modulo $3\mathcal O_L$ is injective on $S$.
Otherwise its kernel contains an element $g$ of prime order $p$.
Write $g=1+3^bA$ with $b\ge1$ maximal, using the freeness of the
matrix ring over $\mathbb Z$. If $p\ne3$, the first term of $g^p-1$
is $p3^b A$ and the remaining terms vanish modulo $3^{b+1}$,
a contradiction. If $p=3$, division by $3^{b+1}$ in the cubic
expansion gives $A+3^bA^2+3^{2b-1}A^3$, nonzero modulo three.
Finally the matrix ring modulo three has $3^{dN^2}$ elements.

In particular this bound applies to the stabilizer of an ordered
simplex of positive definite Hermitian forms on the Minkowski space
of $\mathcal O_L^N$. Fixing one vertex already forces the images of a
lattice basis to have prescribed lengths in a positive definite real
norm. Each image therefore has finitely many choices. 

Now suppose an augmented acyclic ordered simplicial
chain complex with a group action has at most $A$ simplex orbits in
each degree up to $m+1$, and all stabilizers have order at most $s$.
Here the chains are freely generated by the ordered simplices and
the action preserves their face maps. Then
\[
 \log|H_m(G,\mathbb Z)_{\rm tors}|
 \le A\log(m+2)+A\log(s)\sum_{q=1}^m s^q .
\]
To prove this, tensor the chain complex with a free right bar
resolution over $\mathbb ZG$. Exactness of the augmentation gives
$H_*(G,\mathbb Z)$ as the total homology. Filtering by simplex degree
gives in bidegree $(p,q)$ a direct sum of $H_q(S,\mathbb Z)$ over
orbit representatives with stabilizers $S$, by the induced-module
formula. For $q>0$ transfer kills each summand by $|S|$; the bar
complex gives its cycles at most $|S|^q$ generators, since they form
a subgroup of a free group of that rank. Its order is thus at most
$s^{s^q}$. All subsequent terms in this bidegree are subquotients.

In row zero the first differential is the signed face map on orbit
generators. Every column from degree $m+1$ has Euclidean norm at most
$m+2$, including when some faces coincide. If its rank is $b>0$,
Smith normal form bounds the torsion order of its cokernel by the
absolute value of any nonzero $b$-minor, at most $(m+2)^A$ by
Hadamard's inequality; at rank zero the cokernel is free.
Row-zero homology embeds in this cokernel. No later differential
enters bidegree $(m,0)$, so its final torsion satisfies the same
bound. Multiplying the bounds along total degree $m$ proves the
inequality by the filtration of the abutment. Thus for the arithmetic
group it remains in this step to exhibit such a complex with a
sufficiently small orbit count.

Here is an orbit estimate for the faces that have a common frame
of minimal vectors. Put $b=dN$, $D=|\operatorname{disc}L|$ and
$I=D^N b^b$. Write the form as $Q(v)=\sum_w v_w^*H_wv_w$, summing
over the complex places. Normalize its minimum on nonzero integral
vectors to one. In the Euclidean metric given by $Q$ the lattice has
covolume $2^{-b/2}D^{N/2}\prod_w\det H_w$, by the discriminant formula
and the real Jacobian of the complex linear map $H_w^{1/2}$.
Balls of radius $1/2$ have disjoint interiors modulo this lattice;
each contains a cube of side $1/\sqrt b$. Consequently
$\prod_w\det H_w\ge I^{-1}$.

Suppose the vertices of an ordered $p$-simplex share $N$ minimal
vectors forming an invertible matrix $A$ over $L$. For any one of its
vertices let $G_w=A_w^*H_w A_w$. All diagonal entries are at most one,
so Hadamard's inequality gives $\det G_w\le1$. Moreover
$\prod_w\det G_w=k\prod_w\det H_w$, where
$k=|\mathrm N_{L/\mathbb Q}\det A|$ is the index of the column lattice.
It follows that $k\le I$ and each $\det G_w\ge I^{-1}$.
Two frames give the same left $\mathrm{GL}_N(\mathcal O_L)$-orbit
if their lattices $A^{-1}\mathcal O_L^N$ agree, since then
$A'A^{-1}$ preserves $\mathcal O_L^N$ in both directions.
Such a lattice contains $\mathcal O_L^N$ with index $k$, and modulo
$\mathcal O_L^N$ is a subgroup of $(\mathbb Z/k)^b$. It has at most
$b$ generators by Smith normal form, hence at most $k^{b^2}$
possibilities for fixed $k$. There are at most $I^{1+b^2}$ frame orbits.

For any further minimal vector $v$, put $u=A^{-1}v$. Each $G_w$
has trace at most $N$, so its smallest eigenvalue is at least
$I^{-1}N^{1-N}$. Since $u_w^*G_w u_w\le1$ and $ku$ is integral,
every conjugate of every coordinate of $ku$ has modulus at most
$R=I\sqrt{IN^{N-1}}$. There are at most $(2R+1)^d$ algebraic integers
in this box: distinct integers have distance at least one in the
maximum norm on the complex places, by the field norm, and disjoint
open balls of radius $1/2$ fit in the ball of radius $R+1/2$.
Thus at most $(2R+1)^b$ vectors can be minimal with this frame.
A perfect vertex is determined by $h=(d/2)N^2$ linearly independent
equations $Q(v)=1$, since $h$ is the real dimension of the form
space. The orbit count for ordered tuples of such vertices with a common
frame is therefore at most $I^{1+b^2}(2R+1)^{bh(p+1)}$.

For completeness construct the complex on the dual cone. Let
$\mathcal R$ be the set of positive definite tuples $H$ satisfying
$Q_H(v)\ge1$ for every nonzero integral $v$. It is nonempty by scaling
and convex. For each positive definite dual tuple $X$, minimize
$\sum_w\operatorname{tr}(X_wH_w)$ over $\mathcal R$. Bounded
sublevels bound the traces of all $H_w$ from above, uniformly for
$X$ in compact subsets of the open dual cone. The determinant bound
just proved consequently bounds their least eigenvalues away from
zero. These sublevels are compact and only finitely many lattice
vectors can give active constraints there. The minimizing face is
therefore a compact polytope, whose vertices are perfect forms:
at an extreme point the active linear equations must span the dual,
since otherwise a small displacement in both directions preserves
all constraints. Conversely such a spanning set determines a vertex.

The regions of dual tuples minimized by the various vertices form a
locally finite polyhedral cover of the open dual cone. Indeed on a
compact neighborhood the common trace bound applies by comparison
with a single fixed feasible form. Only finitely many vertices can
occur there by the finite active set, and their regions are cut out
locally by the linear comparisons of their values. All nonempty
intersections are convex. Their nerve is contractible by the
polyhedral nerve theorem (subdivide the locally finite polyhedral
decomposition inside the open cone to obtain a cover by subcomplexes).
For vertices in a simplex of this nerve take their average $H$ and
a common minimizing $X$. The active constraints at $H$ are precisely
their common minimal vectors. If those vectors fail to span $L^N$,
take a nonzero $L$-linear functional vanishing on them and let $P$
be the positive semidefinite tuple of its squared absolute values.
For small $s>0$, $H-sP$ remains feasible by local finiteness of the
constraints. But $\sum_w\operatorname{tr}(X_wP_w)>0$, contradicting
minimality. Thus every nerve simplex has the common frame used above.

Use all ordered tuples supported on nerve simplices, allowing repeats,
with the alternating deletion boundary. This augmented complex has
the homology of the nerve: project a tuple to its oriented simplex
(zero on repetitions) and choose a vertex ordering for a chain section.
The composite is chain homotopic to the identity by induction on
degree inside each support simplex, where prepending any fixed
vertex contracts the augmented tuple complex. The acyclicity so
obtained holds integrally; the section need not respect the group
action. The tuple complex itself carries the required action. This
supplies both the acyclicity and the orbit bound used above.

Two integral constructions enter when passing from divisors to
homology classes. First, let $I_j,J_j$ be nonzero fractional ideals
of $F$, indexed by $1\le j\le n$, and suppose
$\prod J_j=\beta\prod I_j$ as fractional ideals. There is an
$F$-linear map carrying $\bigoplus I_j$ isomorphically to
$\bigoplus J_j$ with determinant exactly $\beta$. Indeed Steinitz
classification gives an isomorphism of the two projective modules,
which extends to $F^n$. Its determinant $b$ satisfies
$b\prod I_j=\prod J_j$ by taking top exterior powers. Hence
$\beta/b$ is a unit, and multiplication by this unit on the first
target summand corrects the determinant. In rank zero the assertion
requires $\beta=1$.

Second, one can isolate a prescribed slope over the integers before
reducing the cycles. Put $q=\ell^\nu$, $d_0=\ell^c$,
$\kappa=1+d_0$, $c\le\nu$, and fix $0\le t_0\le M<d_0$. Write
$P(Z)=\prod_{j=0}^{M-1}(Z-\kappa^j)=\sum_i p_i Z^i$.
In the integral group ring of $(\mathbb Z/q)^2$ there is an element
whose image under $(x,y)\mapsto x+ty$ vanishes for
$0\le t\le M$, $t\ne t_0$, and at $t_0$ equals
$F(X)=\sum_i p_iX^{\kappa^i}$, with $X^q=1$.

To prove this, set $u_i=(\kappa^i-1)/d_0$ and
$H(Z)=\sum_i p_iZ^{u_i}$. For $0\le h<M$ the coefficient of $W^h$
in $H(1+W)$ is $\sum_i p_i\binom{u_i}{h}=0$: the binomial is a
polynomial of degree $h$ in $\kappa^i$, and
$\sum_i p_i(\kappa^i)^j=P(\kappa^j)=0$ for $j<M$.
Thus $(Z-1)^M$ divides $H$ in $\mathbb Z[Z]$, and
$(X^{d_0}-1)^M$ divides $F(X)=XH(X^{d_0})$. For each
$t\ne t_0$ in the range, $\gcd(t_0-t,q)$ divides $d_0$.
Consequently $X^{t_0-t}-1$ divides $X^{d_0}-1$ in the group ring:
choose an integer $n\ge0$ with $n(t_0-t)\equiv d_0\pmod q$
and use the geometric sum. Hence $F$ is a multiple of
$\prod_{t\ne t_0}(X^{t_0}-X^t)$ there, say with multiplier $B(X)$.
Using the two generators $X,Y$ of the larger group ring, the required
element is $B(X)\prod_{t\ne t_0}(Y-X^t)$. Substitution $Y=X^t$
proves both assertions, without division in the residue ring.
The scalar in the corresponding separated weight
has a loss independent of the layer. Indeed for $0\le u<M$ its
integer value and valuation are
\[
 C_u=\prod_{0\le j<M,\ j\ne u}(\kappa^u-\kappa^j),\qquad
 v_\ell(C_u)=(M-1)c+v_\ell(u!)+v_\ell((M-1-u)!).
\]
Each factor has valuation $c+v_\ell(|u-j|)$ by the binomial
valuation formula above. In degree three ($M=3,u=1$) this gives
$C_1=-\kappa\ell^{2c}$. Multiplication by $C_1$ on
$\mathbb Z/\ell^\nu$ therefore has kernel of order
$\ell^{\min(2c,\nu)}$, uniformly bounded with $c$ fixed.
This isolates the determinant component in the coefficient construction;
realizing the resulting data as a cycle with the specified reduction
at every admissible residue field remains to be shown.

The chain that realizes a slope relation has coefficients in
$R=\mathbb Z/q$, $q=\ell^\nu$. Put $C=C_q$, $D=C\times C$, and
$d_t(x)=(x,x^t)$. For any $R$-cycle $z$ of degree $h=2e+2$ in
$C$, there exist coefficients $n_t\in R$, $0\le t\le h+1$, at
least one a unit, and a chain $b$ in $D$ satisfying
\[
 \partial b=\sum_t n_t(d_t)_*z.
\]
Indeed the cyclic integral free resolution has alternating
differentials $g-1$ and $1+g+\cdots+g^{q-1}$. Tensoring two copies
over $\mathbb Z$ resolves the trivial module for $D$: both augmented
complexes split over $\mathbb Z$. After taking coinvariants with
coefficients $R$ both differentials vanish. Hence $H_h(D;R)$ is free
of rank $h+1$, one summand for each bidegree of total degree $h$.
Lift the $h+2$ coordinate columns of the indicated classes to
$\mathbb Z^{h+1}$. They have an integral relation with relatively
prime coefficients, obtained by clearing denominators in a nonzero
rational relation and dividing by their gcd. Reduction gives a
relation with a unit coefficient, and its vanishing in homology
gives the asserted $b$ in the bar complex.

For homomorphisms $\rho,\sigma:D\to H$ suppose that
$\rho d_t=k_t^{-1}(\sigma d_t)k_t$ with $k_t\in H$.
The conjugation prism $P_t$ on bar chains satisfies
$\partial P_t+P_t\partial=(\rho d_t)_*-(\sigma d_t)_*$:
it is the simplicial homotopy of the two one-object group functors
given by conjugation, with the orientation chosen as displayed.
Then the degree-$h+1$ chain
\[
 y=\sigma_*b-\rho_*b+\sum_t n_t P_tz
\]
is a cycle. Applying $\partial$ cancels the two differences term by
term since $\partial z=0$. For $e=0$ this gives a degree-three cycle;
the argument neither requires nor produces a lift to a cycle over
$\mathbb Z$. Application to the divisor construction requires the two
representations in the same integral lattice stabilizer and the
prescribed values on the reduced prisms.

To evaluate the reduced prism, let $k$ be a finite field,
$M=|k^\times|$, $q\mid M$, and let $A(a)\in[0,M-1]$ denote the
discrete logarithm to base $u$, a generator of $k^\times$.
The integer carry $B(a,b)=(A(a)+A(b)-A(ab))/M$ is a symmetric
two-cocycle by associativity. Write $\zeta=u^{(M/q)s}$ for the
chosen primitive $q$th root, with $s$ a unit modulo $q$.
For a homomorphism $v:k^\times\to R$ the cup product
$s^{-j}v\smile B^{\smile j}$ is a cocycle. Use the degree-$2j$
cycle obtained by summing $[g^{i_1}|g|\cdots|g^{i_j}|g]$ over
all residues $i_1,\ldots,i_j$, for a generator $g$ of $C_q$.
Its first face has coefficient $q=0$; the adjacent internal faces
cancel in pairs by shifting the summation indices, and the last
internal face cancels the last face by the same shift.
Under $g\mapsto w=\zeta^x$, insert $b\in k^\times$. All terms except the first cancel in pairs:
the two insertions in each carry slot interchange its arguments.
The first term contains $j$ copies of $\sum_i B(w^i,w)=qA(w)/M\equiv sx\pmod q$ by telescoping.
Its value is therefore $x^jv(b)$.

Here is the extension to matrices. Let $\rho:G\to\mathrm{GL}(V)$
be a representation over $k$. Choose a nonzero vector on each line
in $\mathbb P(V)$. Regard $G$ as a category with one object. A bar
simplex lifts to its action groupoid on the lines once the initial
line is specified; sum over all initial lines. This operation commutes
with the boundary: composing consecutive arrows commutes with lifting,
and deleting the first arrow permutes the starting lines. Send each
lifted arrow to the scalar by which it carries the chosen vector on
its source to the chosen vector on its target. Scalars multiply under
composition, giving a chain map to the bar complex of $k^\times$.
Composing with the preceding cocycle defines an evaluation on cycles.
Rescaling the chosen vectors gives naturally isomorphic functors,
whose chain maps are homotopic by the bar prism. The evaluation is
therefore independent of the choices and invariant under isomorphism.
For a one-dimensional representation it is the original evaluation.

For a block sum $V\oplus W$, send each line with nonzero first
projection to its image in $V$ and the remaining lines to $\mathbb P(W)$.
Over a line in $V$ with chosen vector $v$, the lifts are represented
uniquely by $(v,w)$ with $w\in W$. With these representatives the
scalar for a lifted arrow is precisely that on the first projection.
Each line of $W$ has one lift and its usual scalar. Thus the transfer
of the block sum is the sum of $|k|^{\dim W}$ copies of the first
transfer and one of the second. Since $q\mid |k|-1$, the first
multiplicity equals one in $R$. This proves additivity on cycles,
including the contributions of mixed lines. In positive degree a
trivial representation contributes zero, so stabilization preserves
the evaluation and repeating a block $d$ times multiplies it by $d$.

For a nonselected slope the lattice transport can also be made
explicit. Let $J=(\mathbb Z/q)^2$ and write $\eta_z$ for the integral
coefficient at $z$ of the selector above. For a fractional ideal
$\mathfrak a$ form the lattices
$M_0=\bigoplus_{z\in J}\mathcal O_F$ and
$M_1=\bigoplus_{z\in J}\mathfrak a^{\eta_z}$ in $F^J$.
The group $D$ acts diagonally by
$\rho(u,v)e_z=\zeta^{uz_1+vz_2}e_z$ in additive coordinates and
preserves both lattices. At $t\ne t_0$, every fiber of
$z\mapsto z_1+t z_2$ has coefficient sum zero. The product of its
target ideals is therefore $\mathcal O_F$. The determinant construction
above gives on that fiber a map of determinant one carrying the source
to the target lattice. Their direct sum $g_t$ carries $M_0$ onto
$M_1$ and commutes with $\rho d_t$, which acts by a scalar on
each fiber.

At the selected slope write $x=z_1+t_0z_2$ and let $S$ act on
divisors by $S\mathfrak d=\gamma^{-1}\mathfrak d$. The divisor
condition on the input is
$\operatorname{div}\alpha=(S-\kappa^j)\mathfrak d$, with
$\mathfrak d=\operatorname{div}(\mathfrak a)$ here denoting the
divisor of the fractional ideal. Put
$Q(Z)=P(Z)/(Z-\kappa^j)=\sum_i b_i Z^i$ for $0\le j<M$, and set
\[
 \beta=\prod_i(\gamma^{-i}\alpha)^{b_i}.
\]
Equivariance of principal divisors gives
$\operatorname{div}\beta=Q(S)(S-\kappa^j)\mathfrak d
=P(S)\mathfrak d$.

Change coordinates over $L$ as follows. On a coordinate of weight
$x=\kappa^i\bmod q$ apply $\gamma^{-i}$, and on all other coordinates
apply the identity. This is well-defined since $\gamma$ acts faithfully
on $\zeta$ with exponent $\kappa$. Call the resulting map $U$.
In the new coordinates the selected slope acts by $\zeta$ on the whole
orbit of weight one, and by the original eigenvalues elsewhere.
The source lattice is unchanged. In the weight-one block the product
of the transformed target ideals has divisor
$\sum_i p_i S^i\mathfrak d=P(S)\mathfrak d$ by the defining pushforward
of $\eta$. This identity also handles collisions among the
$\kappa^i\bmod q$, since their powers of $S$ then coincide. In each
other block the divisor sum is zero. The weight-one block is nonempty
(it contains $z=(1,0)$). The prescribed-determinant construction
therefore gives a block diagonal map $g'$ from $M_0$ to $UM_1$
with determinant $\beta$ on that block and one on the others.
Now $g_0=U^{-1}g'U$ transports $M_0$ to $M_1$ over $L$ and commutes
with the selected slope. With $H=\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathcal O_L}(M_0)$
and $\sigma=g_0^{-1}\rho g_0$, both representations preserve $M_0$.
Also $k_t=g_0^{-1}g_t\in H$ satisfies
$k_t^{-1}(\sigma d_t)k_t=\rho d_t$, as required in the prism construction.

The scalar that the determinant must recover can be checked before
taking homology. Let $f$ be reduction followed by $v$ at a prime
where all conjugates of $\alpha$ are units. For such units put
$W(b)=\sum_{h=0}^{n-1}\kappa^{hj} f(\gamma^h b)$ in $R$, where
$n=[F:L]$. Since $\kappa^n=1$ in $R$, reindexing gives
$W(\gamma^{-i}\alpha)=\kappa^{ij}W(\alpha)$. Thus
\[
 W(\beta)=Q(\kappa^j)W(\alpha)
 =C_j f\left(\prod_{h=0}^{n-1}
                (\gamma^h\alpha)^{\kappa^{hj}}\right).
\]
To evaluate the cycle, exclude the finitely many primes dividing
$q$, the relative discriminant, or the denominators of the lattices,
maps and their inverses. At a remaining relative degree-one prime
the Galois extension splits completely over the underlying prime.
The Chinese remainder theorem identifies restriction of scalars
after reduction with the direct sum of the $n$ conjugate blocks,
with $\gamma^h$ acting on the entries in the $h$th evaluation.
If $M_0$ is projective over $\mathcal O_L$, choose a projective
complement to make it free and extend its automorphisms by the
identity. Such a complement exists since a torsion-free finite
module over a Dedekind domain is projective. The added trivial
representation contributes zero.

In the localized group, write $P_g$ for the homotopy from a
representation to its conjugate by $g^{-1}$. On cycles the prisms
for two successive conjugations add modulo boundaries: the two-edge
path and its composite bound the natural-transformation triangle
(the usual triangulation of the bar simplex times a two-simplex).
Applying this with $g_t=g_0 k_t$ shows that the image of $y$ is
homologous to $\sum_t n_t P_{g_t}(\rho d_t)_*z$.
Indeed the terms $P_{g_0}(\rho d_t)_*z$ add to
$P_{g_0}\rho_*\partial b$, homologous to
$\sigma_*b-\rho_*b$ by the prism identity. All these maps can now
be reduced at the chosen prime.

For a scalar slope acting by $w$ on a block, evaluation of its prism
is additive as a function of the commuting matrix $g$ by the same
triangle. It factors through the determinant: over the residue field
of size greater than two the elementary matrices are commutators
(conjugate by a diagonal matrix with distinct entries in the two
relevant positions), and generate the special linear group by
elimination. On $g=\operatorname{diag}(b,1,\ldots,1)$ block
additivity and the scalar calculation give $x^jv(b)$ for $w=\zeta^x$;
the identity prism is null in homology. Thus the nonselected slopes
give zero because their block determinants are one. On the selected
slope use $U$ to read the reduced blocks in the coordinates of $g'$.
In the $h$th conjugate the surviving block has weight $\kappa^h$
and determinant the reduction of $\gamma^h\beta$. The evaluation
of $y$ is therefore $n_{t_0}W(\beta)$. Scaling the cycle by the
inverse of the unit $n_{t_0}$ gives the claimed determinant evaluation
$C_jW(\alpha)$ at every allowed degree-one prime.

We now count several divisor classes at once. Fix $m=2j+1$,
$j\ge1$, and put $N_*=4m+5$. Let $T_i$ be the order of the torsion
in $H_i(\mathrm{GL}_{N_*}(\mathcal O_L),\mathbb Z)$, for $i=m,m-1$.
These two numbers are independent of $q$. Consider a finite set of
inputs $\alpha$ satisfying the divisor condition. Write
$u_\alpha=\prod_{h=0}^{n-1}(\gamma^h\alpha)^{\kappa^{hj}}$, and put
$b=m-1+v_\ell(C_j)$. If the elements $u_\alpha^{\ell^b}$ are distinct
in $F^\times/F^{\times q}$, the number of inputs is at most
\[
                     q^{r_2(L)}T_mT_{m-1}.
\]

Indeed exclude the union of the exceptional primes for this finite
set. The power-detection argument supplies, for each distinct pair,
a relative degree-one prime where their quotient after raising to
$\ell^b$ is not a $q$th power. The residue group is cyclic and
contains $\mu_q$, so there exists a homomorphism $v$ to $R$ detecting
this quotient. Collect these finitely many evaluations in a single
map $E$ with target a product of copies of $R$. The evaluations
respect stabilization on positive-degree cycles. They thus define
a homomorphism on stable homology with $R$ coefficients, since bar
chains use finitely many matrices. By the determinant calculation,
the vectors $\ell^{m-1}E(y_\alpha)$ are pairwise distinct: dividing
$C_j$ by $\ell^{v_\ell(C_j)}$ gives a unit modulo $q$.

It remains to bound the image of $\ell^{m-1}E$. In the coefficient
exact sequence for stable group homology, the image of integral
homology modulo $q$ has quotient $H_{m-1}(\mathbb Z)[q]$, of order
at most $T_{m-1}$ by stability at $N_*$. On integral lifts use the
endomorphism $P=\prod_{h=2}^m(D_\ell-\ell^h)$ from the rank calculation.
Additivity under block sums gives
$E(Pz)=\prod_{h=2}^m(\ell-\ell^h)E(z)$ on reduction modulo $q$.
The coefficient on the right is $\ell^{m-1}$ times a unit modulo $q$.
The integral image of $P$ has rank at most $r_2(L)$, and its torsion
embeds in the stable torsion of order $T_m$. Its image in the target
of $E$ therefore has order at most $q^{r_2(L)}T_m$. Each coset
of the integral lifts contributes a translate of this image after
the same unit rescaling. Multiplying by the number of cosets proves
the estimate. In particular the loss in the power tested on the
arithmetic side is fixed before the cyclotomic layer $\nu$ is chosen.

To relate this count to the class group, write $F^\times$ additively
in the following calculation. Put $S=\gamma^{-1}$, $a=\kappa^j$ and
$\delta=S-a$. Form the group of pairs $(\alpha,\mathfrak d)$ with
$\operatorname{div}\alpha=\delta\mathfrak d$, modulo pairs of the form
$(\delta v,\operatorname{div}v)$, and denote it by $W_0$. There is
an exact sequence
\[
 0\longrightarrow \mathcal O_F^\times/\delta\mathcal O_F^\times
 \longrightarrow W_0\longrightarrow \ker(\delta:\mathrm{Cl}(F)
 \to\mathrm{Cl}(F))\longrightarrow0 .
\]
The last map takes the class of $\mathfrak d$. Its surjectivity is
the definition of a principal divisor; if $\mathfrak d=\operatorname{div}v$,
subtracting the relation leaves a unit in the first coordinate.
Two such units differ by a relation precisely when that relation
comes from a unit. Since the class group is finite, the cardinalities
of the kernel and cokernel of $\delta$ on it agree.

Let $T=\sum_{h=0}^{n-1}a^h\gamma^h$. Then
$T\delta=\gamma^{-1}(1-a^n)$, and $q\mid1-a^n$.
Consequently $(\alpha,\mathfrak d)\mapsto\ell^bT\alpha\bmod q F^\times$
is well defined on $W_0$. The preceding homological count bounds its
image. For the kernel estimate divide $F^\times$ by its roots of unity,
writing $Y=F^\times/\mu(F)$ additively, and use the same definition
of pairs with $Y$ in the first coordinate. Denote this quotient of
$W_0$ by $W$. The exact sequence above now has free units modulo
$\delta$ on the left. The target of the counting map becomes $Y/qY$,
so the preceding image bound still applies. Both $Y$ and the divisor
group are torsion-free, and $\delta$ is injective on each: $S^n=1$
and $a^n>1$. In particular $W$ identifies, via the first coordinate,
with a subgroup of $Y/\delta Y$.

If a class in the counting kernel is represented by $y$, then
$\ell^bTy=qz$ in $Y$. Applying $\delta$ and cancelling $q$ in $Y$
gives $\ell^b(1-a^n)Sy/q=\delta z$. Thus the $\ell$-primary kernel
is killed by $\ell^{b+v_\ell(j)}$, since
$v_\ell(a^n-1)=\nu+v_\ell(j)$ and $S$ is invertible.

Its number of generators also admits a bound independent of $\nu$.
If a pair $(y,D')$ represents an element of $W[\ell]$, then
$\ell y=\delta v$, $\ell D'=\operatorname{div}v$. The class of $v$
in $Y/\ell Y$ is well-defined by injectivity of $\delta$, and it
determines the pair class: if $v=\ell z$, the pair is the relation
$(\delta z,\operatorname{div}z)$. This homomorphism embeds $W[\ell]$
in the $S$-invariant classes represented by elements whose valuations
are all divisible by $\ell$, since $a\equiv1\pmod\ell$.

Here is a uniform count of the latter classes. In multiplicative
